Savory Pancakes

Scallion, cabbage, cheese, and chile. Cakes that stand in for the meal.

A savory pancake is built to be dinner or a street lunch: okonomiyaki, pajeon, a buckwheat galette, a pupusa, a dosa with potato.

Sweet breakfast cakes stay in the breakfast booklet unless the recipe itself is salted and filled.

India · Medium · 8 adai

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Adai

A sturdy mixed-lentil pancake, peppery and hot from dried chile, with curry leaf in the batter.

30 min prep · 30 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 adai

History

Adai is a mixed-lentil pancake of Tamil Nadu and the wider South. Toor, chana, urad, and moong are soaked with rice, then ground with dried red chile and curry leaf. Cooks often grind and cook it the same day. A long dosa-style ferment is optional, not the definition. The result is thicker than a hotel dosa and hot enough to stand with a knob of jaggery or a spoon of avial on the side.

Cultural background

Madurai and other Tamil kitchens keep adai as a meal when a fermented batter was not planned. The several dals make it more filling than a thin rice crepe. Dried chile is the usual heat, so this version is medium.

Why it tastes this way

Each dal brings a different note: toor is earthy, chana is sweet, urad is creamy, moong is grassy. Rice keeps the cake from turning dense. Dried red chile and black pepper supply heat that green chile alone would not. Curry leaf, ground in rather than fried on top, perfumes the crumb all the way through.

How it is made

Soak a mix of lentils and rice, grind them coarsely with dried chile, and cook thick savory rounds.

Authentic touch. Leave the grind a little coarse. A silky batter is dosa territory. Adai should show flecks of dal and chile.

How it is used

Serve hot with jaggery, coconut chutney, or avial. The sweet jaggery against the chile is a Tamil habit, not a dessert course.

Field notes

  • Adai uses several lentils at once. It is not a single-dal cheela.
  • Same-day grinding is traditional. An overnight ferment is a choice, not a requirement.

Ingredients

  • 1/4 cup toor dal
  • 1/4 cup chana dal
  • 2 tablespoons skinned urad dal
  • 2 tablespoons moong dal
  • 1/3 cup raw rice
  • 3 dried red chiles
  • 10 curry leaves
  • 1/2 teaspoon black pepper
  • 3/4 teaspoon salt
  • Water and neutral oil

Steps

  1. Rinse the dals and rice together and soak them with the dried chiles for 3 to 4 hours.
  2. Drain and grind coarsely with curry leaves, pepper, salt, and a little water. The batter should be thick and speckled, not a smooth cream.
  3. Cook it promptly, or rest it for 30 minutes. No long ferment is required.
  4. Oil a hot griddle. Spread a ladle into a small, thick round. Make a tiny hole in the center if you like, a common adai habit that helps the middle cook.
  5. Drizzle oil, cook until the underside is deep gold, flip, and cook the other side. Serve at once.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A fistful of chopped onion folded into the batter
  • Less dried chile for a milder cake

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Snack
  • Jaggery
  • Coconut chutney
  • Avial
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Afghanistan · Mild · 4 large stuffed flatbreads

Flying Pancakes of the World

Afghanistan · Central Asia

Afghan bolani

Crisp stuffed flatbread filled with seasoned potato and leek, mild with green chile.

40 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 large stuffed flatbreads

History

Bolani is an Afghan stuffed flatbread. A thin wheat dough is filled with potato, leek, or pumpkin, folded, and cooked on a griddle with a slick of oil until the outside blisters. In Kabul it is street food and home food, cut into wedges and eaten with a green chutney. This filling uses potato and leek together, with a mild green chile. It is not a batter pancake. Central Asia is the region this journal uses for Afghanistan.

Cultural background

Bolani is made for guests and for ordinary afternoons. The filling should be dry enough not to tear the dough. Cilantro and chile are mixed into the potato, not served only as a sauce. A yogurt dip is common and is optional dairy beside a vegan bread.

Why it tastes this way

The crust tastes of toasted wheat and a little frying oil. Potato makes the inside soft and mild, and leek adds a sweet onion flavor without the sharpness of raw scallion. A chopped green chile is warm rather than burning, which is why the heat is mild. Salt and cilantro keep the filling from eating like plain mashed potato.

How it is made

Boil and mash potato, then mix it with softened leek, cilantro, green chile, and salt. Roll dough into thin rounds, fill half, seal, and cook on an oiled griddle until both sides are golden and crisp. Serve with a cilantro chutney.

Authentic touch. Drain the potato filling well and seal it inside a thin dough. Bolani is a stuffed flatbread. A wet filling splits the crust. The chile in this batch is mild.

How it is used

Bolani is a snack, a street food, and a savory share at the table. It is cut and eaten by hand. Yogurt on the side is optional and adds dairy. A meat filling is not this recipe.

Field notes

  • Bolani is a stuffed flatbread, not a batter pancake.
  • Potato and leek are both in this filling. Pumpkin is another common choice.
  • A green chile makes the heat mild rather than hot.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups wheat flour (about 250 g)
  • 3/4 teaspoon fine salt, divided
  • 3/4 cup water
  • 2 potatoes, boiled and mashed
  • 1 leek, finely chopped and softened in a dry pan
  • 1/2 cup chopped cilantro
  • 1 mild green chile, finely chopped
  • 2 tablespoons oil for the filling and the pan

Steps

  1. Mix the flour, half the salt, and the water into a soft dough. Knead and rest it for 15 minutes.
  2. Mix the mashed potato, softened leek, cilantro, chile, remaining salt, and a spoon of oil. The chile is mild. The filling should be thick, not wet.
  3. Divide the dough into 4 and roll each piece thin. Spread filling on half and fold. Seal the edges. This is a stuffed flatbread.
  4. Cook on a griddle filmed with oil, turning once, until both sides are golden and the dough is cooked through.
  5. Cut into wedges. Serve with chutney. Yogurt is optional and is not in the dough.

The dough contains wheat. The chile is mild but still a chile. Oil on the griddle is hot.

Refrigerate leftover bolani and reheat on a skillet so the crust crisps. Do not leave the potato filling out in a warm room. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Leek alone, the green called gandana in many Afghan kitchens, is a classic filling.
  • Pumpkin replaces potato in season and stays vegan if you skip the yogurt.
  • A hotter chile would change the mild tag. This one stays mild.

Pairings and uses

  • Snack
  • Street food
  • Savory meal
  • Cilantro chutney
  • Plain yogurt, which adds dairy
  • Black tea
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Sweden · No chili heat · 1 large cake, 4 wedges

Flying Pancakes of the World

Sweden · Northern Europe

Äggakaka

A thick Scanian egg pancake, served with fried pork and lingonberries.

15 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 1 large cake, 4 wedges

History

Äggakaka is the thick egg pancake of Skåne, in southern Sweden. It is closer to a sturdy omelet than to a thin pannkaka. The plate is the cake plus fried pork and lingonberries. This recipe includes the pork because that is the Scanian plate, so the dish is not vegetarian. It is festival and home food in Skåne, not the Thursday pea-soup pancake of the pannkakor page.

Cultural background

Skåne cooking is often richer than a thin crepe tradition. The egg cake is cut in wedges. Lingonberries cut the fat of the pork and the eggs.

Why it tastes this way

Many eggs make it custardy and savory. A little flour keeps it from collapsing. Pork fat and browned edges add salt and crispness. Lingonberry is sour and sweet at once. Without the jam, the plate is heavier. The cake should be thick enough to cut.

How it is made

Eggs, milk, flour, and salt make a thick batter that cooks slowly in a pan of rendered pork, then is cut and served with lingonberries.

Authentic touch. Cook it slowly, covered, so the center sets without burning the bottom. Serve the pork and the lingonberries on the same plate. Do not roll it like a crepe.

How it is used

In wedges, with the fried pork and lingonberry jam, as a hearty meal.

Field notes

  • Äggakaka is Scanian. It is thick, and it is served with pork and lingonberries.
  • It is not the thin Swedish pannkaka.
  • The name means egg cake.

Ingredients

  • 200 g salt pork or thick bacon, sliced
  • 4 large eggs
  • 200 ml whole milk
  • 80 g plain wheat flour
  • 1/2 tsp fine salt
  • Lingonberry jam, for serving

Steps

  1. Fry the pork in a wide pan until browned. Leave the fat in the pan and set the pork aside.
  2. Whisk the eggs, milk, flour, and salt into a smooth thick batter.
  3. Pour the batter into the hot fat. Cook over medium-low heat, covered, until the center is just set. Turn in sections if you can, or brown the top under a hot grill.
  4. Return the pork to the plate, cut the cake in wedges, and serve with lingonberry jam.

The center should be set, not liquid egg. Salt pork is salty, so taste before you add more salt.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A meat-free egg cake, which is no longer the Scanian plate
  • Crisp bacon instead of salt pork, if that is what you have

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Feast or fast day
  • Lingonberry jam
  • The pork cooked in the pan
  • A glass of milk or beer
  • Nut-free

Algeria · Medium · 6 folded squares

Flying Pancakes of the World

Algeria · North Africa

Algerian mahjouba

Thin semolina flatbread folded around a tomato, onion, and chile filling.

40 min prep · 30 min cook · Serves 6 · 6 folded squares

History

Mahjouba, also spelled mhadjeb, is an Algerian street bread: a soft semolina dough stretched thin, filled with cooked tomato and onion, folded, and cooked on a griddle. English sometimes calls these stuffed semolina crepes because the sheet is thin. The dough is spread by hand. It is not a thin egg batter poured from a jug. Stalls sell it across Algerian cities, folded into squares you can eat walking.

Cultural background

The filling is the cook's signature: tomato, onion, garlic, cumin, and chile. Heat sits in the filling, not in a dry spice rubbed on afterward. The square is cut or eaten whole from paper. It is a snack and a light meal.

Why it tastes this way

Semolina keeps the wrapper faintly sweet and elastic, so it stands up to a wet filling. The tomato cooks down with onion until it is jammy, and chile plus cumin make it savory rather than like a sauce on pasta. Oil on the griddle toasts the outside of the folds. The center stays soft.

How it is made

Cook onion, garlic, tomato, and chile with cumin and salt until the filling is thick. Mix a soft semolina dough, rest it, and stretch each piece thin on an oiled board. Spread filling in the center, fold into a square, and griddle in a film of oil until both sides are spotted.

Authentic touch. Spread the dough by hand until it is thin and even, then fold the filling inside a square. A poured wheat crepe will tear under this tomato filling. The chile in the filling is the heat.

How it is used

Mahjouba is street food, eaten hot off the griddle. It can be a snack or the savory part of a casual meal. It is not a dessert pancake. Extra harissa at the table pushes the heat higher than this recipe.

Field notes

  • Mahjouba is a stuffed semolina flatbread, spread by hand and folded into a square.
  • You will also see the spelling mhadjeb.
  • The heat is in the tomato and chile filling, not in the dough.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups fine semolina (about 320 g)
  • 1/2 cup wheat flour
  • 1 teaspoon fine salt, divided
  • 1 cup warm water
  • 3 tablespoons oil, plus more for the griddle
  • 2 onions, chopped
  • 2 garlic cloves, chopped
  • 4 ripe tomatoes, chopped
  • 1 fresh chile, chopped
  • 1 teaspoon ground cumin

Steps

  1. Cook the onions in a tablespoon of oil until soft. Add the garlic, tomatoes, chile, cumin, and half the salt. Simmer until the filling is thick and not watery.
  2. Mix the semolina, flour, remaining salt, water, and a spoon of oil into a soft dough. Rest it for 15 minutes.
  3. Divide the dough into 6 pieces. Oil the board and stretch each piece by hand into a thin sheet. This is a stuffed flatbread, not a poured crepe batter.
  4. Spread a spoon of filling in the center and fold the sheet into a square, sealing the filling inside.
  5. Griddle the squares in a film of oil, turning once, until both sides are spotted and the dough is cooked through.

The filling is medium heat because of the chile. Wheat is in the semolina and the flour. The pan oil is hot.

Refrigerate filled mahjouba and reheat on a skillet. Do not leave the tomato filling at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A greener filling uses more herbs and less tomato.
  • Some cooks add a spoon of paprika and leave out the fresh chile. That batch is milder.
  • The name mhadjeb is another spelling of the same folded bread.

Pairings and uses

  • Street food
  • Savory meal
  • Snack
  • Harissa on the side, if you want more heat
  • A glass of buttermilk, for drinkers of dairy
  • A simple green salad
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Pakistan · Mild · 6 stuffed breads

Flying Pancakes of the World

Pakistan · South Asia

Aloo paratha

A wheat flatbread stuffed with spiced potato, griddled in ghee until the surface is spotted and flaky.

30 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 6 stuffed breads

History

Aloo paratha is a stuffed griddled bread of Punjabi kitchens in Pakistan and north India. This page places it in Lahore and lists India as well. Mashed potato, green chile, cilantro, and spices fill a wheat dough that is rolled and cooked on a tawa with ghee. It is a stuffed bread, not a batter pancake. Breakfast stalls and homes both cook it. The potato is the filling that made this paratha the everyday one.

Cultural background

The dough is atta, the wheat flour used for chapati. The filling must be dry and cool or it tears the dough. Lahore breakfasts often set the paratha next to yogurt, pickle, or a fried egg. The egg would be an extra allergen.

Why it tastes this way

Wheat toasted in ghee tastes nutty and rich. Potato takes on cumin, chile, and salt and stays soft inside the flake. A little ajwain or cumin keeps the filling from tasting bland. Because the bread is rolled, you get layers of dough around one pocket of spice, which a poured batter cannot do.

How it is made

Stuff a wheat dough with seasoned mashed potato, roll it, and griddle it in ghee until both sides spot.

Authentic touch. Seal the edges before you roll. A leak of potato on the tawa burns and sticks. Roll from the center outward with a light hand.

How it is used

Serve hot, torn with the hands, with pickle or yogurt. It is a breakfast and a travel food.

Field notes

  • Aloo paratha is a stuffed wheat bread cooked on a griddle.
  • Pakistan and north India both claim it as everyday breakfast food.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups whole wheat flour (atta)
  • 3/4 cup water, plus more if needed
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt for the dough
  • 3 medium potatoes, boiled and mashed
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • 2 tablespoons chopped cilantro
  • 1/2 teaspoon cumin
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt for the filling
  • Ghee for cooking

Steps

  1. Mix the flour and 1/2 teaspoon salt with water into a soft dough. Rest it 15 minutes.
  2. Mix the mashed potato with chile, cilantro, cumin, and salt. The filling should be cool and dry, not wet.
  3. Divide the dough into six balls. Flatten one, add a spoon of potato, pinch it closed, and roll gently into a thick round.
  4. Cook on a hot tawa, spreading a little ghee, until both sides are browned in spots and the dough is cooked through.
  5. Repeat with the rest. Serve immediately.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Chopped fresh fenugreek in the potato
  • Oil instead of ghee, which removes the dairy if the dough has none

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Mango pickle
  • Plain yogurt
  • A salad of onion and lime
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

India · No chili heat · 6 ambolis

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Amboli

A soft, spongy, lightly sour rice pancake from the Konkan coast.

30 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 3 · 6 ambolis

History

Amboli is a fermented rice pancake of the Konkan, especially Malvani kitchens in Maharashtra and the coast down toward Goa. Rice is soaked and ground, then left to sour. Some cooks add a little urad. This recipe uses a spoon of flattened rice for softness and keeps the batter wheat-free. It is served with coconut curries and chutneys, a monsoon and breakfast food of the coast rather than a crisp hotel dosa.

Cultural background

Ratnagiri and Malvan cook amboli as a sponge that soaks up a spicy side. The pancake on this page is not itself a hot dish. The heat lives in the fish or vegetable curry beside it. A fish curry would change the allergens, so the pairing listed here is a coconut chutney.

Why it tastes this way

A long rice ferment in humid coastal air turns sweet soaked rice into something mildly sour and bready. The cake is thick, so the sourness stays soft instead of toasting away. Coconut chutney adds fat and sweetness the batter does not have. The coast's humidity is not a myth here: it is why an overnight bowl sours reliably without a starter.

How it is made

Grind soaked rice, ferment it overnight, and cook thick covered rounds on a moderate griddle.

Authentic touch. Cook it covered and do not chase a deep brown crust. Amboli is done when the top is set and the crumb looks spongy, like a pancake that never tried to be a wafer.

How it is used

Serve in a stack with coconut chutney. On the Konkan it also meets spicy curries. Those are separate dishes.

Field notes

  • Amboli is a Konkan fermented rice cake, soft on purpose.
  • This batter has no wheat, so the pancake is gluten-free.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup raw rice
  • 2 tablespoons flattened rice (poha)
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Water for soaking and grinding
  • Neutral oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Rinse the rice and soak it with the flattened rice for 4 hours.
  2. Drain and grind to a smooth batter a little thicker than crepe batter. Stir in the salt.
  3. Ferment covered overnight, until bubbly and mildly sour. Do not count those hours as active prep. Discard the bowl if it spoils.
  4. Oil a pan over medium-low. Pour a thick round and cover it.
  5. Cook until the top is set and spongy and the underside is pale gold. Flip only if the center needs it. Repeat.

Discard batter that smells rotten or shows mold.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A tablespoon of soaked urad ground with the rice
  • A pinch of sugar if the ferment went very sour

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Coconut chutney
  • A dry potato bhaji
  • Sweetened coconut milk
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

India · No chili heat · 8 appams

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Appam

A bowl-shaped rice hopper with a lacy crisp rim, a spongy sweet center, and coconut in the batter.

35 min prep · 30 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 appams

History

Appam is Kerala's fermented rice hopper, cooked in a curved appam pan so the center stays thick and the sides climb into a lace. Coconut milk is a usual part of the batter. Where toddy is still used, it is the ferment. This home recipe uses a pinch of yeast as the reliable stand-in and says so. Sri Lankan hoppers are a close cousin with their own page. Nei appam, the sweet ghee cake, is not this dish.

Cultural background

Kerala breakfasts pair appam with stew: vegetable, egg, or meat. The hopper itself is rice and coconut. A yeast ferment will not taste identical to a toddy ferment, and the page does not pretend it will. The bowl shape is non-negotiable.

Why it tastes this way

Rice and coconut milk make the center sweet and tender. Fermentation, whether from toddy or from yeast, adds a light sourness that keeps the coconut from tasting flat. The thin batter that climbs the hot wall of the pan dries into lace and picks up a toasted edge. Salt is the only savory note in the cake. The stew around it does the rest.

How it is made

Ferment a rice and coconut-milk batter and swirl it in a covered curved pan so it finishes bowl-shaped.

Authentic touch. Swirl the pan as soon as you pour, so a puddle remains in the center and only a film coats the sides. Then cover the pan. An open flat griddle cannot make an appam.

How it is used

Serve the bowls at once, center side up, with a mild vegetable stew. Meat stew is a common pairing and would add a meat allergen.

Field notes

  • The bowl shape comes from a curved appam pan and a swirl, not from a mold pressed into flat batter.
  • This savory hopper is a different cake from sweet nei appam.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup raw rice
  • 1/2 cup cooked rice
  • 1/2 cup coconut milk, plus more if needed
  • 1/4 teaspoon dry yeast
  • 1 teaspoon sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Water as needed
  • Neutral oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Soak the raw rice for 3 hours. Drain and grind it with the cooked rice and a little water to a smooth batter.
  2. Stir in coconut milk, yeast, sugar, and salt. The batter should be thinner than dosa batter and thicker than milk.
  3. Cover and ferment until bubbly and lightly sour, often 6 to 10 hours in a warm kitchen. Active prep excludes that wait.
  4. Heat a curved appam pan, grease it, and pour a ladle of batter. Swirl so the sides take a thin coat and the center stays deep.
  5. Cover and cook on medium-low until the rim is lacy and the center is set and spongy. Ease it out and repeat.

Discard batter that smells rotten or grows mold. Cook the center until it is set, not glossy and raw.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Toddy in place of yeast where you can get it and trust it
  • A spoon of sugar for a sweeter palappam

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Mild vegetable stew
  • Sweetened coconut milk
  • A ripe banana
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Colombia · No chili heat · 4 fried arepas

Flying Pancakes of the World

Colombia · South America

Arepa de huevo

A fried corn cake with a whole egg cooked inside, crisp outside and set in the middle.

15 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 fried arepas

History

Arepa de huevo is a coastal Colombian street food, especially in Cartagena and Barranquilla. A corn cake is fried, slit, filled with a raw egg, sealed, and fried again until the egg sets. It is fried, not griddled dry like an Antioquia breakfast arepa. The egg is the filling, so this recipe is not vegetarian. The plain corn cake is older than this stuffed, fried way of eating it. Frying an egg inside is a coastal technique, not a single ancient recipe, and the dough is precooked cornmeal, not nixtamalized masa and not fresh choclo.

Cultural background

Stalls along the Caribbean coast fry them to order and hand them over in a napkin. Cheese or a drizzle of suero, a sour cream, sometimes goes on afterward. Those dairy additions are not in this recipe. The written cake is corn, salt, egg, and oil.

Why it tastes this way

Frying gives the corn a deep, crisp crust that a dry griddle does not. The second fry sets the egg so the white is firm and the yolk stays just soft if you move quickly. The corn dough tastes plain and salty, a shell for the egg. There is no cheese in this batch, so the flavor stays with corn and fried egg. Oil, not chile, is the richness.

How it is made

Mix yellow precooked cornmeal with salted water, form disks, and fry them. Slit each cake, slide in an egg, close it, and fry until the egg is set.

Authentic touch. Fry the sealed cake once so it is firm enough to hold a slit. Add only one egg, press the opening closed, and fry again until the egg is cooked. A raw white in the center is not done. Keep the oil deep enough to brown both sides.

How it is used

Eat them immediately, from a napkin or a plate, as a street snack or a quick meal. They are greasy on purpose. Add suero only if you accept the dairy.

Field notes

  • Arepa de huevo is fried twice: once to set the shell, again to cook the egg.
  • The egg means this recipe is not vegetarian, even though there is no meat.
  • The dough is precooked cornmeal. It is not masa and not fresh sweet corn.

Ingredients

  • 250 g yellow precooked cornmeal
  • 360 ml warm water
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 4 eggs
  • Neutral oil for frying

Steps

  1. Stir the cornmeal, salt, and warm water into a soft dough. Rest 5 minutes. Form 4 thick disks and smooth the cracks.
  2. Heat 2 centimeters of oil over medium heat until a pinch of dough sizzles at once.
  3. Fry the disks, turning once, until they are firm and lightly golden. Drain them a moment.
  4. Cut a slit in the side of each cake, pour in one egg, and press the opening closed.
  5. Fry again, turning carefully, until the outside is deep gold and the egg is set. Drain and serve at once.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A slice of cheese in with the egg adds dairy.
  • Ground beef in place of the egg is a different stuffed fry and adds meat.
  • Griddling this dough without the egg gives you a plain coastal arepa, which is a different dish from the fried egg version.

Pairings and uses

  • Street food
  • Savory meal
  • Snack
  • Suero or sour cream, which adds dairy
  • A fresh tomato and onion relish
  • Lime and a cold drink
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Colombia · No chili heat · 4 arepas and chicharrón

Flying Pancakes of the World

Colombia · South America

Arepa santandereana

A thick yellow-corn cake, griddled, served with crisp pork chicharrón.

20 min prep · 50 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 arepas and chicharrón

History

In Santander the arepa is often yellow corn, thicker than the white arepa of Antioquia, and it is eaten with chicharrón. The pork is part of this plate, so the recipe contains meat and is not vegetarian. Yellow corn dough here is cooked corn that has been ground, or yellow precooked cornmeal at home. It is not nixtamalized masa and not a fresh-corn choclo cake. The corn cake as a form is older than wheat hotcakes in the region. Serving it with fried pork belly is a Santander breakfast, not a stuffed coastal egg arepa.

Cultural background

Bucaramanga breakfasts pair the yellow arepa with chicharrón, sometimes with hogao or avocado. This recipe keeps the cake plain and puts the pork on the same plate, because that is how the dish is recognized. The arepa can be eaten alone, but then you are no longer cooking the plate this page describes.

Why it tastes this way

Yellow corn tastes deeper and a little sweeter than white precooked meal. A thick cake stays moist inside and toasts outside. Chicharrón is salt, rendered fat, and a crackling crust, which is why the mild corn can stand next to it without salsa. There is no chile in the written recipe. The richness is pork fat, not cheese.

How it is made

Form thick cakes from yellow precooked cornmeal and salt water and griddle them. Simmer pork belly, then fry it until crisp, and serve the two together.

Authentic touch. Simmer the pork belly until it is tender, dry it, then fry it in its own fat until the skin blisters and the meat is cooked through. Griddle the arepas separately so they do not sit in that fat. Season the dough only with salt.

How it is used

Serve one or two arepas with a piece of chicharrón for breakfast. Avocado on the side is common. The pork is still part of the plate.

Field notes

  • Santander breakfast arepas are yellow corn and are often eaten with chicharrón.
  • The pork in this recipe means the plate is not vegetarian.
  • Yellow precooked cornmeal stands in for cooked, ground yellow corn. It is not masa harina.

Ingredients

  • 250 g yellow precooked cornmeal
  • 340 ml warm water
  • 1 teaspoon salt, plus more for the pork
  • 400 g skin-on pork belly, in four pieces

Steps

  1. Cover the pork belly with water, salt it, and simmer until the meat is tender, about 30 minutes. Drain and pat dry.
  2. Stir the cornmeal, 1 teaspoon salt, and warm water into a soft dough. Rest 5 minutes. Form 4 thick cakes and smooth the cracks.
  3. Griddle the arepas over medium heat until both sides are crusty and the center is cooked, about 7 minutes a side.
  4. Fry the dried pork in a skillet over medium heat, turning, until the skin is crisp and the meat is cooked through. Pour off excess fat as it renders.
  5. Serve the arepas with the chicharrón.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Skip the pork and the plate is a plain yellow arepa, which can be vegetarian. This written recipe includes the chicharrón.
  • A little cheese on the arepa adds dairy.
  • White precooked cornmeal changes the color Santander cooks expect from this cake.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Sliced avocado
  • Hogao, if you want a sauce
  • Small black coffee
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Azerbaijan · No chili heat · 8 half-moons

Flying Pancakes of the World

Azerbaijan · Caucasus

Azerbaijani qutab

Thin half-moon flatbread stuffed with herbs, finished with sumac.

35 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 half-moons

History

Qutab is an Azerbaijani stuffed flatbread, cooked on a saj or a dry griddle and folded into a half-moon. The two fillings people ask for are herbs, göy qutab, and pumpkin. This recipe is the herb one. A dusting of sumac after cooking is part of the plate. It is a griddled flatbread, not a batter pancake, and it belongs to the Caucasus on this map, with Baku as the pin.

Cultural background

Qutab is everyday food and guest food. The dough is rolled thin enough that the green of the herbs shows as a shadow. Yogurt or sour cream is a common side and is dairy, so it is kept off this vegan recipe and mentioned as a pairing. The bread itself is oil, flour, herbs, and sumac.

Why it tastes this way

The herbs taste green and fresh because they are barely cooked inside the dough, not fried first into a paste. Wheat from the thin wrapper toasts on the saj. Sumac on the finished bread is sour and fruity, which is why a separate sauce is optional. There is no chile in this version.

How it is made

Make a soft dough and rest it. Chop herbs and scallions finely, salt them, and bind them with a spoon of oil. Fill thin rounds, fold them into half-moons, and cook on a hot griddle. Dust with sumac.

Authentic touch. Roll the dough thin, use a generous handful of herbs, and sprinkle sumac after the bread leaves the pan. Pumpkin qutab is the other canonical filling and is listed as a variation, not cooked in this batch.

How it is used

Qutab is a savory snack or a light meal, eaten hot. Yogurt on the side is traditional and adds dairy. Sumac is the finish on the bread. It is not a sweet pancake.

Field notes

  • Qutab is a stuffed griddled flatbread, folded into a half-moon.
  • Herbs and pumpkin are the two fillings most often named.
  • Sumac is the sour red sprinkle on the finished bread.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups wheat flour (about 250 g)
  • 3/4 teaspoon fine salt, divided
  • 3/4 cup water
  • 1 cup finely chopped scallions
  • 1 cup finely chopped cilantro
  • 1 cup finely chopped spinach
  • 2 tablespoons oil
  • 1 teaspoon ground sumac

Steps

  1. Mix the flour, half the salt, and the water into a soft dough. Rest it, covered, for 15 minutes.
  2. Mix the scallions, cilantro, spinach, remaining salt, and one tablespoon of oil. The herbs should look abundant, not like a garnish.
  3. Divide the dough into 8 and roll each piece thin. Place a spoon of herbs on one half and fold into a half-moon. Seal the edge.
  4. Cook on a hot dry griddle, turning once, until both sides are spotted and the dough is no longer raw. This is a stuffed flatbread.
  5. Brush lightly with the remaining oil and sprinkle with sumac.

The dough contains wheat. Sumac is a sour spice made from dried berries. Yogurt on the side is dairy and is not required.

Eat qutab soon after cooking. Refrigerate leftovers and reheat them on a dry pan. The herbs dull after a day. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Pumpkin qutab uses grated, salted, squeezed pumpkin instead of herbs.
  • A meat filling exists and is not vegetarian.
  • Melted butter instead of the oil finish adds dairy and is not in this written dough.

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Snack
  • Sumac, already on the bread
  • Plain yogurt, which adds dairy
  • Black tea
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

United States · No chili heat · 12 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Bacon griddle cakes

Salty, smoky bacon in a lightly sweet wheat pancake, cooked in bacon fat.

20 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 cakes

History

Bacon griddle cakes cook chopped bacon into a wheat pancake so the breakfast meat and the cake come off the same griddle. The idea is a savory American morning plate. Nothing about it is older than the habit of serving bacon beside pancakes and then putting the bacon inside them. Madison is the map pin for a widespread plate, not a local invention.

Cultural background

The bacon is cooked first, and some of its fat greases the griddle. The batter is a plain buttermilk pancake. This recipe contains meat, so it is not vegetarian.

Why it tastes this way

Rendered bacon fat browns the cake and carries smoke and salt into the crumb. Pieces of bacon stay chewy if they were cooked crisp before they went in. Maple syrup at the table is the sweet against that salt. A batter that is very sweet fights the bacon.

How it is made

Buttermilk pancakes with chopped cooked bacon, griddled in bacon fat.

Authentic touch. Cook the bacon first and use a spoon of the fat on the griddle. Raw bacon stirred into batter will not crisp.

How it is used

Hot, with maple syrup if you want the salty-sweet plate.

Field notes

  • The bacon has to be cooked before it is added, or it stays flabby inside the cake.
  • This is a meat pancake. The diet list does not call it vegetarian.

Ingredients

  • 6 slices bacon
  • 1 1/2 cups all-purpose flour
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 2 teaspoons baking powder
  • 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 1/2 cups buttermilk
  • 1 large egg
  • 1 tablespoon unsalted butter, melted

Steps

  1. Cook the bacon in a skillet until crisp. Drain it on a rack, reserve 2 tablespoons of the fat, and chop the bacon.
  2. Whisk the flour, sugar, baking powder, baking soda, and salt.
  3. Stir in the buttermilk, egg, and melted butter. Fold in the chopped bacon.
  4. Heat a griddle over medium heat and brush it with the reserved bacon fat.
  5. Pour 1/4 cup cakes. Flip when the tops set and the undersides are brown.
  6. Cook until the center is done. Serve hot, with maple syrup if you want it.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A fully savory cake leaves out the sugar.
  • Sausage in place of bacon is a different meat and still not vegetarian.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Maple syrup
  • Fried eggs
  • Black coffee
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Vietnam · Mild · 24 mini cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Vietnam · Southeast Asia

Banh khot

Tiny crisp-edged rice cakes from a dimpled pan, each topped with a shrimp and a soft coconut center.

20 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 24 mini cakes

History

Banh khot are mini savory pancakes of southern Vietnam, cooked in a dimpled pan. Vung Tau is especially known for them. The batter is rice flour, turmeric, and coconut milk, close to a small banh xeo but shaped by the mold and usually topped with one shrimp rather than folded around pork. They are street food, served with herbs and a fish-sauce dip. This recipe has shrimp and fish sauce, so it is not vegetarian. It is a mold cake, not the large crepe.

Cultural background

The pans look like a cluster of small cups. A crisp rim and a slightly custardy center are the textures to chase. Quail eggs or pork appear in some stalls. Shrimp is the topping this page writes. The cakes are eaten as soon as they leave the mold.

Why it tastes this way

Coconut milk and turmeric give the same yellow, lightly bitter sweetness as a larger rice crepe, concentrated in a bite. The shrimp on top tastes briny and sweet. Fish sauce in the dip adds salt and acid. Because the cake is small and molded, the rim fries harder than the center. Herbs make each bite fresher than the batter alone.

How it is made

Pour turmeric-coconut rice batter into a hot dimpled pan, set a shrimp in each cup, and cook until the rims are crisp.

Authentic touch. Heat the molds until oil shimmers before you pour. A cool cup sticks and never crisps. One shrimp, pressed in while the batter is still wet, stays attached.

How it is used

Lift them out and eat them at once with herbs and nuoc cham. They are a snack or a street meal.

Field notes

  • Banh khot are the small molded cakes of southern Vietnam, especially Vung Tau.
  • They share a turmeric rice batter with banh xeo and use a dimpled pan instead of a wide crepe.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup rice flour, with no wheat added
  • 3/4 cup coconut milk
  • 1/2 cup water
  • 1/2 teaspoon turmeric
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 24 small shrimp, peeled
  • 1 tablespoon fish sauce
  • Neutral oil
  • Scallion greens

Steps

  1. Whisk rice flour, coconut milk, water, turmeric, and salt into a batter thinner than pancake batter. Rest 15 minutes.
  2. Toss the shrimp with a teaspoon of fish sauce. Keep the rest for a dip with lime, sugar, and water.
  3. Oil a hot dimpled pan. Fill each cup about halfway.
  4. Press one shrimp and a few scallion rings into each cup. Cook until the rims are golden and the centers are set.
  5. Loosen the cakes and serve them with herbs and the dip.

Cook the shrimp until they are opaque and pink.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A quail egg in a few cups
  • Finely sliced scallion in the batter

Pairings and uses

  • Street food
  • Savory meal
  • Snack
  • Nuoc cham
  • Lettuce and mint
  • Pickled carrot
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Vietnam · Mild · 4 folded crepes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Vietnam · Southeast Asia

Banh xeo

A turmeric-yellow rice crepe, crisp at the edge, filled with pork, shrimp, and bean sprouts.

25 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 folded crepes

History

Banh xeo is a Vietnamese turmeric rice crepe filled with pork and shrimp, eaten with herbs. The batter is rice flour and coconut milk, poured onto a hot pan so it crackles. The name is often explained as the sizzle of that pour. Central cities such as Da Nang and southern kitchens both have their own size and filling. A related crepe is also eaten in Cambodia, so Cambodia is listed beside Vietnam. The written recipe includes pork, shrimp, and fish sauce. It is not vegetarian.

Cultural background

The crepe is folded over the filling and brought to the table with a pile of lettuce, mint, and perilla. Diners tear pieces and wrap them. Nuoc cham, a dipping sauce of fish sauce, lime, sugar, and chile, is the usual bowl. The cake itself is only mildly hot. The dip can be hotter.

Why it tastes this way

Turmeric colors the rice and adds a light bitter note. Coconut milk softens that bitterness and helps the edge fry crisp. Pork and shrimp make the center savory and briny. Bean sprouts stay watery and fresh against the crackle. Herbs and the lime in the fish-sauce dip are what make a bite taste complete. The rice batter has no wheat, which is why a careful batch is gluten-free.

How it is made

Fry pork and shrimp, pour a turmeric rice batter over them in a hot pan, add bean sprouts, and fold the crepe.

Authentic touch. Use a hot pan and a thin pour, then leave it alone until the edge lifts and sounds dry. Stirring turns it into a scramble. The pork and shrimp should be nearly cooked before they are covered by the batter if they are in large pieces.

How it is used

Eat it in herb and lettuce wraps with nuoc cham. It is a savory street meal, shared from the pan.

Field notes

  • Banh xeo is a turmeric rice crepe with pork and shrimp, eaten with herbs.
  • Cambodia eats a related crepe, so it is listed beside Vietnam.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup rice flour, with no wheat added
  • 1 cup coconut milk
  • 1/2 teaspoon turmeric
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 150 grams thinly sliced pork belly or pork shoulder
  • 150 grams shrimp, peeled
  • 1 tablespoon fish sauce
  • 2 cups bean sprouts
  • Neutral oil
  • Lettuce and herbs to serve

Steps

  1. Whisk rice flour, coconut milk, turmeric, salt, and a splash of water into a thin batter. Rest 15 minutes.
  2. Season pork and shrimp with a teaspoon of fish sauce. Stir the rest of the fish sauce into a small dip with lime, sugar, water, and chile if you want nuoc cham.
  3. Oil a hot pan. Fry a portion of pork and shrimp until the pork loses its pink and the shrimp turn opaque.
  4. Pour a ladle of batter over them and swirl a thin crepe. When the edge crisps, add bean sprouts, fold, and cook another minute.
  5. Repeat. Serve with herbs, lettuce, and the dip.

Cook the pork through and the shrimp until opaque.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A smaller crepe, one shrimp each, closer to some southern plates
  • Mung beans soaked and scattered in, a central-region habit

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Street food
  • Lettuce, mint, and perilla
  • Nuoc cham
  • Pickled carrot and daikon
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Belarus · No chili heat · 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Belarus · Eastern Europe

Belarusian draniki

Belarusian potato pancakes bound with potato starch and egg, sour cream on the side.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 pancakes

History

Draniki are the potato pancakes of Belarus, grated and fried. They are a national home dish, served with sour cream. A pork gravy called machanka is a traditional accompaniment and is not in this recipe, so the cakes stay vegetarian. This batter uses the potato's own starch and an egg, not wheat flour, so it is gluten-free. Sour cream is a topping. The cakes are fried in oil and the recipe is dairy-free until you add that topping.

Cultural background

Potato is the staple. Draniki are everyday food, not a single holiday. They are cousins of deruny, placki, and latkes, with a Belarusian name and this flourless method.

Why it tastes this way

Crisp fried potato and onion, salted, with a soft center. Egg binds the shreds without flour. The flavor is direct. Sour cream cools the crust if you add it. Machanka, if you serve it, makes a heavier meat plate. The oil should be hot enough to crust the cake before it absorbs grease.

How it is made

Potatoes and onion are grated, mixed with their starch, an egg, and salt, and fried in oil.

Authentic touch. Do not add wheat flour if you want this gluten-free version. Keep the starch that settles out of the grated potato. Sour cream is the usual condiment and is optional if the meal must stay dairy-free.

How it is used

Hot, with sour cream. Machanka, a pork gravy, is the meat accompaniment and is not part of this batter.

Field notes

  • Draniki are the Belarusian potato pancakes.
  • This recipe uses no wheat flour.
  • Sour cream is the usual topping. Machanka is a pork gravy served beside them, not in this batter.

Ingredients

  • 700 g starchy potatoes, peeled
  • 1 small onion
  • 1 large egg
  • 1 tsp fine salt
  • Neutral oil, for frying

Steps

  1. Grate the potatoes and onion. Save the liquid, let the starch settle, pour off the water, and mix the starch back in.
  2. Stir in the egg and salt.
  3. Fry flattened rounds in hot oil until both sides are brown and the centers are cooked.
  4. Serve hot. Add sour cream at the table if you want it.

The potato must cook through. Do not leave the frying pan.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of wheat flour, which removes the gluten-free diet
  • Ukrainian deruny, which use flour on their page

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Sour cream
  • Machanka, a pork gravy, for a meat meal
  • Salt
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

India · Mild · 6 cheelas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Besan cheela

A savory gram-flour pancake with onion, ajwain, and cumin, soft in the middle and spotted brown.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 3 · 6 cheelas

History

Besan cheela is a North Indian gram-flour pancake, mixed and cooked at once. Besan is chickpea flour. Delhi breakfasts and Rajasthani kitchens both keep it as a fast meal, not as a fermented dosa. There is no rice and no urad soak. Onion, ajwain, and a green chile go into the bowl. A Gujarati pudla is a related besan cake with its own page and its own breakfast habits.

Cultural background

Cheela, also spelled chilla, is weeknight cooking: flour, water, spices, a pan. It does not ask for a grindstone or an overnight bowl. That speed is why it sits in a different column from dosa, even though both are round and griddled.

Why it tastes this way

Chickpea flour tastes nutty and slightly sweet once it cooks. Ajwain adds a thyme-like sharpness that North Indian batters use to keep besan from feeling heavy. Cumin and onion make it savory. Because nothing ferments, there is no sour smell. The mild chile is a lift, not a contest.

How it is made

Whisk besan with water, onion, and spices, rest it briefly, and cook small pancakes in a filmed pan.

Authentic touch. Rest the batter 10 minutes so the besan hydrates, then cook on medium. High heat scorches the outside and leaves raw flour inside.

How it is used

Eat hot with mint-cilantro chutney or ketchup. It is a breakfast or a tiffin, not a dessert.

Field notes

  • Besan is chickpea flour. This cheela is unfermented.
  • With only besan and no wheat, the written recipe is gluten-free.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup besan (chickpea flour), with no wheat added
  • 3/4 cup water
  • 1 small onion, finely chopped
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • 1/2 teaspoon cumin seeds
  • 1/4 teaspoon ajwain seeds
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 2 tablespoons chopped cilantro
  • Neutral oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Whisk besan, salt, and water into a lump-free batter about as thick as heavy cream.
  2. Stir in onion, chile, cumin, ajwain, and cilantro. Rest 10 minutes.
  3. Heat a pan and oil it lightly. Pour a small ladle and spread it into a round.
  4. Cook until the top looks set and the underside is brown in spots. Flip and cook the other side.
  5. Repeat, stirring the bowl so the onion stays mixed in.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Grated carrot in the batter
  • A pinch of turmeric if you want color without making the Gujarati pudla

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Snack
  • Mint and cilantro chutney
  • Sliced onion
  • Plain yogurt, a dairy side
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

South Korea · Mild · 6 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

South Korea · East Asia

Bindaetteok

A coarse mung-bean pancake with pork, sour kimchi, and a toasted sesame edge.

25 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 6 pancakes

History

Bindaetteok, also called nokdujeon, is Seoul's mung-bean pancake. Split yellow mung beans are soaked, ground coarsely, and fried with pork and kimchi. A spoon of wheat flour appears in some pans. This recipe leaves that flour out, so the gluten-free tag matches the bowl. The pork and a kimchi made with fish sauce and salted shrimp mean it is not vegetarian. It is street food and home food, especially in colder months.

Cultural background

The grind should stay a little gritty. A silky batter is a different cake. Vendors press the rounds flat so the beans crust. The name bindaetteok is the one Seoul menus use. Nokdu simply means mung bean.

Why it tastes this way

Mung bean tastes earthy and sweet when it fries, closer to a fritter than to a wheat jeon. Pork adds fat and salt. Kimchi adds acid and mild chile. Sesame oil on the pan perfumes the crust. Because there is no wheat gluten, the cake crumbles at the edge in a way a pajeon does not, and that crumble is part of the pleasure.

How it is made

Soak and coarsely grind mung beans, fold in pork and kimchi, and pan-fry thick savory cakes.

Authentic touch. Grind the beans coarse and drain them well. A wet, fine puree will not crust. Fry in enough oil that the edge sputters.

How it is used

Eat them hot, cut or whole, with a vinegary dip. They are a snack or a side with rice, not a dessert.

Field notes

  • Bindaetteok is a mung-bean pancake. Nokdu is the bean.
  • This version skips wheat flour so the gluten-free tag is honest. Many stalls add a spoonful.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup split yellow mung beans
  • 80 grams ground pork
  • 1/3 cup chopped kimchi with fish sauce and salted shrimp
  • 2 scallions, chopped
  • 1 garlic clove, minced
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Sesame oil and neutral oil

Steps

  1. Rinse and soak the mung beans for 3 hours. Drain very well.
  2. Grind coarsely with a spoon or two of water. The texture should be nubbly, not a smooth milk.
  3. Mix in pork, kimchi, scallions, garlic, and salt.
  4. Heat a pan with neutral oil and a teaspoon of sesame oil. Spread a small thick round.
  5. Fry on medium until both sides are deep brown and the pork is cooked through. Repeat.

Cook the pork until no pink remains in the center of each cake.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of wheat flour, which would remove the gluten-free tag
  • Bean sprouts folded in, a common addition

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Street food
  • Snack
  • Vinegar-soy dip
  • Steamed rice
  • Raw onion
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Russia · No chili heat · 8 filled crepes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Russia · Eastern Europe

Blinchiki

Thin Russian crepes rolled around a sweet farmer-cheese filling.

25 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 filled crepes

History

Blinchiki are the thin unleavened Russian crepes, often filled and rolled, as distinct from thicker yeasted blini. A sweet filling of tvorog, the farmer cheese, is written here. Meat fillings exist and would change the diet. The Ukrainian nalysnyky page is the rolled crepe baked in sour cream. These blinchiki are filled and served after a brief second fry, not baked in a dish of cream.

Cultural background

They are home dessert food and a way to use tvorog. The crepe has to be thin enough to roll without cracking. A dusting of sugar or a spoon of sour cream finishes them.

Why it tastes this way

The crepe is mild and a little eggy. Tvorog is tangy, sweetened just enough, with lemon to lift it. A second fry browns the outside of the roll so you get a crisp seam and a warm cheese center. They taste fresher than a heavy baked pasta, because the sauce is only what you add at the table.

How it is made

Thin wheat crepes are filled with sweetened tvorog, rolled, and fried briefly in butter.

Authentic touch. Drain the tvorog or the filling will burst the crepe. Roll seam down and fry lightly. For the baked sour-cream dish, use the nalysnyky page instead.

How it is used

Warm, with sour cream or sugar, as dessert. A savory meat filling is a different version and not vegetarian.

Field notes

  • Blinchiki are thin and unleavened. Yeasted blini are the other page.
  • This filling is sweet tvorog, a farmer cheese.
  • They are fried after rolling, not baked in a pan of sour cream.

Ingredients

  • 120 g plain wheat flour
  • 2 large eggs
  • 300 ml whole milk
  • 1 pinch fine salt
  • 40 g unsalted butter, divided
  • 300 g tvorog or farmer cheese, drained
  • 2 tbsp sugar
  • 1 tsp lemon zest

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, eggs, milk, and salt. Rest 10 minutes. Cook thin crepes in a little butter.
  2. Mix the tvorog, sugar, and lemon zest. If it is wet, drain it first.
  3. Fill and roll the crepes, seam side down.
  4. Fry the rolls in the remaining butter until the outsides are golden. Serve with sour cream or sugar.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A savory mushroom filling
  • Nalysnyky, the Ukrainian crepes baked in sour cream

Pairings and uses

  • Dessert
  • Savory meal
  • Sour cream
  • Sugar
  • A little jam
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Brazil · No chili heat · 8 filled crepes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Brazil · South America

Brazilian panquecas

Thin wheat crepes rolled around seasoned ground beef and a simple tomato sauce.

20 min prep · 30 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 filled crepes

History

Panqueca in Brazil is a thin wheat crepe, a home and restaurant dish rather than a street tapioca. The usual savory version is rolled around ground beef and often finished with tomato sauce and cheese. This recipe uses ground beef, so it contains meat and is not vegetarian. The batter is wheat, egg, and milk, closer to a European crepe than to anything made from corn or cassava. São Paulo kitchens turn them out for lunch and dinner.

Cultural background

A pan of panquecas is family food: the crepes can be made ahead, filled, and warmed. The beef filling is seasoned with onion, garlic, tomato, and cumin, not with a sweet dessert sauce. Cheese on top is optional and is not required here because the batter already has dairy.

Why it tastes this way

The crepe tastes of milk and browned flour, flexible enough to roll. The beef is savory from onion and garlic, with tomato for acid and cumin for a warm note. There is no chile in the written filling, so the heat stays at none. Salt and black pepper do the rest. The contrast is a mild wheat pancake and a meat filling that has been cooked down until it is not watery.

How it is made

Make a thin wheat crepe batter and cook it in a lightly oiled pan. Simmer ground beef with onion, garlic, and tomato, season it, and roll it inside the crepes.

Authentic touch. Rest the batter so the flour hydrates, and cook the crepes thin. A thick pancake will not roll. Cook the beef until it is browned and cooked through, and let the tomato reduce so the filling does not soak the crepe into mush.

How it is used

Serve two or three rolls for lunch, with the tomato left in the filling rather than a heavy blanket of sauce. A salad on the side is enough.

Field notes

  • This panqueca is a wheat crepe with ground beef. It is not gluten-free and not vegetarian.
  • Tapioca crepes are a different Brazilian page, made from cassava starch.
  • Reduce the tomato so the filling stays thick enough to roll.

Ingredients

  • 120 g wheat flour
  • 1 egg
  • 250 ml milk
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt, plus more for the filling
  • 1 tablespoon oil, plus more for the pan
  • 250 g ground beef
  • 1 small onion, chopped
  • 1 garlic clove, minced
  • 1 tomato, chopped
  • 1/2 teaspoon ground cumin
  • Black pepper

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, egg, milk, 1/4 teaspoon salt, and 1 tablespoon oil into a thin batter. Rest it for 15 minutes.
  2. Fry the onion and garlic in a spoon of oil. Add the beef and cook, breaking it up, until it is browned and cooked through.
  3. Add the tomato, cumin, salt, and pepper. Simmer until the filling is thick, not watery.
  4. Film a skillet with oil and cook thin crepes, flipping once, until spotted. Stack them.
  5. Roll a spoon of beef in each crepe and serve warm.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Shredded chicken is another meat filling and is still not vegetarian.
  • A cheese and tomato filling, with the beef left out, can be vegetarian. It is a different recipe from this one.
  • A sweet panqueca with dulce de leche exists. This page is the savory beef one.

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • White rice
  • A simple green salad
  • Hot sauce at the table, which would add heat
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

South Korea · No chili heat · 2 chive pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

South Korea · East Asia

Buchujeon

A garlic-chive pancake, green and savory, with a light wheat crust and no seafood.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 2 chive pancakes

History

Buchujeon is the garlic-chive pancake. Buchu is the chive, flat and garlicky, not a substitute for the scallion in pajeon. The leaves are cut long, dipped in a thin wheat and egg batter, and fried in loose clusters or one round. It is a side dish on Korean tables and a snack with a soy dip. This recipe has no fish sauce and no shrimp, so it stays vegetarian.

Cultural background

Garlic chives are a spring and summer market bunch. When they are young they are sweet. Older stems are stronger and still correct for this cake. The batter is only a coating. The chive should dominate the pan the way scallion dominates pajeon.

Why it tastes this way

Garlic chive tastes oniony and distinctly garlicky, greener than bulb garlic and wilder than scallion. A thin wheat batter lets that flavor lead and adds a toasted edge. Egg helps the strands hold together. Sesame is absent here so the chive stays clear. A salty, sour dip is the contrast.

How it is made

Coat garlic chives in a thin wheat batter and pan-fry them until the leaves wilt and the batter spots brown.

Authentic touch. Cut the chives long, about the length of a finger, and fry them in a thin coat. A thick dough buries the vegetable that names the dish.

How it is used

Serve as a side with rice and a dip, cut into pieces. It is savory, not a street sweet.

Field notes

  • Buchu is garlic chive. It is a different green from the scallion in pajeon.
  • This recipe stays vegetarian: no fish sauce and no seafood in the batter.

Ingredients

  • 200 grams garlic chives
  • 1/2 cup all-purpose wheat flour
  • 1 egg
  • 1/3 cup cold water
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Neutral oil
  • Soy sauce and rice vinegar for dipping

Steps

  1. Wash the chives and cut them into finger lengths.
  2. Whisk flour, egg, water, and salt into a thin batter. Fold in the chives.
  3. Heat a film of oil over medium-high heat. Spread half the mixture into a loose round.
  4. Cook until the underside browns and the chives soften. Flip carefully and cook through.
  5. Repeat. Serve with a soy and vinegar dip.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A few sliced red chiles for color and mild heat
  • Carrot matchsticks in the cluster

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Snack
  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Soy and vinegar dip
  • Steamed rice
  • A bowl of doenjang-jjigae on the same table
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Russia · No chili heat · 12 blini

Flying Pancakes of the World

Russia · Eastern Europe

Buckwheat blini

Yeasted buckwheat blini, earthier than the wheat cakes, for butter and sour cream.

1 hr 40 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 blini

History

Buckwheat blini, grechnevye bliny, are the other classic Russian blin. Buckwheat and wheat blini both belong to Maslenitsa, butter week, which is not one Tuesday. This page is buckwheat only, so it is gluten-free if the flour was milled apart from wheat. The wheat page is separate. The batter is still yeasted. Sour cream, butter, and, on a festive table, herring or caviar are the toppings. Herring is not in this recipe.

Cultural background

Buckwheat was a peasant staple and a feast grain. The cakes are smaller and more assertive than white blini. They are still butter-week food: dairy, not a lean fast.

Why it tastes this way

Buckwheat tastes earthy and slightly bitter. Yeast adds a sour edge that fits sour cream. Butter softens the bitterness without hiding it. They brown faster than wheat cakes and stay a little drier. Salt in the batter matters more because the grain is strong.

How it is made

Buckwheat flour, yeast, milk, egg, and butter rise into a batter and are cooked as small thick blini.

Authentic touch. Do not sweeten them like dessert crepes. Sour cream and butter are the pairing. If you need them gluten-free, buy buckwheat that was not milled with wheat. A mixed wheat-and-buckwheat batter is honest and is no longer this page.

How it is used

With butter and sour cream during butter week or at any rich breakfast. Smoked fish is an optional topping and adds a fish allergen.

Field notes

  • Buckwheat blini and wheat blini are both traditional. This page is buckwheat.
  • Maslenitsa is a week, not a Tuesday.
  • There is no wheat in this batter. Cross-contact depends on how the flour was milled.

Ingredients

  • 200 g buckwheat flour
  • 1 tsp instant yeast
  • 1 tsp sugar
  • 350 ml warm milk
  • 1 large egg
  • 1/2 tsp fine salt
  • 30 g unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the pan
  • Sour cream, for serving

Steps

  1. Whisk the buckwheat flour, yeast, sugar, milk, egg, salt, and melted butter. Cover until bubbly, about 1 hour. The batter will look darker than a wheat batter.
  2. Stir it down and loosen with a spoon of milk if it is stiff.
  3. Cook small rounds in butter until the tops are set, then turn and brown them.
  4. Serve hot with sour cream and more butter.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Half wheat flour, which removes the gluten-free diet and becomes a mixed blin
  • A spoon of sour cream stirred into the batter

Pairings and uses

  • Feast or fast day
  • Savory meal
  • Sour cream
  • Butter
  • Pickled herring, for a fish plate
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

France · No chili heat · 8 galettes

Flying Pancakes of the World

France · Western Europe

Buckwheat galette

A savory Breton buckwheat crepe, lacy and a little bitter, cooked on a billig.

20 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 galettes

History

In Brittany a galette is the buckwheat crepe, and it is usually savory. Buckwheat became a staple grain of inland Brittany. This page does not assign a century to that shift. The batter here is buckwheat flour, water, egg, and salt, cooked with butter. Wheat crepes are the other page. A galette complète, with ham, cheese, and egg, is also its own page. Use buckwheat milled apart from wheat if you need the cake to be gluten-free.

Cultural background

Galettes are everyday food, sold at markets and creperies with cider. The word galette means other flat cakes elsewhere in France. On this page it means the Breton buckwheat crepe.

Why it tastes this way

Buckwheat tastes earthy and slightly bitter, which is why sugar is the wrong default. Water, not milk, keeps that flavor clear. Butter on the billig browns the lace. An egg makes the sheet less fragile. Salt is not optional. The holes and crisp edge come from a hot surface and a thin batter.

How it is made

Buckwheat flour is whisked with salt, egg, and water, rested, and cooked in a thin lacy round on a buttered griddle.

Authentic touch. A galettière spreads the batter with a rozell, a wooden T, and lifts it with a spanell, a small spatula. A hot pan and a quick spiral are the home version of that pair of tools.

How it is used

Plain with a rub of butter, or as the base for cheese, egg, or ham. It is a savory meal, not a dessert crepe.

Field notes

  • In Brittany, galette means buckwheat and usually savory.
  • The wheat crepe is a different page, and so is the ham-and-egg complète.
  • This batter has no wheat. The gluten-free claim depends on buckwheat that was not milled with wheat.

Ingredients

  • 200 g buckwheat flour
  • 1 large egg
  • 450 ml cold water
  • 1/2 tsp fine salt
  • 30 g unsalted butter, for the pan

Steps

  1. Whisk the buckwheat flour, egg, water, and salt into a thin batter. Rest 30 minutes. It will look darker than a wheat batter.
  2. Heat a wide pan until hot and grease it with butter.
  3. Pour a thin layer, swirl, and cook until the edge crisps and the top looks dry. Turn if you want both sides colored. Many cooks leave the top soft.
  4. Rub with a little butter and serve, or add a savory filling before folding.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Galette complète, with ham, cheese, and an egg
  • A wheat-flour blend, which some shops use and which removes the gluten-free diet

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Salted butter
  • Dry cider
  • Grated firm cheese
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Bulgaria · No chili heat · 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Bulgaria · Eastern Europe

Bulgarian palachinki

Thin Bulgarian pancakes with yogurt in the batter, sweet or with white cheese.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 pancakes

History

Palachinki are Bulgaria's thin pancakes. A spoon of yogurt in the batter is a home habit and is how this page is written. They are eaten with jam or powdered sugar, and also in a savory way with sirene, the brined white cheese. Both servings are real. This recipe writes the sweet jam plate and names sirene as a variation so the ingredients stay a dessert batter. They are not Serbian palačinke and not the same filling as the Croatian page.

Cultural background

They are breakfast, snack, and dessert. Yogurt is a staple in Bulgarian cooking, so it is a natural liquid in a batter. The cakes are still thin, not a risen stack.

Why it tastes this way

Yogurt adds a gentle tang that milk alone does not. The crepe browns in butter and tastes slightly sour under the jam. Sirene, if you choose the savory plate, is salty and briny against that tang. The sweet version lets the jam stay bright.

How it is made

Flour, egg, milk, and yogurt make a thin batter. The crepes are cooked in butter and served with jam.

Authentic touch. Use plain yogurt, not a sweetened one. Keep the cakes thin. If you fill them with sirene, call the plate savory and expect a salty cheese, not a dessert.

How it is used

Rolled with jam, as breakfast or dessert. A sirene filling turns them savory.

Field notes

  • Yogurt in the batter is the Bulgarian home touch on this page.
  • Jam is the sweet serving. Sirene, a brined white cheese, is the savory one.
  • The cakes are thin palachinki, not small yeast pancakes.

Ingredients

  • 150 g plain wheat flour
  • 1 tbsp sugar
  • 1 pinch fine salt
  • 2 large eggs
  • 200 ml whole milk
  • 100 ml plain yogurt
  • 30 g unsalted butter, for the pan
  • Jam, for serving

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, sugar, salt, eggs, milk, and yogurt into a thin batter. Rest 10 minutes.
  2. Cook thin pancakes in butter until both sides are spotted.
  3. Spread with jam, or leave them plain for a savory cheese filling.
  4. Roll or fold and serve.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Crumbled sirene and dill, a savory plate
  • A stack dusted only with sugar

Pairings and uses

  • Dessert
  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Jam
  • Powdered sugar
  • Sirene, for the savory variation
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free
