Nepal · Mild · 4 topped crepes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Nepal · South Asia

Chatamari

A thin Newar rice crepe topped with egg, onion, and a little chile.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 topped crepes

History

Chatamari is a Newar rice crepe from the Kathmandu Valley. English speakers sometimes call it a Nepali pizza. That is a comparison. The Newar name is chatamari. Rice flour batter is cooked like a crepe and topped with egg, minced meat, sugar, or a simpler savory mix, depending on the kitchen and the day. This recipe writes an egg, onion, and chile topping and leaves minced meat as a variation that would add a meat allergen.

Cultural background

Newar feast food and everyday snacks overlap on this pan. A plain sweet chatamari, dusted with sugar, is one old home habit. The topped savory one is what invited the pizza comparison. The comparison is not the recipe.

Why it tastes this way

Rice flour makes a mild, slightly sweet sheet with a chew that wheat crepes do not have. Egg on top adds richness and a soft set. Onion and chile keep it savory. Because the batter is rice and not a sourdough, the tang is missing on purpose. The flavor is the grain, the egg, and the topping, eaten while the edge is still a little crisp.

How it is made

Pour a thin rice-flour crepe, crack an egg onto it with onion and chile, and cover until the white sets.

Authentic touch. Cook the crepe only until it can hold the egg. A brittle cracker cannot carry a soft topping. Medium heat is enough.

How it is used

Cut into wedges if you are sharing. Eat it hot, with a spoon of achar if you want more heat. The cake itself is mild.

Field notes

  • Chatamari is the Newar name. Nepali pizza is only an English comparison.
  • The base is rice flour, cooked as a crepe in the Kathmandu Valley.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup rice flour, with no wheat added
  • 1 1/4 cups water
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 4 eggs
  • 1 small onion, finely chopped
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • Neutral oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Whisk rice flour, salt, and water into a thin batter. Rest 10 minutes.
  2. Oil a pan over medium heat and pour a crepe-sized round. Cook until the top is just set.
  3. Crack an egg on the crepe. Scatter onion and chile over it. Salt lightly.
  4. Cover and cook until the egg white sets and the edge of the rice crepe is dry. The yolk may stay soft.
  5. Slide it out and repeat with the remaining batter and eggs.

Set the egg white fully if you need the egg well cooked.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Minced meat under the egg, which adds a meat allergen
  • A plain crepe sprinkled with sugar and ghee

Pairings and uses

  • Snack
  • Savory meal
  • Feast or fast day
  • Tomato achar
  • A cup of black tea
  • A fresh cucumber salad
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Chile · No chili heat · 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Chile · South America

Chilote milcao

Pan-fried cakes of raw and cooked potato, salty, crisp at the edge, and dense inside.

30 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 cakes

History

Milcao is a potato cake from Chiloé, where potato diversity is old and specific. The dough mixes grated raw potato with mashed cooked potato. The starch from the raw half is what holds the cake together. Some Chilote cooks fold in pork cracklings. This recipe leaves the pork out, so the cakes stay vegetarian and vegan. A curanto, the pit cook of the archipelago, can include milcaos. The method here is the home skillet, not a pit.

Cultural background

On Chiloé, milcaos are a staple next to fish stews and in the curanto, and they are also simply pan-fried for a meal. Castro is a fair place-name for a food that belongs to the whole archipelago. The cake is potato and salt. Wheat never enters the bowl.

Why it tastes this way

Cooked potato brings a soft, familiar mash. Raw potato brings sticky starch and a fresher, sharper taste that mellows as it fries. Salt is the seasoning. Oil browns the outside. Because there is no egg and no wheat, the cake tastes only of potato, with a crisp face and a dense center. Pork cracklings would add salt and fat. They are a variation, not this batch.

How it is made

Grate raw potatoes and save the settled starch. Mash an equal weight of cooked potatoes. Mix them with salt, form cakes, and pan-fry until both sides are crisp and the raw potato is cooked through.

Authentic touch. Grate the raw potatoes, squeeze the liquid into a bowl, and let the starch settle. Pour off the water and put that starch back into the potatoes. Losing it is how the cake falls apart. Use a starchy potato, and mix half raw and half cooked by weight.

How it is used

Serve them hot beside a stew, or with butter if you are willing to add dairy. They are a bread as much as a side dish on a Chilote table.

Field notes

  • Milcao needs both raw and cooked potato. The raw starch is the binder.
  • Let the potato liquid settle and return the starch. Pouring it all away weakens the cake.
  • This batch has no pork. Cracklings are a separate, not-vegetarian variation.

Ingredients

  • 500 g starchy potatoes, peeled and left raw
  • 500 g starchy potatoes, cooked and mashed
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 2 tablespoons neutral oil for the skillet

Steps

  1. Grate the raw potatoes. Squeeze the liquid into a bowl and let it sit 10 minutes. Pour off the water and keep the starch that settled.
  2. Mix the grated potato, the reserved starch, the mashed potato, and the salt into a firm dough.
  3. Form 8 flat cakes.
  4. Pan-fry in oil over medium heat, turning once, until both sides are crisp and the center is cooked through, about 5 minutes a side. Break one open to check that the raw potato has turned tender.
  5. Serve hot.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Chicharrón folded into the dough is traditional in many houses. It adds meat and is not vegetarian.
  • Milcaos can be cooked in a curanto. This recipe stays in a skillet.
  • A little cooked pumpkin in the mash is a home addition on some islands.

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Snack
  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • A fish stew, which adds fish
  • Pebre
  • Butter, which adds dairy
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Colombia · No chili heat · 6 arepas

Flying Pancakes of the World

Colombia · South America

Colombian arepas

A thinner white-corn arepa with cheese in the dough, buttered, beside tomato and scallion hogao.

15 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 3 · 6 arepas

History

In Antioquia the everyday arepa is a thin white-corn round, cooked on a griddle and set beside eggs, beans, or hogao. Cheese may be mixed into the dough. Butter on a hot arepa is a normal breakfast. This is a different plate from the thick split arepa of a Venezuelan counter. Both are corn cakes, and corn cakes in this part of the world are older than wheat hotcakes. The thin Antioquia cake is not a fresh-corn arepa de choclo and not a Mexican tortilla. Precooked cornmeal is the home flour, not nixtamalized masa.

Cultural background

Medellín breakfasts often include an arepa, a small cup of coffee, and eggs. Hogao, scallions and tomato cooked in oil with cumin, is the sauce that shows up next to beans and rice as well. This recipe puts a little cheese in the dough and serves the cake whole, with butter and hogao, rather than splitting it into a sandwich.

Why it tastes this way

A thinner cake dries and toasts more than a thick Caracas arepa, so you taste browned corn at the surface. Cheese in the dough adds salt and a little fat, which helps the round brown. Butter on the hot cake smells milky. Hogao is sweet from cooked tomato, green from scallion, and warm from cumin, without chile heat. The plate is savory and mild.

How it is made

Mix precooked white cornmeal, warm water, salt, and grated cheese. Pat thin rounds, griddle them, and serve with butter and a cooked hogao of scallion, tomato, and cumin.

Authentic touch. Use a bit more water than you would for a thick stuffed arepa, and pat the rounds thinner, under a centimeter. Cook them longer than you think, until the outside is dry and spotted. Hogao should be cooked until the tomato collapses, not served as a raw salsa.

How it is used

Serve the arepas whole at breakfast, with butter melting on top and hogao on the side. Eggs and red beans can share the plate. They are not folded over cheese the way a cachapa is.

Field notes

  • Antioquia's daily arepa is usually thinner than a stuffed Caracas arepa and is often left whole.
  • Hogao is cooked scallion and tomato. It is not a raw Mexican salsa.
  • The flour is precooked cornmeal, not masa harina and not fresh choclo.

Ingredients

  • 200 g precooked white cornmeal
  • 300 ml warm water
  • 3/4 teaspoon salt, divided
  • 80 g grated fresh white cheese
  • 2 scallions, chopped
  • 1 ripe tomato, chopped
  • 1 tablespoon oil, plus butter for the table
  • 1/4 teaspoon ground cumin

Steps

  1. Stir the cornmeal, 1/2 teaspoon salt, cheese, and warm water into a soft dough that can be patted thin. Rest 5 minutes.
  2. Fry the scallions in the oil, add the tomato, cumin, and remaining salt, and cook until the tomato collapses. That is the hogao.
  3. Pat 6 thin rounds. Cook them on a griddle over medium heat until both sides are dry and spotted, several minutes a side.
  4. The cake should be cooked through, not doughy in the center.
  5. Serve whole, with butter and hogao.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Arepa de choclo, on its own page, is fresh corn and a different batter.
  • A coastal arepa de huevo is fried with an egg inside and is not vegetarian.
  • Leave the cheese and butter out, and cook the cake in oil, if you need it dairy-free and vegan.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Hogao and a pat of butter
  • Red beans and a fried egg, which adds egg
  • Small coffee, a tinto
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

China · No chili heat · 4 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

China · East Asia

Cong you bing

A flaky wheat cake layered with scallion and oil, crisp outside and chewy within.

30 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 pancakes

History

Cong you bing is a scallion pancake made from laminated dough, not from a poured batter. Wheat dough is rolled thin, painted with oil, scattered with salt and scallion, rolled into a log, coiled, and rolled out again so the oil and onion form layers. It is then pan-fried. Shanghai is one city strongly associated with it, and northern and Taiwanese kitchens make close versions. The flake comes from the laminating, the same idea as a pastry, done on a griddle.

Cultural background

Street griddles press the coil flat and fry it until the layers separate. At home the dough needs a rest so it rolls without springing back. This is a savory snack or a side, not a dessert and not a crepe. There is no egg in the written recipe.

Why it tastes this way

Oil trapped between sheets of dough fries the layers from the inside, which is why the pancake shatters and then chews. Scallion perfumes those layers instead of sitting in a batter. Salt on the oil makes each leaf savory. The toasted wheat tastes nuttier than a boiled noodle. Because nothing is fermented, the flavor is clean fat, onion, and crust.

How it is made

Laminate a wheat dough with oil, salt, and scallion, coil it, roll it flat, and pan-fry until the layers flake.

Authentic touch. Roll, oil, sprinkle, and coil. If you knead the scallions into a ball of dough, you lose the layers and the cake tastes like a bun. Fry from a cold-medium start or the outside browns before the coil cooks.

How it is used

Cut into wedges and eat hot. It is street food and a side with soup or congee. The crisp does not last.

Field notes

  • Cong you bing is laminated dough. It is not a batter you pour.
  • The coil is what makes the flake. Scallion alone does not.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups all-purpose wheat flour
  • 3/4 cup hot water
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt, plus more for the layers
  • 3 tablespoons neutral oil or sesame oil
  • 1 bunch scallions, finely chopped
  • More oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Stir flour and salt with hot water into a dough. Knead until smooth. Rest 20 minutes.
  2. Divide into four. Roll one piece very thin. Brush with oil, salt it, and cover it with scallion.
  3. Roll the sheet into a log, coil the log into a spiral, and gently roll the spiral into a thick round.
  4. Pan-fry over medium heat in a little oil until both sides are golden and the layers have separated at the edge.
  5. Repeat with the remaining dough. Cut and serve.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A pinch of white pepper in the oil
  • A few drops of chile oil on the finished wedge

Pairings and uses

  • Street food
  • Savory meal
  • Snack
  • A small dish of black vinegar
  • Soy milk
  • Congee
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · About 16 fritters

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Corn oysters

Sweet corn, egg, and pepper, fried in small irregular cakes.

20 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · About 16 fritters

History

Corn oysters are small fried cakes of grated sweet corn, egg, and a little flour. American cookbooks knew them in the nineteenth century. The name comes from their shape, which reminded cooks of fried oysters, and the fritter does not contain shellfish. Urbana, in the Illinois sweet-corn country, is the map pin.

Cultural background

Fresh summer corn is the point. Grating or chopping the kernels releases starch that helps the fritter bind. Canned creamed corn is a duller substitute and is not what the old recipes meant.

Why it tastes this way

Corn juice caramelizes against the pan and tastes sweeter than boiled corn. Egg sets the cake. A small amount of flour keeps it together without making it bready. Black pepper is the seasoning. They taste of corn, not of oyster.

How it is made

Small spoonfuls of grated corn fried in butter.

Authentic touch. Fry spoonfuls, not large pancakes. The irregular oval shape is why they were called oysters. Do not add shellfish.

How it is used

Hot, as a side dish beside ham or tomatoes, or as a snack.

Field notes

  • The name comes from the shape, which looked like a fried oyster to nineteenth-century cooks.
  • There is no shellfish in the fritter.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups fresh corn kernels, from about 4 ears
  • 2 large eggs
  • 1/4 cup all-purpose flour
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1/4 teaspoon black pepper
  • 2 tablespoons unsalted butter

Steps

  1. Grate the corn on the large holes of a box grater, or chop it finely, and catch the juice.
  2. Stir in the eggs, flour, salt, and pepper. The batter should be thick but spoonable. If it is watery, add another teaspoon of flour.
  3. Melt the butter in a skillet over medium heat.
  4. Drop heaping tablespoons into the pan, leaving space between them.
  5. Fry until the underside is brown and the fritter holds together, then flip and cook until set, about 2 minutes per side.
  6. Serve hot. They soften as they cool.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Oil in place of butter makes them dairy-free, if the rest of the recipe stays the same.
  • A sliced scallion makes them more savory.

Pairings and uses

  • Snack
  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Savory meal
  • Sliced tomatoes
  • Salt
  • Ham, if you eat meat
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Mexico · No chili heat · 12 tortillas

Flying Pancakes of the World

Mexico · Mexico & Central America

Corn tortillas

Soft, toasted corn with a faint mineral note from nixtamal, cooked dry on a hot comal.

25 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 tortillas

History

The corn tortilla is the foundational griddle cake of Mexico, older than wheat hotcakes by a very long stretch. The dough is masa: dried corn simmered with an alkali, rested, rinsed, and ground. That process is nixtamalization. Fresh nixtamal, ground wet, is the older method. Masa harina is dried nixtamalized corn, milled and sold as flour, and it is the practical home shortcut. Neither one is a bowl of plain untreated cornmeal.

Cultural background

A comal, a round griddle, sits on the stove in home kitchens and in tortillerías. Tortillas wrap food, accompany stews, and become the next day's chips or enchiladas. This recipe is the plain cake, just masa and water.

Why it tastes this way

Nixtamal gives the tortilla its corn aroma and a slight mineral taste that plain cornmeal never has. The alkali loosens the skins, changes the flavor, and makes the amino acid niacin more available. Cooked dry, the surface smells toasted. There is no wheat gluten to make it bready, so the cake stays flexible only while it is warm and moist.

How it is made

Stir masa harina with warm water and salt, rest it covered, and press thin rounds. Cook on a hot dry comal until the tortilla releases, flips, and puffs in spots.

Authentic touch. The dough should feel like soft clay that does not crack at the edge and does not glue itself to your palm. If it cracks, add water a teaspoon at a time. If it sticks, knead in a sprinkle of masa harina. Keep the comal dry.

How it is used

Eat them warm, wrapped in a cloth so they steam themselves soft. They are the plate under eggs, beans, salsas, and stews, and they are a meal by themselves with salt.

Field notes

  • A tortilla that puffs has enough moisture and a hot enough comal. The puff is steam, not baking powder.
  • Masa harina is already nixtamalized. Do not add cal again, and do not swap in untreated cornmeal.
  • Corn tortillas in this recipe are masa and water. Lard belongs to some flour tortillas, not to this dough.

Ingredients

  • 250 g masa harina
  • 360 ml warm water, plus more if the dough cracks
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Extra masa harina for dusting if the dough sticks

Steps

  1. Stir the masa harina, salt, and warm water until a dough forms. Knead for a minute. It should feel like soft clay.
  2. Cover and rest for 20 minutes so the flour hydrates. Adjust with a spoon of water or a dust of masa harina.
  3. Divide into 12 pieces. Press each between plastic sheets in a tortilla press, or under a heavy skillet, into a thin round.
  4. Cook on a dry comal over medium-high heat for about 45 seconds, flip, cook the second side, then flip once more until the tortilla smells toasted and puffs in places.
  5. Wrap the tortillas in a clean cloth as they come off the heat so they stay soft.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Blue or red masa harina changes the color and keeps the same method.
  • Fresh nixtamal from a mill can replace masa harina. Use less water, because the dough is already wet.
  • A flour tortilla is a different bread, made with wheat and fat in the north.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Frijoles de la olla and a salsa
  • Scrambled eggs and avocado
  • A stew that needs a soft scoop
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Italy · No chili heat · 8 crepes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Italy · Southern Europe

Crespelle

Italian crepes, plain or baked. Crespelle alla fiorentina is one variation, not the only form.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 crepes

History

Crespelle are Italian crepes, thin egg pancakes used both sweet and savory. Crespelle alla fiorentina, filled with spinach and ricotta and baked in béchamel, are a famous Florentine way to serve them. They are one variation, not the only form. This page cooks the plain crepe and writes a simple butter and cheese finish, with the Florentine bake described as a variation you can build. The pin is Florence because of that famous dish, and the crepe itself is cooked across Italy.

Cultural background

Crespelle stand in for pasta in baked dishes and also appear with sugar as a dessert. The batter is the same idea as other European crepes. The Italian part is how they are filled and sauced.

Why it tastes this way

A thin crepe tastes of milk, egg, and browned butter. Parmesan on a hot crepe melts and salts it. That is the simple plate. The Florentine version adds the green bitterness of spinach, the mildness of ricotta, and the nutmeg of béchamel. Both are crespelle. The plain one should still be good with nothing but butter.

How it is made

A thin batter is cooked as crepes and served with butter and grated cheese, or finished as the Florentine variation.

Authentic touch. Cook the plain crepe first and treat alla fiorentina as a variation, not as the definition. If you bake the spinach version, use a béchamel and ricotta, and do not claim it is the only Italian crepe.

How it is used

Hot, as a savory course with cheese, or as the base for a baked filling. Sugar turns the same crepe into a dessert.

Field notes

  • Crespelle alla fiorentina are one baked form, not the only crespella.
  • The same thin crepe is used sweet and savory.
  • This recipe serves the plain crepe with butter and cheese.

Ingredients

  • 125 g plain wheat flour
  • 2 large eggs
  • 300 ml whole milk
  • 1 pinch fine salt
  • 30 g unsalted butter, plus more for the pan
  • 40 g grated Parmesan

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, eggs, milk, and salt into a thin batter. Rest 15 minutes.
  2. Cook eight thin crepes in a lightly buttered pan.
  3. Brush each with butter and sprinkle with Parmesan while hot.
  4. Fold and serve. For the Florentine variation, fill the crepes with seasoned spinach and ricotta, cover with béchamel, and bake until bubbling.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Crespelle alla fiorentina: fill with cooked spinach and ricotta, cover with béchamel and cheese, and bake
  • A sweet crepe with jam

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Dessert
  • Butter and grated Parmesan
  • A spinach and ricotta filling, the Florentine variation
  • Sugar and lemon, for a dessert crepe
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

United Kingdom · No chili heat · 10 oatcakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

Derbyshire oatcakes

A yeasted oatmeal pancake from Derbyshire, a little thicker than the Staffordshire shop cake.

1 hr 20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 10 oatcakes

History

Derbyshire oatcakes are the Peak District's yeasted oatmeal pancakes. They share a method with Staffordshire oatcakes and they are not the same batter. This page uses equal parts oatmeal and wheat flour and an egg, and it cooks a smaller, thicker cake. Bakers in the two counties do not treat the recipes as interchangeable. Neither cake is a Scottish oatcake, which is unleavened and dry.

Cultural background

They are breakfast food in Derbyshire, eaten with butter, cheese, or bacon. The batter is mixed ahead so the yeast can work. A cold kitchen will take longer than an hour.

Why it tastes this way

The extra wheat and the egg make a cake that holds together when it is thicker. Oats keep it soft. Yeast keeps the sweetness in check. You taste grain first, then the salty filling.

How it is made

Oatmeal, wheat flour, yeast, egg, and warm milk ferment into a batter. Spoonfuls cook on a greased griddle until set.

Authentic touch. Do not roll these as thin as a Potteries oatcake. If the batter will not drop from a spoon in a thick ribbon, add a splash of milk.

How it is used

Buttered, or filled with cheese. They are a morning savory cake, not a dessert stack.

Field notes

  • Derbyshire and Staffordshire both keep yeasted oat pancakes, with different local batters.
  • This batter includes an egg. The Staffordshire page does not.
  • They are soft griddle cakes, not the crisp Scottish biscuit.

Ingredients

  • 150 g fine oatmeal
  • 150 g plain wheat flour
  • 1 tsp instant yeast
  • 1 tsp sugar
  • 1 large egg
  • 400 ml warm milk
  • 1/2 tsp fine salt
  • Butter, for the griddle

Steps

  1. Mix the oatmeal, flour, yeast, sugar, egg, milk, and salt. Cover until the batter is bubbly, about 1 hour.
  2. Stir it down. It should drop from a spoon in a ribbon. Add a spoon of milk if it is stiff.
  3. Grease a medium-hot griddle. Cook small rounds until the top sets, then turn and brown the second side.
  4. Serve warm with butter or cheese. They reheat in a dry pan.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • The oat-heavier Staffordshire oatcake, made to fold
  • A spoon of honey on a plain one

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Butter
  • Cheese
  • Bacon, for a meat plate
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Ukraine · No chili heat · 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Ukraine · Eastern Europe

Deruny

Ukrainian grated-potato pancakes, fried crisp and served with sour cream.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 pancakes

History

Deruny are Ukrainian potato pancakes made from grated raw potato, onion, egg, and flour. They are everyday food, served with sour cream. They share a technique with Polish placki, Belarusian draniki, and Ashkenazi latkes, and they are not the same plate. Latkes on this journal are the Hanukkah cake, with that history. Deruny are the Ukrainian home pancake. Flour means they are not gluten-free.

Cultural background

A cast-iron pan and a fine grater are the tools. Some cooks add a little salo, cured pork fat. This recipe fries in oil and keeps the cake vegetarian, with sour cream at the table.

Why it tastes this way

Onion and potato fry together so the edge is sweet and crisp. Egg and flour hold the shreds. Sour cream cools the crust. Salt and pepper are the seasoning. If the batter sits, the potato darkens and the flavor dulls, so cook it promptly.

How it is made

Potato and onion are grated, mixed with egg, flour, and salt, and fried in oil until brown.

Authentic touch. Grate onion into the potato and fry the cakes thin enough to crisp. Sour cream is the serving. A pork-fat fry is a real variation and is not this vegetarian recipe.

How it is used

Hot, with sour cream, as a meal. They are savory.

Field notes

  • Deruny are the Ukrainian grated-potato pancakes.
  • They are related to draniki, placki, and latkes, and the histories are not identical.
  • This recipe is fried in oil and served with sour cream.

Ingredients

  • 600 g starchy potatoes, peeled
  • 1 small onion
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tbsp plain wheat flour
  • 1 tsp fine salt
  • 1/4 tsp black pepper
  • Neutral oil, for frying
  • Sour cream, for serving

Steps

  1. Grate the potatoes and onion. Squeeze lightly, leaving them moist. Stir in the egg, flour, salt, and pepper.
  2. Heat a shallow layer of oil over medium-high heat.
  3. Fry flattened rounds until deep brown on both sides and cooked through.
  4. Drain briefly and serve with sour cream.

Stay with the hot oil. Cut one cake open to be sure the potato is cooked.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Fried in salo, which adds meat and removes the vegetarian diet
  • Belarusian draniki, the next-door potato cake with its own page

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Sour cream
  • Dill
  • A mushroom sauce
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Dominican Republic · No chili heat · 6 flat rounds

Flying Pancakes of the World

Dominican Republic · Caribbean

Dominican casabe

A thin, dry cassava flatbread, lightly salty, crisp at the edge and bland in a way that suits a stew.

15 min prep · 30 min cook · Serves 6 · 6 flat rounds

History

Casabe comes from Taíno cooking on the island. People in the Dominican Republic still make it and buy it. What you find in a shop today is a processed flatbread: cassava that has already been turned into flour or into packaged rounds, then cooked until dry. This recipe uses commercial cassava flour. It does not reconstruct a pre-colonial kitchen, and it does not teach you to process raw bitter cassava at home. Wheat breads arrived later. Casabe is the older starch on this table.

Cultural background

Casabe is everyday bread next to beans, eggs, or a stew, and it keeps because it is dried on the griddle rather than left soft. Treat it as a plain cracker-bread, not as a symbol. The Indigenous origin is specific: Taíno. The modern product is a factory or home griddle food made from flour that is already safe to cook.

Why it tastes this way

Once the moisture is cooked out, cassava starch tastes mild, almost neutral, with a faint sweetness and a dry crunch. Salt is the seasoning. There is no fermentation, no wheat, and no fat required in the dough. It tastes quiet on purpose, so the beans or the stew on top can be the loud part.

How it is made

Mix commercial cassava flour, salt, and water into a stiff paste. Press very thin rounds and cook them on a dry skillet until crisp and dry.

Authentic touch. Spread the dough thinner than a tortilla and cook it longer, until it is dry and snaps. If you take it off while it is still flexible, you have made a different cake. Do not expect it to puff.

How it is used

Break a round and use it to scoop beans, avocado, or a sauced dish. Packaged casabe, already baked in large circles, is the same food if you are not cooking.

Field notes

  • Today's casabe is a processed flatbread. Naming the Taíno origin does not turn a bag of flour into a ceremony.
  • Raw bitter cassava can be poisonous. Use commercial cassava flour or packaged casabe.
  • Casabe should dry out on the griddle. A soft tortilla texture means it needs more time.

Ingredients

  • 250 g commercial cassava flour
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 180 ml water
  • Extra commercial cassava flour if the dough sticks to your hands

Steps

  1. Stir the commercial cassava flour, salt, and water into a stiff dough. Add a spoon of water only if it will not hold together.
  2. Divide into 6 pieces and press each into a very thin round between plastic sheets.
  3. Cook on a dry skillet over medium-low heat. Turn often.
  4. Keep cooking until the round is dry, spotted, and crisp at the edge. This takes longer than a wheat tortilla.
  5. Cool on a rack. Serve with beans or another savory plate, or store the dry rounds in a tin.

Raw bitter cassava can be poisonous. This recipe uses commercial cassava flour or packaged casabe and does not teach home processing of raw cassava.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A brush of oil after cooking is a modern table habit. The older shop casabe is usually dry.
  • Whole packaged rounds can be warmed in a dry pan instead of made from flour.
  • Leave the salt out only if the stew is very salty. The bread itself is plain.

Pairings and uses

  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Snack
  • Savory meal
  • Habichuelas and rice
  • Avocado and a slice of cheese, which adds dairy
  • Scrambled eggs
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Netherlands · No chili heat · 4 large pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Netherlands · Western Europe

Dutch pannenkoeken

Large, thin Dutch pancakes, plate-sized, with syrup, apple, or bacon.

10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 large pancakes

History

A Dutch pannenkoek is a large thin pancake, often the size of the plate. Pannenkoeken restaurants cook them to order, sweet or savory. Stroop, a dark Dutch syrup, and powdered sugar are the usual sweet finish. Bacon and cheese are the usual savory ones. This page writes a plain batter and a syrup finish, and names bacon as a variation so the base recipe stays vegetarian. Poffertjes, the small yeast cakes, are a different pan and a different page.

Cultural background

They are family restaurant food and home food. Children and adults share the same batter. The pancake is dinner as often as it is dessert, which is why both uses are tagged.

Why it tastes this way

The batter is a little thicker than a French crepe, so the crumb is soft with a lacier edge. Butter browns it. Syrup adds bitterness and molasses-like depth that maple syrup does not quite copy. A pinch of salt in a sweet pancake keeps it from tasting flat.

How it is made

A batter of flour, egg, and milk is cooked as one wide pancake, then finished with syrup or sugar.

Authentic touch. Cook one large pancake per person rather than a stack of small ones. Spread inclusions such as apple on the raw batter in the pan, instead of stirring a heavy handful into the bowl.

How it is used

Flat on a plate, sweet with stroop or sugar, or savory if you add bacon and cheese. Cut into wedges if you share.

Field notes

  • A pannenkoek is often cooked one to a plate.
  • Poffertjes are the small dimpled cakes, not this pancake.
  • Stroop, a dark syrup, is the usual sweet topping.

Ingredients

  • 200 g plain wheat flour
  • 2 large eggs
  • 500 ml whole milk
  • 1 pinch fine salt
  • 30 g unsalted butter, for the pan
  • Dark syrup, for serving

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, eggs, milk, and salt into a smooth batter, a little thicker than a crepe batter.
  2. Heat a wide pan and melt a little butter.
  3. Pour enough batter to cover the pan in a thick crepe layer. Cook until the top is set and the underside is brown, then turn and finish.
  4. Slide onto a plate and serve with syrup. Repeat for each person.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Bacon and cheese, which adds meat and is no longer vegetarian
  • Apple pieces pressed into the uncooked top

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Dessert
  • Breakfast
  • Dutch stroop or another dark syrup
  • Powdered sugar
  • Apple slices cooked on the batter
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 4 egg cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Egg foo young

A thick egg pancake with bean sprouts and onion, covered in a brown gravy.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 2 · 4 egg cakes

History

Egg foo young is a Chinese-American restaurant dish. It took shape in diaspora dining rooms in the United States, including Chinatown kitchens such as those in San Francisco, as a thick egg pancake under a brown gravy. Cantonese cooking has egg dishes with related names. The gravy-covered pancake on American menus is not a single mainland classic with one agreed recipe. This page says that plainly. The written cake uses egg, bean sprouts, and mushrooms. Shrimp or roast pork versions are common on menus and would add shellfish or meat.

Cultural background

The pancake is deeper than a crepe and firmer than a French omelet. Gravy is part of the plate, ladled on when the cake is browned. It is dinner-menu food and also a late-night order. The recipe here is home cooking in that restaurant style, not a claim about one village.

Why it tastes this way

Egg is the main flavor, fried at the edge so it tastes browned and rich. Bean sprouts add water and a fresh crunch that keeps the cake from being dense. Onion and mushroom make it savory. Soy sauce in the gravy is salty and dark, thickened so it clings. The combination reads as a Chinese-American restaurant plate: soft egg, a little vegetable, and enough sauce to eat with rice.

How it is made

Pan-fry thick egg cakes with bean sprouts and onion, and serve them under a soy and mushroom gravy.

Authentic touch. Fry the cakes in a shallow film of oil until the outside is truly brown. A pale, steamed-looking round will not hold the gravy. Keep the gravy moving so the cornstarch does not lump.

How it is used

Serve two cakes to a person with rice, gravy spooned over. It is a savory restaurant-style plate.

Field notes

  • Egg foo young is a Chinese-American restaurant pancake, not one mainland classic.
  • The brown gravy is part of the plate people order in the United States.

Ingredients

  • 4 eggs
  • 1 cup bean sprouts
  • 2 scallions, chopped
  • 1/4 onion, sliced
  • 4 mushrooms, sliced
  • 1 tablespoon soy sauce
  • 1 teaspoon cornstarch
  • 3/4 cup water or unsalted vegetable stock
  • Neutral oil and a pinch of sugar

Steps

  1. Beat the eggs with a pinch of salt. Stir in bean sprouts, scallions, onion, and half the mushrooms.
  2. Oil a pan over medium heat. Pour a quarter of the egg mixture and cook it as a thick pancake until the underside browns. Flip and brown the other side. Repeat.
  3. In a small pot, fry the remaining mushrooms in a teaspoon of oil. Add soy sauce, sugar, and water.
  4. Stir cornstarch with a spoon of water, pour it in, and simmer until the gravy thickens.
  5. Serve the cakes with the gravy over them and rice beside.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A few shrimp in the egg, which adds shellfish
  • Roast pork in the egg, which adds meat

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Street food
  • Steamed rice
  • A vinegary cabbage slaw
  • Hot sauce on the side
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Sri Lanka · No chili heat · 4 egg hoppers

Flying Pancakes of the World

Sri Lanka · South Asia

Egg hoppers

A coconut rice hopper with a soft egg set in the center, crisp at the rim.

30 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 egg hoppers

History

An egg hopper is a Sri Lankan hopper with an egg broken into the thick center so the white sets against the rice and the yolk stays soft. The batter is the same fermented rice and coconut milk used for plain hoppers. The egg is the addition that gives this page its own name. It is a breakfast from hopper stands in Colombo and elsewhere on the island. It is not a string hopper, and it is not a wheat omelet.

Cultural background

The cook pours and swirls the batter, then cracks the egg at once so it cooks under the lid with the rice. A firm yolk is a missed hopper. Chile sambol is served beside it, not baked inside, in the usual plate.

Why it tastes this way

The rice bowl is lightly sour and sweet from coconut. The egg adds richness the plain hopper does not have, and a soft yolk sauces the spongy center. The crisp rim stays free of egg if the crack is careful, so you still get the cracker edge. Salt and the heat of a sambol finish the plate.

How it is made

Swirl fermented rice batter in a hopper pan, crack an egg into the center, and cover until the white sets.

Authentic touch. Crack the egg into the center puddle, not onto the thin wall. An egg on the lace burns and welds the hopper to the pan.

How it is used

Eat at once with a spicy onion sambol. The yolk should still move. Dried-fish sambols add a fish allergen on the side.

Field notes

  • The egg goes into the center of a rice hopper. It does not replace the rice batter.
  • String hoppers are pressed rice noodles, a different dish from this pan.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups fermented rice and coconut-milk hopper batter
  • 4 eggs
  • Salt and pepper
  • Neutral oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Use hopper batter that has fermented until bubbly, as on the plain hopper page.
  2. Heat and grease a hopper pan. Pour a ladle and swirl so the center remains deep.
  3. Immediately crack one egg into that center. Sprinkle a little salt and pepper.
  4. Cover and cook on medium-low until the rim is crisp, the egg white is set, and the yolk is still soft.
  5. Loosen the rim and slide the hopper out. Repeat with the remaining eggs.

Cook the egg white until it is set. People who need fully cooked yolks should leave the lid on longer.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A pinch of salt and pepper on the yolk
  • A firmer yolk if you cover the pan longer

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Onion sambol
  • Seeni sambol
  • Banana and a second plain hopper
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Eritrea · No chili heat · About 8 large sheets

Flying Pancakes of the World

Eritrea · East Africa

Eritrean taita

Sour teff flatbread with a spongy top, meant to carry tsebhi.

20 min prep · 40 min cook · Serves 6 · About 8 large sheets

History

In Eritrea the fermented teff bread is called taita. It belongs to the same teff family as Ethiopian injera and uses the same grain, but the meal around it is organized as tsebhi, the Tigrinya name for the stews. Zigni is a berbere stew, alicha is the milder one, hamli is greens, and shiro is a paste of ground chickpeas or broad beans. This page is that Asmara bread, not a second copy of the Ethiopian platter.

Cultural background

Taita is torn at the table and used to scoop tsebhi from a shared tray. The bread is the plate. Bakeries in Asmara sometimes blend wheat into the batter for a paler, less fragile sheet. This home recipe does not. It stays with teff.

Why it tastes this way

The sourness comes from a teff batter that has had time to ferment, and the earthy taste is the grain itself. Cooking on one side leaves a soft, holey top that soaks up a rich tsebhi. A wheat blend tastes milder and tighter. This version keeps the sharper teff flavor because nothing else is in the flour.

How it is made

Mix teff flour and water, cover the bowl, and let it sour until bubbly. Salt it at the end. Cook thin rounds on a mogogo, the Tigrinya clay griddle, or on a wide nonstick pan. Cover each round and cook one side until the surface is set and full of holes. Stack the sheets in a cloth. The stews are cooked apart from the bread.

Authentic touch. Serve the bread with at least two tsebhi, one hot with berbere and one milder, so the same piece of taita tastes different from bite to bite. Do not flip the bread.

How it is used

Taita is the base of an Eritrean meal: a tray of tsebhi zigni, hamli, and shiro, with the bread torn as the utensil. It is an accompaniment, not a rolled snack. The stews can be meat or fasting dishes. The bread recipe itself has neither.

Field notes

  • Taita is the Tigrinya name for this teff bread. The stews beside it are tsebhi.
  • The clay griddle is a mogogo. The bread cooks on one side.
  • South of the city, the same teff batter is still the everyday starch, with the stew changing by season.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups teff flour (about 280 g)
  • 3 1/4 cups water
  • 1/2 teaspoon fine salt
  • A few drops of neutral oil if the griddle sticks

Steps

  1. Stir the teff flour and water into a lump-free batter a little thinner than pancake batter. Cover it. Do not add wheat flour.
  2. Leave it at cool room temperature until it is sour and foamy, usually two or three days. Stir once each day.
  3. Add the salt. Thin with a splash of water if the batter no longer pours in a ribbon.
  4. Heat a mogogo or a wide nonstick skillet. Spread a ladle of batter from the outside inward into a round that reaches the edges.
  5. Cover and cook until the top changes from glossy to set and the holes stay open. Do not flip. Lay each sheet on a cloth and cover it while the next one cooks.

Gluten-free only if the teff was not milled with wheat. Berbere stews served alongside can be very hot even though the bread is not.

Keep taita wrapped so it does not dry out. Refrigerate leftovers and warm them in a covered pan. Do not leave a meat stew on the bread at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of an older sour batter makes the next batch more reliable.
  • Some Asmara bakeries add wheat. That bread is not gluten-free and is not written here.
  • Brown teff gives a darker sheet than ivory teff. Both are taita.

Pairings and uses

  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Savory meal
  • Tsebhi zigni, a berbere stew
  • Hamli, cooked greens
  • Shiro, a chickpea or broad-bean stew
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Ethiopia · No chili heat · About 8 small rounds

Flying Pancakes of the World

Ethiopia · East Africa

Ethiopian kita

Mild, soft teff flatbread with a light toasted face and no sour ferment.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · About 8 small rounds

History

Kita is a quick unfermented flatbread, cooked the day it is mixed. It is not injera. Injera needs days of souring and is cooked on one side. Kita is mixed, rested only long enough for the flour to drink the water, and cooked on a skillet. Torn kita becomes chechebsa, tossed with spiced butter and berbere, or kita firfir, soaked in a spiced sauce. This recipe uses teff and stays unfermented.

Cultural background

Kita is a breakfast and a way to use a hot pan when there is no sour batter waiting. In home kitchens it is torn by hand. The bread itself is plain. The chile and the spiced butter arrive when it is turned into chechebsa or firfir.

Why it tastes this way

Because the batter is unfermented, kita tastes of toasted teff rather than of lactic sourness. There are no ayen. The crumb is closer to a soft griddled flatbread than to a sponge. If it sits for days it will start to become something else, which is why this method cooks it the same morning.

How it is made

Stir teff flour, water, and salt into a thick batter and rest it for about ten minutes so the flour hydrates. Do not ferment it. Cook small rounds on a moderate skillet, flipping once, until both faces are set and lightly spotted. Tear the bread while it is warm if you are making chechebsa or kita firfir.

Authentic touch. Say it plainly while you cook: this dough is unfermented. Tear the finished bread for chechebsa or kita firfir instead of leaving it as a neat stack. A fermented sour loaf is injera, and it does not belong on this page.

How it is used

Plain kita is breakfast with tea. Torn and mixed with niter kibbeh, the spiced clarified butter, and berbere, it becomes chechebsa. Torn into a berbere sauce, it becomes kita firfir. Those finishes add dairy and chile. They are not in the bread.

Field notes

  • Kita is unfermented. Leaving the batter for days would turn the method into a different bread.
  • Chechebsa is torn kita tossed with spiced butter and berbere.
  • Kita firfir is the torn bread soaked in a spiced sauce rather than folded.

Ingredients

  • 1 1/2 cups teff flour (about 210 g)
  • 1 1/2 cups water
  • 1/2 teaspoon fine salt
  • 1 teaspoon neutral oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Stir the teff flour, water, and salt into a thick batter with no dry pockets. This dough is unfermented. Do not add yeast or sourdough.
  2. Rest it for 10 minutes so the flour hydrates. Do not leave it overnight.
  3. Heat a skillet over medium heat and wipe it with the oil. Spread a small ladle of batter into a round about the width of a saucer.
  4. Cook until the underside is set and spotted, then flip once and cook the second side. Kita is cooked on both sides, unlike injera.
  5. Stack the rounds under a cloth. Tear them for chechebsa or kita firfir, or eat them plain while warm.

The bread is gluten-free only if the teff flour is free of wheat. Berbere used later for chechebsa is a chile blend and can be very hot.

Eat kita the day it is cooked. Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Do not ferment leftover batter and call it this recipe. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Wheat kita is common and must be tagged as wheat. It is not gluten-free. This written recipe does not use it.
  • Chechebsa uses spiced butter, so that version is not vegan or dairy-free.
  • A drier dough makes a firmer bread for tearing. A looser batter makes a softer round.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Tea
  • Niter kibbeh and berbere, for chechebsa
  • A berbere sauce, for kita firfir
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

France · No chili heat · 8 rolled crepes

Flying Pancakes of the World

France · Western Europe

Ficelle picarde

A Picardy crepe rolled around ham and mushrooms in cream, then baked with cheese.

35 min prep · 30 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 rolled crepes

History

Ficelle picarde is a baked stuffed crepe from Picardy, in northern France. A thin wheat crepe is rolled around a slice of ham and a duxelles of mushrooms bound with cream, then covered with more cream and cheese and baked. Ficelle means string, which fits the thin rolled shape. Stories that name a single inventor are disputed, so this page describes the dish without picking one. It is a first course in Picard restaurants, not a Breton galette.

Cultural background

Picardy cooking uses cream, mushrooms, and ham. The ficelle is that pantry rolled inside a crepe and browned. It is served hot, as a starter or a light meal with salad.

Why it tastes this way

The crepe goes crisp at the edges in the oven while the mushroom cream stays soft. Ham salts the mild mushrooms. Cheese browns and adds a savory crust. Nutmeg, if you use a scrape, should stay in the background. The flavor is cream and browned cheese more than sugar. There is no sugar in this recipe.

How it is made

Crepes are filled with ham and a mushroom-cream mixture, laid in a buttered dish, covered with cream and cheese, and baked until bubbling.

Authentic touch. Cook the mushroom moisture out before you add cream. A wet filling makes the crepe soggy in the dish. Roll them tight, seam down.

How it is used

Hot, as a first course or a light savory meal. It is not a sweet crepe.

Field notes

  • Ficelle means string. The crepe is rolled thin.
  • This is a Picardy baked dish, not a Breton galette.
  • Single-inventor stories are disputed, so the page does not name one.

Ingredients

  • 100 g plain wheat flour
  • 2 large eggs
  • 250 ml whole milk
  • 1 pinch fine salt
  • 40 g unsalted butter, divided
  • 250 g mushrooms, finely chopped
  • 200 ml heavy cream
  • 4 thin slices cooked ham
  • 60 g grated firm cheese

Steps

  1. Make a thin batter with the flour, eggs, milk, and salt. Rest 15 minutes and cook eight small crepes in a little butter.
  2. Fry the mushrooms in the remaining butter with a pinch of salt until the liquid has gone and they start to brown. Stir in half the cream and cook until thick.
  3. Lay a piece of ham and a spoon of mushrooms on each crepe. Roll tightly and place seam side down in a buttered baking dish.
  4. Pour over the rest of the cream, add the cheese, and bake at 200°C until bubbling and spotted brown, about 15 minutes.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Leeks instead of part of the mushrooms
  • A meat-free filling of mushrooms and cheese, which can be vegetarian if the crepe has no ham

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • A green salad
  • A dry white wine from the north, if you drink wine
  • Nothing sweet on the same plate
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Albania · No chili heat · 1 round dish, 8 wedges

Flying Pancakes of the World

Albania · Eastern Europe

Flija

A layered Albanian batter dish, baked in many thin sheets until the top is golden.

20 min prep · 1 hr 20 min cook · Serves 8 · 1 round dish, 8 wedges

History

Flija, also spelled fli, is a layered dish of a thin flour-and-water batter, cooked in many successive sheets with butter or cream between them. It is food of Albanian communities in Kosovo, northern Albania, and North Macedonia. This page does not claim that a single village invented it. The older tool is a saç, a metal lid heaped with embers. The recipe here uses an oven, and it says so. The country pin is Albania. North Macedonia is marked as well. Kosovo is named in the place because that is where the dish is widely cooked.

Cultural background

Flija is feast and family food, made when there is time to stand by the pan and paint on layer after layer. It is cut in wedges. Yogurt or kaymak is served beside it. The dish is savory, not a sugar dessert.

Why it tastes this way

Each layer sets before the next goes on, so you get soft sheets and drier, toasted ones in the same wedge. Butter between layers is the richness. The batter itself is only flour, water, and salt, so the flavor is toasted wheat and butter, not egg. Yogurt on the side adds acid. If you rush the layers, it becomes one pudding instead of a stack of sheets.

How it is made

A thin batter is brushed into a buttered pan in repeated layers. Each layer is baked until set, with dots of butter, until the dish is thick and the top is brown.

Authentic touch. Paint thin layers and let each one dry on top before you add the next. The saç and live embers are the older heat. This oven method is a home stand-in, not a claim that every kitchen abandoned the lid. Do not name one village as the inventor.

How it is used

Hot, in wedges, with yogurt, as a shared savory dish.

Field notes

  • Flija is layered batter, not a single poured pancake.
  • It is shared across Albanian communities in Kosovo, northern Albania, and North Macedonia. No one village is credited here.
  • A saç lid with embers is the older tool. This recipe uses an oven.

Ingredients

  • 400 g plain wheat flour
  • 1 tsp fine salt
  • 700 ml water
  • 150 g unsalted butter, melted
  • Plain yogurt, for serving

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, salt, and water into a thin batter, like cream. Rest it for 15 minutes.
  2. Heat the oven to 220°C. Butter a wide round pan and brush in a thin layer of batter. Bake until that layer is set, about 5 minutes.
  3. Brush on butter, then another thin layer of batter. Return it to the oven. Repeat until the batter is used and the top is golden. Each layer should set before the next.
  4. Rest 10 minutes, cut in wedges, and serve with yogurt.

A saç lid and live embers are dangerously hot. This recipe uses the oven. The pan will be hot when you brush on new layers.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Cream brushed between layers instead of only butter
  • The saç-and-ember method, which is hotter to manage and not written as the home steps

Pairings and uses

  • Feast or fast day
  • Savory meal
  • Plain yogurt
  • Kaymak or thick cream
  • A salad of pickles
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Mexico · No chili heat · 8 tortillas

Flying Pancakes of the World

Mexico · Mexico & Central America

Flour tortillas

Soft, wheaty, and a little rich from fat, with browned spots and a flexible crumb.

40 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 tortillas

History

Flour tortillas belong to northern Mexico, especially Sonora, where wheat became a staple crop. The dough is wheat flour, fat, salt, and water, rolled thin and cooked on a comal. Corn tortillas are older. Wheat hotcakes are later still. This northern flatbread is its own thing: a pliable wheat wrap for machaca, beans, and burros, not a corn tortilla made with different flour.

Cultural background

In Sonora and neighboring northern states, the flour tortilla is daily bread. Home cooks roll them larger than the corn tortillas of the center and south. This version uses vegetable shortening so the recipe stays vegetarian. Many family recipes use lard instead.

Why it tastes this way

Wheat gluten, handled lightly, stretches into a soft sheet instead of a tough cracker. Fat shortens that gluten, which is why the tortilla bends without snapping while it is warm. The comal adds toasted spots. There is no egg and no baking powder in this dough, so the flavor stays plain and wheaty, a carrier for the filling.

How it is made

Cut shortening into flour and salt, add warm water, knead briefly, and rest. Roll thin rounds and cook them on a hot comal until they blister.

Authentic touch. Rest the dough at least 30 minutes before rolling. The flour hydrates, and the rounds relax instead of springing back. Cook them dry or with only a trace of fat. Stack them in a cloth at once.

How it is used

Use them warm as the wrap for beans, eggs, or stews, or fold one around cheese while it is still hot. They are a bread, not a dessert.

Field notes

  • Sonoran flour tortillas are often much larger than a central Mexican corn tortilla.
  • This dough uses vegetable shortening. Swap in lard and the tortilla is no longer vegetarian.
  • Corn cakes in Mexico are older than this wheat flatbread and older than wheat hotcakes.

Ingredients

  • 250 g wheat flour, plus more for rolling
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 40 g vegetable shortening
  • 150 ml warm water

Steps

  1. Stir the flour and salt. Rub in the shortening until the mixture looks like coarse crumbs.
  2. Add the warm water and knead just until a smooth, soft dough forms. Cover and rest for 30 minutes.
  3. Divide into 8 pieces. Roll each into a thin round on a floured board.
  4. Cook on a hot dry comal for about 30 to 45 seconds a side, until brown spots appear and the surface blisters.
  5. Stack the tortillas in a cloth so they steam and stay flexible.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Lard in place of vegetable shortening is common in northern kitchens. That version contains meat and is not vegetarian.
  • A spoon of wheat flour more or less sets the size. Keep the dough soft, not wet.
  • Some cooks add a pinch of baking powder for a softer puff. This recipe leaves it out.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Mashed beans and a mild salsa
  • Scrambled eggs and roasted chile
  • Carne asada, on a plate that is no longer vegetarian
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

France · No chili heat · 4 filled galettes

Flying Pancakes of the World

France · Western Europe

Galette complète

A buckwheat galette with ham, cheese, and an egg cooked in the middle.

20 min prep · 30 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 filled galettes

History

A galette complète is the Breton creperie standard: a buckwheat galette topped with ham, a firm cheese, and an egg that cooks on the hot cake. Complète means the usual complete filling, not a claim that other fillings are incomplete meals. The batter matches the buckwheat galette page. The ham makes this a meat dish. Stories about a single creperie inventing the trio are not needed to cook it, and this page does not pick one.

Cultural background

It is lunch and dinner in Brittany, folded in a square so the yolk stays visible, and served with cider. The egg should still be soft unless you ask for it set.

Why it tastes this way

Buckwheat is bitter and toasted. Ham is salty. Cheese melts into fat. The egg yolk sauces the dry lace of the galette. Butter on the griddle ties them together. A hard, dry yolk misses the point of the dish.

How it is made

A buckwheat galette is cooked, then ham and cheese go on, an egg is cracked on top, and the cake is covered until the white sets and folded square.

Authentic touch. Crack the egg onto the galette while the top is still hot, cover the pan for a minute, and fold the four sides in after the white sets. Use a firm cheese such as Gruyère. Do not build this on a sweet wheat crepe.

How it is used

As a meal, with a green salad and cider. It is not a dessert.

Field notes

  • Complète means ham, cheese, and egg on buckwheat.
  • The egg is cooked on the galette, not fried in a separate pan.
  • Fold it square so the yolk stays in the middle.

Ingredients

  • 200 g buckwheat flour
  • 1 large egg, for the batter, plus 4 eggs for the filling
  • 450 ml cold water
  • 1/2 tsp fine salt
  • 40 g unsalted butter
  • 4 thin slices cooked ham
  • 120 g grated firm cheese, such as Gruyère

Steps

  1. Whisk the buckwheat flour, the batter egg, water, and salt. Rest 30 minutes.
  2. Cook a thin galette in butter on a hot pan until the top is set.
  3. Lay on a slice of ham and a quarter of the cheese. Crack an egg in the center. Cover the pan and cook until the white sets and the yolk is still soft.
  4. Fold the four sides over the filling, slide onto a plate, and repeat.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Cheese and egg without ham, which drops the meat allergen and can be vegetarian
  • A wheat-flour galette, which is a different batter and not gluten-free

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Dry cider
  • A green salad
  • A second galette if the table is sharing
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

France · No chili heat · 4 wraps

Flying Pancakes of the World

France · Western Europe

Galette-saucisse

A pork sausage rolled in a buckwheat galette, the Rennes street snack.

25 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 wraps

History

Galette-saucisse is a buckwheat galette rolled around a hot pork sausage. It is street food in Rennes and across Ille-et-Vilaine, sold at markets and at football matches. It is not the ham-and-egg complète, and it is not a sweet crepe. This recipe grinds the sausage at home from pork, salt, and pepper so the filling has no wheat filler. A shop sausage can contain cereal. If you use one, read the label before you call the plate gluten-free.

Cultural background

It is food you eat in your hand, often with a line of mustard. Mustard is optional here and, if you add it, it is a mustard allergen. The galette has to be flexible enough to wrap a hot sausage without cracking.

Why it tastes this way

Buckwheat is earthy and dry at the edge. Pork is salty and rich. Together they do not need a sauce. The contrast is the point: a crisp galette and a juicy sausage. Pepper in the meat is enough heat for this page, which stays at no chili.

How it is made

A buckwheat galette batter is cooked in thin rounds. Pork is seasoned, shaped into thin sausages, fried, and rolled inside the galettes.

Authentic touch. Wrap the sausage while both parts are hot, galette first, sausage inside, seam down. A cold sausage in a cold galette is a different and worse snack. Cook the galette in oil here so the cake itself stays dairy-free.

How it is used

In the hand, as street food or a match-day snack. It is a meat meal.

Field notes

  • This is the Rennes street wrap: sausage in buckwheat, not ham and egg.
  • It is sold at markets and at football matches.
  • A packaged sausage may contain wheat. This recipe seasons plain pork so the wrap stays gluten-free.

Ingredients

  • 200 g buckwheat flour
  • 1 large egg
  • 450 ml cold water
  • 1/2 tsp fine salt, plus 1 tsp for the pork
  • 400 g ground pork
  • 1/2 tsp black pepper
  • 2 tbsp neutral oil, for cooking

Steps

  1. Whisk the buckwheat flour, egg, water, and 1/2 tsp salt. Rest 20 minutes.
  2. Mix the pork with 1 tsp salt and the pepper. Shape four thin sausages. Fry them in a spoon of oil until cooked through and browned. Set aside.
  3. Cook four thin galettes in a lightly oiled pan.
  4. Roll each hot sausage in a hot galette. Serve at once, with mustard if you want it.

Cook the pork until it is no longer pink in the center.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A shop pork sausage with no cereal filler
  • Cheese added, which is tasty and no longer dairy-free

Pairings and uses

  • Street food
  • Savory meal
  • Mustard, which adds a mustard allergen
  • Dry cider
  • Nothing else required
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

South Korea · No chili heat · 6 potato pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

South Korea · East Asia

Gamjajeon

A pan-fried potato cake, crisp at the lace and soft inside, with scallion and sesame.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 3 · 6 potato pancakes

History

Gamjajeon is Korea's potato pancake. Gamja means potato. Shredded potato is salted, squeezed, and fried, sometimes with a little potato starch so it holds. Wheat pancake mix is common in quick recipes. This one uses only potato and potato starch, so it stays gluten-free. It is a home side dish and a snack, plainer than seafood pajeon and sharper in texture than a mung-bean cake.

Cultural background

Potato starch or the starch that leaks from the potato itself is the binder. Rinsing every drop away makes the cake fall apart, so the method keeps that liquid. A soy dip is usual. This recipe uses gluten-free tamari so the dip does not smuggle wheat back in.

Why it tastes this way

Potato fries into a sweet, toasty lace where the shreds hit the oil, and the center stays steaming. Scallion adds a green bite. Sesame oil is the Korean pantry note that keeps it from tasting like a plain hash brown. There is no ferment and no chile in the cake. Salt and the dip do the seasoning.

How it is made

Shred potato, bind it with its own starch and a little potato starch, and pan-fry thin cakes.

Authentic touch. Squeeze the potato, then let the liquid sit and pour off the water, keeping the starch that settles. Stir that starch back in.

How it is used

Cut while hot and dip lightly. It is a side with rice or a snack. It softens as it cools.

Field notes

  • Gamja is potato. This is a potato cake, not a wheat jeon with a few cubes on top.
  • The dip uses gluten-free tamari so the plate matches the gluten-free tag.

Ingredients

  • 3 medium starchy potatoes, peeled
  • 1 tablespoon potato starch
  • 2 scallions, chopped
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Neutral oil
  • 1 teaspoon sesame oil
  • Gluten-free tamari and rice vinegar for dipping

Steps

  1. Shred the potatoes. Salt them and squeeze hard over a bowl. Let the liquid stand 5 minutes, pour off the water, and keep the starch at the bottom.
  2. Mix the shreds with that starch, the potato starch, and the scallions.
  3. Heat neutral oil with the sesame oil in a pan. Spread small thin rounds.
  4. Fry until both sides are golden and the centers are tender.
  5. Serve with a dip of gluten-free tamari and rice vinegar.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A little carrot shred for color
  • Wheat buchimgaru instead of potato starch, which adds wheat

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Snack
  • Gluten-free tamari and vinegar dip
  • Steamed rice
  • A cabbage salad
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Georgia · No chili heat · 6 thick cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Georgia · Caucasus

Georgian mchadi

Dense cornmeal cakes with a toasted crust and a mildly sweet grain center.

15 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 6 · 6 thick cakes

History

Mchadi are Georgian cornmeal cakes, a staple of western Georgia and a bread you also meet in Tbilisi. Maize is an American crop, so mchadi belongs to the centuries after corn reached the Caucasus, not to a pre-Columbian Georgian pantry. The cakes are often cooked in a pan, and some kitchens bake them. This recipe uses a skillet. They are served with sulguni cheese and with lobio, a bean stew. Neither of those is in the dough.

Cultural background

Cornmeal and salt are enough. The dough is stiff, shaped into thick rounds, and fried in a little oil until a crust forms. In Imereti, around Kutaisi, mchadi is ordinary food beside beans and cheese. It is not a sweet cornbread with sugar.

Why it tastes this way

Cornmeal tastes sweet and toasted when the crust browns, and plain when you hit the center. There is no wheat gluten to make it bready, so the crumb breaks rather than stretches. Oil in the pan adds richness. Salt is the seasoning. Sulguni on the side is salty and milky, which is why the plain cake works.

How it is made

Stir cornmeal, salt, and hot water into a dough you can press. Form thick cakes and pan-fry them in a little oil, covered, turning once, until the crust is brown and the center is no longer raw. Rest them a minute before you cut them.

Authentic touch. Shape a stiff corn dough and cook it in a pan, which is the method given here. Do not pour a wheat pancake batter. Cheese and beans are the company, not ingredients inside this gluten-free cake.

How it is used

Mchadi is an accompaniment to lobio and to sulguni, and it is a savory breakfast or supper. Sulguni is dairy and is not in the dough. The cake can also be eaten with nothing but salt.

Field notes

  • Mchadi are cornmeal cakes. This recipe cooks them in a skillet.
  • Some kitchens bake mchadi instead. That is a different pan.
  • One hundred percent cornmeal is gluten-free if the meal was not milled with wheat.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups cornmeal (about 280 g)
  • 3/4 teaspoon fine salt
  • 1 1/4 cups hot water
  • 2 tablespoons oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Stir the cornmeal and salt together. Pour in the hot water and mix to a stiff dough. It should not pour. There is no wheat in this recipe.
  2. Rest the dough for 10 minutes. Divide it into 6 pieces and press each into a cake about a finger thick.
  3. Heat the oil in a skillet over medium heat. Add the cakes. This method is the pan, not the oven.
  4. Cover and cook, turning once, until both sides have a brown crust and the center is cooked through, not chalky.
  5. Drain on a rack for a minute. Serve with beans or cheese if you want the usual plate. The cheese is dairy and is not in the dough.

Gluten-free only if the cornmeal is free of wheat. The pan oil is hot. Sulguni on the side is dairy.

Mchadi dry out quickly. Wrap them once cool and reheat in a covered pan. Refrigerate leftovers after a day in a cool kitchen, sooner if the room is warm. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • An oven batch, baked as thicker cakes, is the other common method and is not the one written here.
  • A spoon of oil in the dough makes a richer crumb.
  • Yellow or white cornmeal both work. A coarse grind makes a grainier cake.

Pairings and uses

  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Savory meal
  • Lobio, a bean stew
  • Sulguni cheese, which adds dairy
  • Pickles
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Mexico · Mild · 8 gorditas

Flying Pancakes of the World

Mexico · Mexico & Central America

Gorditas

Thick toasted masa, split and filled with mashed beans, fresh cheese, and a mild salsa.

30 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 gorditas

History

Gorditas are thick masa cakes, griddled until the outside is set, then split and filled. The corn dough is older than the habit of stuffing it, and corn cakes in Mexico are older than wheat hotcakes. A gordita is not a sope. A sope keeps a pinched rim and an open top. A gordita is fatter and is opened like a pocket after cooking. This recipe fills the pocket with beans and cheese. Chicharrón is a common filling in many towns and is not vegetarian.

Cultural background

Gorditas are antojitos, the category of small cooked snacks sold from comals and market stalls. León and many other cities have their own fillings. The masa base is the shared part.

Why it tastes this way

Because the cake is thick, the center stays steamy and corny while the outside dries and toasts. Nixtamalized masa tastes fuller than raw cornmeal. Salt in the dough, salt in the beans, and a mild chile salsa keep the plate savory. Cheese adds a milky salt note. The split lets the filling heat through against the hot masa.

How it is made

Mix masa harina with water and salt, rest it, and pat thick rounds. Griddle them dry, split, and fill with warm mashed beans, cheese, and salsa.

Authentic touch. Cook the cakes long enough that the crust is dry before you split them. A wet cake tears instead of opening into a pocket. Use a thin knife and turn the gordita as you cut.

How it is used

Eat them hot, standing or at the table, with the salsa on the filling rather than cooked into the dough. They are a meal or a heavy snack.

Field notes

  • The name means little fat ones, which describes the thickness, not a dessert.
  • Split the gordita after it cooks. Filling it raw is the method for a tlacoyo, a different shape.
  • Masa harina in this recipe is nixtamalized corn, not plain cornmeal from a baking aisle.

Ingredients

  • 250 g masa harina
  • 320 ml warm water
  • 1 teaspoon salt, divided
  • 300 g cooked beans, mashed and warmed with a spoon of their broth
  • 80 g crumbled fresh white cheese
  • 2 ripe tomatoes, 1 jalapeño, and a pinch of salt for a quick salsa

Steps

  1. Stir masa harina with 1/2 teaspoon salt and the warm water. Knead, cover, and rest 20 minutes. The dough should be soft and not cracked.
  2. Blend or finely chop the tomatoes and jalapeño with a pinch of salt for a mild salsa.
  3. Divide the dough into 8 pieces and pat each into a thick round, about 1 centimeter.
  4. Cook on a dry comal over medium heat for 4 to 5 minutes a side, until the outside is dry and spotted and the center is set.
  5. Split each cake along the edge with a knife, leaving one side attached. Fill with warm beans and cheese, then spoon on salsa.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Chicharrón and potatoes are a classic filling. That version contains meat and is not vegetarian.
  • Nopales and beans, with the cheese left out, can be made dairy-free and vegan.
  • Blue masa harina changes the color and not the shape.

Pairings and uses

  • Street food
  • Savory meal
  • Snack
  • A thin guajillo or tomato salsa
  • Shredded lettuce and onion, if you want crunch
  • Agua fresca or black coffee
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

South Korea · Mild · 2 seafood pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

South Korea · East Asia

Haemul pajeon

A crisp scallion pancake threaded with shrimp and squid, mild chile in the dip.

25 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 2 seafood pancakes

History

Haemul pajeon is the seafood scallion pancake. Haemul means seafood. Busan and other coastal tables pile shrimp, squid, and sometimes oyster or mussel into a wheat batter with scallion. This recipe uses shrimp and squid and tags shellfish. Squid is a mollusk. The allergen list here uses shellfish for it, and the ingredients name the squid plainly. It is a different pancake from the vegetable pajeon, even though the dip and the griddle are similar.

Cultural background

Coastal markets sell the seafood already cleaned. At home, pat it dry or the pancake steams. The heat of this version stays mild: gochugaru is in the dip, not a thick paste in the batter. Egg and wheat bind the cake.

Why it tastes this way

Shrimp and squid taste sweet and briny against the green scallion. Wheat and oil make the crust. A little rice flour keeps that crust from going limp under the moisture of the seafood. The dip's vinegar cuts the richness. Because the seafood is folded in, not laid on after, every wedge has some of it.

How it is made

Fold dried shrimp, squid, and scallion into a wheat batter and pan-fry until the center is cooked and the crust is brown.

Authentic touch. Dry the shrimp and squid well and cook the pancake longer than a vegetable one. Wet seafood in a thick batter leaves a raw middle.

How it is used

Cut into pieces and serve hot with the dip. It is a shared plate, not a dessert.

Field notes

  • Haemul pajeon is the seafood pancake. The vegetable scallion pancake is a separate page.
  • Squid is listed with shellfish in this allergen tag, and the recipe names it.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup all-purpose wheat flour
  • 2 tablespoons rice flour
  • 1 egg
  • 3/4 cup cold water
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 bunch scallions, cut in lengths
  • 150 grams shrimp, peeled and patted dry
  • 100 grams squid, cleaned, sliced, and patted dry
  • Neutral oil
  • Soy sauce, rice vinegar, and gochugaru for the dip

Steps

  1. Whisk both flours, egg, water, and salt. Fold in scallions, shrimp, and squid.
  2. Mix a dip of 2 tablespoons soy sauce, 1 tablespoon vinegar, and 1/2 teaspoon gochugaru.
  3. Heat a generous film of oil in a wide pan. Spread half the batter and nestle any protruding seafood into it.
  4. Cook on medium until the underside is brown and the shrimp and squid are opaque. Flip and cook through.
  5. Repeat with the second half. Rest a minute, then cut and serve.

Cook shrimp and squid until they are opaque and firm, not translucent.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A few oysters added with the shrimp, still shellfish
  • More gochugaru if you want medium heat

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Street food
  • Snack
  • Soy-vinegar dip
  • Sliced raw garlic
  • Steamed rice
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free
