Haiti · No chili heat · 16 fritters

Flying Pancakes of the World

Haiti · Caribbean

Haitian accras

Fried grated malanga, herb-heavy and crisp, with a soft center that has to be cooked through.

25 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 16 fritters

History

Haitian accras, often akra, are street fritters. This page is the malanga or taro version: a peeled root sold for cooking, grated, mixed with herbs and egg, and fried. Salt-cod accras are a different fritter, common on other Caribbean islands and also known in Haiti. They are not in this bowl. The recipe does not use cassava and does not teach cassava processing. Cook the fritters until the center is soft.

Cultural background

Vendors in Port-au-Prince fry accras and sell them with a spicy sauce on the side. The heat lives in that sauce more than in this batter. Scallion, garlic, and parsley are the seasoning inside the fritter. Leave the fish out and the recipe stays vegetarian.

Why it tastes this way

Malanga fries to a nutty, starchy crust. The inside stays moist and slightly earthy if it is cooked through, and it stays harsh and chalky if it is not. Garlic, scallion, and parsley perfume the batter without making it hot. Egg holds the shreds together. Oil browns the herbs at the edge, which is where a lot of the aroma comes from.

How it is made

Grate peeled malanga, mix with egg, scallion, garlic, parsley, and salt, and fry spoons of the mixture until the center is soft.

Authentic touch. Use peeled malanga or taro that was sold for cooking. Grate it, squeeze out only a little liquid, and fry until a broken fritter is soft in the center, not glassy or raw. A golden crust is not enough.

How it is used

Eat them hot as a street snack, with pikliz or a pepper sauce if you want heat. Those sauces are extra. The fritter on this page is not spicy.

Field notes

  • This accra is malanga or taro, not a cassava recipe and not a salt-cod fritter.
  • Cook the center through. Color on the outside is not a test of doneness.
  • With no fish in the bowl, the fritters stay vegetarian.

Ingredients

  • 400 g peeled malanga or taro, sold for cooking
  • 1 egg
  • 2 scallions, finely chopped
  • 1 garlic clove, minced
  • 2 tablespoons chopped parsley
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Neutral oil for frying

Steps

  1. Grate the peeled malanga. Squeeze lightly if it weeps a lot of liquid. Leave it moist.
  2. Mix in the egg, scallions, garlic, parsley, and salt until the shreds hold together.
  3. Heat 2 centimeters of oil over medium heat until a shred sizzles immediately.
  4. Fry spoons of the mixture, turning, until deep gold, about 4 minutes. Break one open. The center should be soft and cooked, not raw or glassy.
  5. Drain and serve hot.

Use peeled taro or malanga sold for cooking. Cook the fritters until the center is soft all the way through. This recipe does not use cassava and does not teach cassava processing.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A small hot chile in the batter raises the heat and is a common vendor version.
  • Salt cod mixed in is a different accra. It adds fish and is not vegetarian.
  • A spoon of wheat flour is a binder some cooks use. Those fritters contain gluten.

Pairings and uses

  • Street food
  • Snack
  • Savory meal
  • Pikliz, the cabbage pickle, on the side
  • A tomato sauce
  • Fried plantain
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Japan · No chili heat · 2 layered cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Japan · East Asia

Hiroshima okonomiyaki

A layered savory crepe: thin batter, a mound of cabbage, noodles, pork, and egg, finished with sauce.

25 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 2 · 2 layered cakes

History

Hiroshima okonomiyaki is built in layers on the griddle. A thin crepe is spread first. Cabbage goes on in a high pile, then pork and yakisoba noodles, and the stack is finished on a fried egg. The cabbage is not mixed into the batter the way it is in Osaka. Both cities call the result okonomiyaki. They are different dishes. This recipe writes the layers in order and keeps them separate from the Osaka page.

Cultural background

Hiroshima shops cook the stack on a wide teppan and flip it with large spatulas. At home a big pan works if you keep the portions small. The noodles are wheat. The egg is its own layer, not a binder stirred through cabbage.

Why it tastes this way

Each layer stays distinct. The crepe tastes toasted. The cabbage steams and turns sweet under the lid of the other ingredients. Yakisoba adds a seasoned, chewy wheat note. Pork salts the stack. The egg fries into a soft sheet that holds everything. Sauce on the outside is sweet and savory. You taste the sequence, not a single mixed batter.

How it is made

Layer a thin crepe, cabbage, pork, noodles, and a fried egg, flipping the stack so the egg becomes the base, then sauce it.

Authentic touch. Start with a truly thin crepe and a taller pile of cabbage than looks reasonable. The cabbage cooks down. If you mix it into the batter, you have left Hiroshima.

How it is used

Slide the whole stack onto a plate and cut it with a spatula. Eat it hot as a savory street meal.

Field notes

  • Hiroshima okonomiyaki is layered. Osaka mixes the cabbage into the batter.
  • The usual layers are crepe, cabbage, noodles, and egg, with pork in the pile.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup all-purpose wheat flour
  • 1/2 cup water
  • A pinch of salt
  • 300 grams shredded green cabbage
  • 80 grams thinly sliced pork
  • 1 portion cooked yakisoba wheat noodles, about 150 grams
  • 2 eggs
  • Neutral oil
  • Okonomiyaki sauce and aonori

Steps

  1. Whisk flour, water, and salt into a thin crepe batter.
  2. Oil a large pan. Spread a thin crepe of about half the batter. On it pile half the cabbage, then the pork.
  3. Cover and cook until the cabbage shrinks and the pork loses its raw color. Season the noodles and set them on the pile.
  4. In a clear spot of the pan, fry one egg as a flat sheet. Flip the whole stack onto the egg.
  5. Slide the cake onto a plate egg side down, spread sauce and aonori, and repeat for the second serving.

Cook the pork until it is done before you add the noodles and egg.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Bean sprouts with the cabbage
  • A second crepe on top if your pan runs dry

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Street food
  • Okonomiyaki sauce
  • Aonori
  • Pickled ginger
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Honduras · No chili heat · 6 baleadas

Flying Pancakes of the World

Honduras · Mexico & Central America

Honduran baleadas

A soft griddled wheat tortilla folded over refried beans and crumbled cheese.

35 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 6 · 6 baleadas

History

Baleadas are a north-coast Honduran meal, especially around La Ceiba and San Pedro Sula. A large wheat tortilla is griddled, spread with refried beans and cheese, and folded. The tortilla is the bread, cooked on a comal, not fried. Corn tortillas are older in the region than this wheat wrap, and older than wheat hotcakes. The baleada became the everyday breakfast and late-night plate of the coast. This recipe keeps it to beans and cheese.

Cultural background

Stands sell baleadas simples, just beans and cheese, and baleadas especiales with egg, avocado, or crema added. The simple one is the recipe here. It is breakfast, lunch, and a snack after a dance, depending on the hour.

Why it tastes this way

The wheat tortilla tastes soft and slightly toasted, with enough fat in the dough to bend without cracking. Refried red beans are salty and earthy. Fresh cheese crumbles rather than stretching, so the bite stays milky and dry against the beans. There is no chile in the written filling, which is why a plain baleada is gentle. Hot sauce is a choice at the table.

How it is made

Make a soft wheat dough with oil, rest it, and griddle large thin rounds. Mash beans with onion, season them, and fold each tortilla over beans and crumbled cheese.

Authentic touch. Griddle the tortilla until it puffs in spots and is still flexible. A crisp tortilla will not fold. Spread the beans while the bread is hot so the cheese softens. Fold once, like a taco, not a tight roll.

How it is used

Eat them hot, folded, for breakfast or a street meal. Two make a plate. Avocado on top is common and does not change the allergens.

Field notes

  • The tortilla for a baleada is griddled wheat bread, then folded. It is not a corn pupusa.
  • The simple street version is beans and cheese. Eggs and meat are additions.
  • Corn cakes in this region are older than the wheat tortilla used here.

Ingredients

  • 250 g wheat flour, plus more for rolling
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt, plus more for the beans
  • 2 tablespoons neutral oil
  • 120 ml warm water
  • 300 g cooked red beans
  • 1 small onion, chopped
  • 100 g crumbled fresh white cheese

Steps

  1. Mix the flour, 1/2 teaspoon salt, oil, and warm water into a soft dough. Cover and rest 20 minutes.
  2. Fry the onion in a spoon of oil, add the beans, and mash them with a little bean broth until spreadable. Salt to taste.
  3. Divide the dough into 6 pieces and roll thin rounds.
  4. Cook each round on a dry or lightly oiled comal until it blisters and browns in spots, under a minute a side. Keep them soft.
  5. Spread hot beans and cheese on one half and fold. Serve at once.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A baleada especial adds egg, avocado, and crema. The egg adds an allergen. The crema is more dairy.
  • A spoon of hot sauce raises the heat above none.
  • Lard in the tortilla dough adds meat and is not vegetarian. This dough uses oil.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Street food
  • Honduran sour cream, which is still dairy
  • Sliced avocado
  • A fried egg, which adds the egg allergen
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Hungary · Mild · 8 filled crepes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Hungary · Eastern Europe

Hortobágyi palacsinta

Hungarian crepes filled with paprika chicken, folded, and baked with sour cream.

30 min prep · 45 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 filled crepes

History

Hortobágyi palacsinta are crepes filled with a paprika meat stew, folded, and served with a paprika sauce and sour cream. The name points to the Hortobágy plain. Stories that credit a single inventor or a single fair are disputed, so this page does not name one. It should not be described as an ancient herdsman's recipe. It is a restaurant and home dish of filled paprika pancakes. The sweet jam palacsinta is the other page. Sweet paprika makes the heat mild, not a chile blast.

Cultural background

Paprika, onion, and meat are the core of the filling. Veal is traditional in many restaurant versions. This recipe uses chicken thigh, which is a common home substitute, and says so. The crepes are wheat, egg, and milk.

Why it tastes this way

Onion cooked in fat and sweet paprika tastes sweet and earthy, not burnt, if the paprika never fries dry. Chicken takes up that sauce. The crepe is mild. Sour cream cools the paprika and turns the sauce pink. The heat stays mild because this recipe uses sweet paprika, not hot.

How it is made

Chicken is simmered with onion and sweet paprika, chopped, and folded into thin crepes. A sauce of the cooking liquid and sour cream is poured over, and the dish is warmed.

Authentic touch. Take the pan off the heat before you stir in the paprika, so it does not scorch. Fold the crepes rather than rolling them tight. Sour cream goes on as a sauce. Do not tell the Hortobágy story as a settled invention.

How it is used

Hot, as a savory main course. It is a meat dish.

Field notes

  • The name points to the Hortobágy. A single inventor story is disputed.
  • It is a filled paprika crepe, not the sweet jam palacsinta.
  • This recipe uses chicken and says so. Many restaurant versions use veal.

Ingredients

  • 150 g plain wheat flour
  • 2 large eggs
  • 300 ml whole milk
  • 1 pinch fine salt
  • 40 g unsalted butter or lard, divided
  • 1 onion, finely chopped
  • 500 g boneless chicken thigh, cut into small pieces
  • 1 tbsp sweet paprika
  • 200 ml sour cream

Steps

  1. Make a thin crepe batter with the flour, eggs, milk, and salt. Cook eight crepes in a little of the fat and set them aside.
  2. Cook the onion in the remaining fat until soft. Add the chicken and a pinch of salt and cook until it loses its raw color. Take the pan off the heat, stir in the paprika, then add 150 ml water and simmer until the chicken is tender and the sauce is thick.
  3. Chop the chicken. Mix a few spoons of sour cream into the sauce. Fill and fold the crepes around the meat.
  4. Lay them in a dish, spoon over the remaining sauce and sour cream, and warm at 180°C for 10 minutes.

Cook the chicken until it is no longer pink. Scorched paprika tastes bitter, so do not fry the spice in a dry pan.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Veal instead of chicken
  • Hot paprika, which raises the heat above mild and should be labeled if you use it

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • The paprika sour-cream sauce
  • A cucumber salad
  • Plain rice, if you want a starch beside the crepe
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Ethiopia · No chili heat · About 8 large rounds

Flying Pancakes of the World

Ethiopia · East Africa

Injera

Sour, spongy teff bread with an earthy crumb and open holes on top.

25 min prep · 40 min cook · Serves 6 · About 8 large rounds

History

Teff, Eragrostis tef, was domesticated in the Horn of Africa, and injera is the fermented flatbread made from it. A teff batter is left to sour, then cooked on one side on a mitad, the clay griddle. The bread covers a platter and is torn to pinch stews. This recipe is 100 percent teff. Wheat blends used by some bakeries are a different loaf and are not in this method.

Cultural background

Injera is both the starch and the utensil at a shared Ethiopian meal. Guests tear pieces and use them to pick up wat. The sourness is expected. A fresh injera should bend and hold a stew without cracking into dust.

Why it tastes this way

Two to three days of fermentation feed lactic acid bacteria and wild yeast, so the bread tastes sour rather than sweet. Teff itself is earthy and slightly nutty. On the hot mitad the bubbles set into ayen, the eyes. The top stays soft because the bread is never flipped, and steam under a cloth finishes it so it can soak up sauce.

How it is made

Whisk teff flour and water into a batter like heavy cream and ferment it, covered, for 2 to 3 days until sour and bubbly. Salt it only after that rest. Pour it in a spiral on a hot mitad or a wide nonstick skillet, cover, and cook on one side until the ayen are open and the top is matte. Do not flip. Stack the rounds in a cloth so they steam and soften.

Authentic touch. Cook on one side only and leave the ayen open. Do not flip. If the eyes close under a second side of heat, the surface toughens and stops drinking the stew.

How it is used

Injera lines a platter under meats and vegetable stews, or under a fasting spread of lentils and greens. It is the accompaniment to the meal, not a side dish in a separate basket. Leftover bread is torn into firfir the next day.

Field notes

  • The holes on the surface are called ayen, the Amharic word for eyes.
  • A mitad is the round clay griddle used to cook injera on one side.
  • One hundred percent teff injera is gluten-free only if the flour was not milled with wheat.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups teff flour, ivory or brown (about 280 g)
  • 3 cups water, plus more for thinning
  • 1/2 teaspoon fine salt
  • A few drops of neutral oil, only if the pan sticks

Steps

  1. Whisk the teff flour and water into a smooth batter about as thick as heavy cream. There is no wheat in this recipe. Cover the bowl.
  2. Leave the batter at room temperature for 2 to 3 days, until it smells sour and looks bubbly. Stir it once a day. This is the ferment. Do not add baking powder.
  3. Stir in the salt only after the ferment. Add a spoon of water if the batter is thicker than pourable cream.
  4. Heat a mitad or a wide nonstick skillet over medium heat. Wipe it with a few drops of oil only if the surface sticks. Pour the batter in a spiral and tilt the pan into a large thin round.
  5. Cover and cook on one side until the top is set and the ayen, the eyes, are open. Do not flip. Move the bread to a clean cloth and let the steam soften it while you cook the rest.

This loaf is gluten-free only when the teff flour has not been milled or stored with wheat. A shared griddle can transfer wheat flour.

Wrap cooked injera in a clean cloth for the day, then refrigerate and reheat in a covered pan. Keep sour batter refrigerated if you are not cooking it the same day. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Ivory teff makes a paler injera with the same ferment.
  • A spoon of saved batter from a previous batch can start the next one.
  • Adding wheat flour is a bakery shortcut. It is not this recipe, and it removes the gluten-free diet tag.

Pairings and uses

  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Savory meal
  • Feast or fast day
  • Red lentil stew
  • Collard greens
  • Spiced chicken stew, for cooks who eat meat
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free

Ireland · No chili heat · 8 pan cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Ireland · Western Europe

Irish boxty

Pan boxty: raw grated potato and mashed potato, fried as cakes.

25 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 pan cakes

History

Boxty is an Irish potato cake. There are three forms: pan boxty, loaf boxty, and dumpling boxty. This page is only the pan cake. The batter mixes raw grated potato with mashed potato and flour, then the cakes are fried. Potatoes reached Irish kitchens and became a staple. The cake is one of the ways a raw potato was cooked without waiting for a roast. Flour means this recipe is not gluten-free.

Cultural background

Pan boxty is breakfast and supper food. It is associated with the north and the midlands as much as with any single county. This page does not assign it to one village.

Why it tastes this way

Raw potato brings starch and a clean flavor. Mash brings softness. Flour binds the two. Frying browns the starch. If you rinse all the starch off the grated potato, the cake falls apart and tastes watery. Salt is the seasoning. A crisp edge and a soft center is the point.

How it is made

Equal parts raw grated potato and mashed potato are mixed with flour, salt, and a little milk, then fried as flat cakes in butter or oil.

Authentic touch. Grate the raw potato and keep the liquid that settles into starch. Pour off the water and put the starch back. That paste helps the cake hold.

How it is used

Hot, with butter, eggs, or bacon. The cake on this page is vegetarian. Bacon is a serving choice, not part of the batter.

Field notes

  • Pan, loaf, and dumpling boxty are three methods. This is the pan cake.
  • The mix is raw grated potato plus mashed potato, not mash alone.
  • The starch from the grated potato helps bind the cake.

Ingredients

  • 250 g raw potatoes, peeled
  • 250 g cold mashed potato
  • 100 g plain wheat flour
  • 1 tsp fine salt
  • 60 ml milk
  • 30 g unsalted butter, for frying

Steps

  1. Grate the raw potatoes onto a clean cloth. Squeeze the liquid into a bowl. Let it sit so the starch settles, pour off the water, and keep the starch.
  2. Mix the squeezed potato, the starch, the mash, flour, salt, and enough milk to make a stiff batter.
  3. Heat the butter in a frying pan. Shape flat cakes and fry over medium heat until both sides are brown and the raw potato is cooked through, about 4 minutes a side.
  4. Rest them a minute and serve with butter or eggs.

The raw potato must cook through. Cut one cake open. The center should be soft and hot, not glassy.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Boxty loaf, baked as a bread, which is a different method
  • Boxty dumpling, boiled, which is also a different method

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Butter
  • Fried eggs
  • Bacon, if you want a meat breakfast
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Ireland · No chili heat · 8 farls

Flying Pancakes of the World

Ireland · Western Europe

Irish potato farls

Mashed-potato farls, cut in quarters and cooked on a griddle.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 farls

History

Potato farls are a mashed-potato bread cooked on a griddle and cut into quarters. Farl means a fourth. They are an Ulster breakfast bread eaten in Ireland, which is why this page is pinned to Ireland. The wheat soda farl, a different dough, is on the Ulster soda farl page under the United Kingdom. Potato farls use mash, flour, and fat. They do not use the raw potato that defines pan boxty.

Cultural background

They are weekend breakfast food beside eggs, bacon, or soda bread. The dough should be soft and the cakes thin enough to cook through on a dry griddle.

Why it tastes this way

Mashed potato makes a mild, stretchy crumb once it meets a hot griddle. Flour keeps the shape. Butter in the dough tastes nutty when the surface toasts. They are saltier and drier than a fried potato cake, because the pan is only lightly greased.

How it is made

Cold mash is mixed with butter, salt, and flour, pressed into rounds, quartered, and cooked on a hot griddle until spotted brown.

Authentic touch. Cut the round into four before you cook it. Do not grate in raw potato. If you do, you have started a boxty, and that recipe is the other page.

How it is used

At breakfast, with butter, eggs, or a fry. The farls themselves are vegetarian.

Field notes

  • A farl is a quarter of the round.
  • This cake is mashed potato. Boxty also uses raw potato.
  • The pin is Ireland. Ulster soda farls are the wheat cake on the United Kingdom page.

Ingredients

  • 500 g cold mashed potato
  • 40 g unsalted butter
  • 1 tsp fine salt
  • 100 g plain wheat flour, plus more for pressing

Steps

  1. Beat the mash with the butter and salt. Work in the flour until you have a soft dough that does not stick.
  2. Divide in two. Press each half into a round about 1 cm thick on a floured board and cut into quarters.
  3. Cook on a hot dry griddle until brown spots show, then turn and cook the other side.
  4. Serve warm with butter.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of chopped spring onion
  • Tattie scones, the Scottish thin triangle of the same family

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Butter
  • Eggs
  • Bacon or sausage on a meat plate
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Jamaica · No chili heat · 4 bammies

Flying Pancakes of the World

Jamaica · Caribbean

Jamaican bammy

A dense, mild cassava round, dry and toasted on the outside, then fried so the edge crisps.

15 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 bammies

History

Bammy is a Jamaican cassava flatbread, long associated with St. Elizabeth parish and with roadside plates of fried fish. Cassava bread in the Caribbean is older than the wheat johnny cakes that came later. The root itself has to be processed before it is safe to eat. This recipe starts from commercial cassava flour, which is already processed, or you may pan-fry a packaged bammy. It does not teach you to peel, grate, and press raw cassava at home.

Cultural background

Cooks griddle the dough into thick rounds, and many then dip the cake in salted water or coconut milk and fry it to serve. The plain griddled round is the bread. The fry is the usual plate next to fish. This version leaves the fish off so the recipe stays vegan.

Why it tastes this way

Commercial cassava flour tastes clean, starchy, and slightly sweet, without the toasted corn of a tortilla or the gluten of wheat. Salt is almost the only seasoning in the dough. Frying after a brief soak gives a soft center and a crisp rim. If you stop after the dry griddle, the bammy is firmer and better for soaking up sauce.

How it is made

Mix commercial cassava flour with salt and water into a stiff dough. Flatten thick rounds, griddle them dry, dip briefly in salted water, and pan-fry.

Authentic touch. Press the rounds firmly so they do not crack at the edge, and cook them on a dry skillet until the surface looks dry before you even think about the fry. A wet center will not finish in the oil.

How it is used

Serve hot beside fried fish, callaloo, or a stew. The bammy is the bread on the plate. Packaged bammy can be dipped and fried the same way if you are not making the dough.

Field notes

  • Raw bitter cassava can be poisonous. This page uses commercial cassava flour and does not teach home processing.
  • Bammy is often fried after the first cook, which is why it shows up next to fried fish.
  • There is no wheat in this dough, so it is gluten-free if the flour was not milled with wheat.

Ingredients

  • 280 g commercial cassava flour
  • 1 teaspoon salt, plus more for the dipping water
  • 200 ml water
  • 250 ml water for a brief dip
  • Neutral oil for pan-frying

Steps

  1. Stir the commercial cassava flour, 1 teaspoon salt, and 200 ml water into a stiff dough. If it cracks, add a spoon of water.
  2. Divide into 4 pieces and press each into a round about 1 centimeter thick.
  3. Cook on a dry skillet over medium heat for 6 to 8 minutes a side, until the surface is dry and spotted.
  4. Dip each round for a few seconds in salted water, then drain.
  5. Pan-fry in a thin layer of oil until the outside is crisp. Serve hot.

Raw bitter cassava can be poisonous. This recipe uses commercial cassava flour, or a packaged bammy, and does not teach home processing of raw cassava.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Soak the griddled round in coconut milk instead of salted water before frying.
  • Eat it after the dry griddle, without the fry, if you want a firmer bread.
  • A packaged bammy, already cooked and sold in rounds, can be dipped and fried. Read the package if you need to confirm it is only cassava.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Escovitch or fried fish, a plate that is not vegan
  • Callaloo and salt fish, also not vegan
  • A pepper sauce on the side for people who want heat
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Armenia · No chili heat · 4 stuffed flatbreads

Flying Pancakes of the World

Armenia · Caucasus

Jingalov hats

A thin griddled flatbread packed with a large handful of salted herbs and greens.

40 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 stuffed flatbreads

History

Jingalov hats is an herb-stuffed flatbread associated with Artsakh and with Armenian kitchens, including those in Syunik. The pin is Goris. The filling is a pile of finely chopped greens and herbs, lightly salted, sealed in a thin dough and cooked on a griddle. It is a flatbread, not a batter pancake. Cooks use a mix of what is in season. The point is the quantity of greens, not a single spice blend.

Cultural background

The bread is everyday food where the herbs grow, and it is also a bread people make to show what the place tastes like. A thin filling is considered a missed loaf. The dough is only the envelope. No cheese is required, and this recipe does not add any.

Why it tastes this way

A large mass of parsley, cilantro, dill, scallion, and chard tastes green, slightly bitter, and fresh, because the griddle wilts the herbs inside the dough instead of frying them hard. Salt is the seasoning. The toasted wheat wrapper is mild, so the herbs stay in front. Different seasonal leaves change the bitterness from batch to batch.

How it is made

Rest a soft dough. Finely chop a mix of tender herbs and greens, salt them, and squeeze out any heavy liquid. Fill a thin oval of dough, seal it, and cook it on a dry griddle, pressing so the center heats through.

Authentic touch. Chop a large volume of greens, salt them, and taste the filling before it goes into the dough. If it tastes watery or shy, add more herbs, not more flour. This is an herb-stuffed flatbread.

How it is used

Jingalov hats is a savory snack or a light meal, eaten hot, sometimes with tea or a simple salad. It does not need a sauce. Pickles on the side are a home choice and are not in the dough.

Field notes

  • Jingalov hats is an herb-stuffed flatbread, associated with Artsakh and with Armenian home cooking.
  • The filling should be a large amount of greens, not a sprinkle of herbs.
  • The pin is Goris, in Syunik. The bread is eaten well beyond that town.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups wheat flour (about 250 g)
  • 3/4 teaspoon fine salt, divided
  • 3/4 cup water
  • 1 cup finely chopped parsley
  • 1 cup finely chopped cilantro
  • 1/2 cup finely chopped dill
  • 1 cup finely chopped scallions
  • 2 cups finely chopped chard or spinach
  • 1 tablespoon oil

Steps

  1. Mix the flour, half the salt, and the water into a soft dough. Rest it for 15 minutes.
  2. Combine the parsley, cilantro, dill, scallions, and chard with the remaining salt and the oil. You want a generous pile. Squeeze out excess liquid.
  3. Divide the dough into 4 and roll each piece into a thin oval. Spread a thick layer of herbs over it, leaving a border.
  4. Fold and seal the dough around the herbs, then flatten gently so the packet is even. This is a stuffed flatbread.
  5. Cook on a dry griddle over medium heat, turning and pressing, until both sides are spotted and the dough is cooked. The herbs should steam inside, not burn.

The dough contains wheat. Taste a pinch of the salted herbs before cooking so the filling is not overly salty. Wild herbs you cannot identify do not belong in the bowl.

Eat the bread the day it is cooked. Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry pan. The greens darken as they sit. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Sorrel or other seasonal greens can join the mix when you have them.
  • A few walnut pieces appear in some fillings. They would add a tree nut and are not in this recipe.
  • A slightly thicker dough is easier and less like the thin market bread.

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Snack
  • Tea
  • A tomato and cucumber salad
  • Pickled vegetables
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

India · Mild · 8 jowar dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Jowar dosa

A pale sorghum dosa, mildly sweet and peppery, with a dry crisp edge.

20 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 jowar dosas

History

Jowar dosa is a sorghum griddle cake from the dry-grain belts of Karnataka and Maharashtra, where jowar is a daily flour. This recipe ferments jowar flour with a spoon of rice flour and no wheat, so it can be tagged gluten-free. Sorghum rotis are the unleavened cousin. The dosa is the watered, soured, griddled one. The page does not pin the habit to a century.

Cultural background

Hubballi sits in a sorghum-growing region, and the flour is ordinary pantry stock there. A jowar dosa is a breakfast use of that flour, not a novelty millet. Cooks who skip the ferment and add yogurt are making the instant kind. This bowl is the fermented one.

Why it tastes this way

Sorghum tastes mildly sweet and a little grassy, less bitter than finger millet. Fermentation brings a gentle sour note that keeps the sweetness from tasting raw. Rice flour helps the fragile sorghum starch hold together on the pan. Cumin and green chile are surface seasoning, so the grain still leads.

How it is made

Ferment a sorghum and rice-flour batter, then cook it in small thin rounds with cumin and chile.

Authentic touch. Spread gently. Jowar batter tears if you press the ladle the way you would on a glossy urad batter. A nonstick or very well-seasoned pan is the practical tool.

How it is used

Eat it hot with coconut chutney or a dry peanut chutney. Peanut chutney would add a peanut allergen, so the written cake stays nut-free.

Field notes

  • Jowar is sorghum, not millet and not wheat.
  • This recipe stays gluten-free by using sorghum and rice flour only.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup jowar flour (sorghum), with no wheat added
  • 2 tablespoons rice flour, with no wheat added
  • 1 1/2 cups water
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1/2 teaspoon cumin seeds
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • Neutral oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Whisk the flours, salt, and water into a lump-free batter.
  2. Ferment covered 8 to 12 hours until slightly sour. Count only the mixing as active prep. Discard batter that spoils.
  3. Stir in cumin and green chile. Loosen with water if needed.
  4. Oil a medium-hot pan. Spread a small round with a light hand.
  5. Cook until the underside is spotted and the edge is dry. Flip for a short minute. Repeat, oiling the pan each time.

Discard fermented batter that smells rotten or shows mold.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Chopped spinach stirred into the batter
  • Black pepper instead of green chile

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Coconut chutney
  • A sprinkle of chopped onion
  • Sambar
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Germany · No chili heat · 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Germany · Western Europe

Kartoffelpuffer

Shredded German potato pancakes, crisp, with applesauce.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 pancakes

History

Kartoffelpuffer are German shredded-potato pancakes, fried until crisp. In the Rhineland the same family is often called Reibekuchen. They are street and kitchen food, served with applesauce or with a savory garnish. This recipe binds the potato with egg and potato starch, not wheat flour, and fries it in oil, so it is gluten-free and dairy-free. Flour is a common substitute and would add a wheat allergen. The cake is vegetarian.

Cultural background

Christmas markets and home kitchens both sell them. Applesauce is the sweet side that people expect. A savory plate with herbs or smoked fish is the other direction. This page writes the applesauce plate.

Why it tastes this way

Raw potato shreds brown and crisp because the starch fries in oil. Onion, if you add it, sweetens the edge. Salt matters. Applesauce is cold, soft, and tart against a hot crust. Egg binds without making it an omelet. Squeeze out some water and keep enough starch or the cake will not hold.

How it is made

Potatoes and onion are grated, squeezed, mixed with egg, potato starch, and salt, and fried in shallow oil until crisp.

Authentic touch. Grate just before frying. Potato starch in this recipe keeps it free of wheat. If you use wheat flour instead, drop the gluten-free label. Serve applesauce on the side, not in the batter.

How it is used

Hot, with applesauce, as a snack or a light meal. Sour cream is a dairy addition and would change the diet tags.

Field notes

  • Reibekuchen is the Rhineland name in this family.
  • Applesauce is the usual sweet side.
  • This version uses potato starch, not wheat flour.

Ingredients

  • 700 g starchy potatoes, peeled
  • 1 small onion
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tbsp potato starch
  • 1 tsp fine salt
  • Neutral oil, for frying
  • Applesauce, for serving

Steps

  1. Grate the potatoes and onion. Squeeze them in a cloth, leaving them damp rather than bone-dry. Stir in the egg, potato starch, and salt.
  2. Heat a shallow layer of oil in a frying pan over medium-high heat.
  3. Fry flattened rounds until deep golden and crisp, turning once. The potato must be cooked through.
  4. Drain on a rack and serve with applesauce.

Do not leave the oil unattended. The center of a thick cake should not taste raw.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Reibekuchen, the Rhineland name for the same kind of cake
  • Wheat flour instead of potato starch, which is no longer gluten-free

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Snack
  • Street food
  • Applesauce
  • Salt
  • Chives, for a savory plate
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Kenya · No chili heat · 8 flatbreads

Flying Pancakes of the World

Kenya · East Africa

Kenyan chapati

Soft, flaky wheat flatbread with a lightly oiled, toasted surface.

30 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 flatbreads

History

Kenyan chapati is a griddled flatbread, not a batter pancake. It came through the Indian Ocean with South Asian sailors, traders, and families, and it became a daily bread in Kenya, Uganda, and Tanzania. The East African loaf is usually brushed with oil and rolled so the layers flake, then cooked on a dry pan. It is closer to a soft paratha than to a puffed dry phulka, and it has its own place at this table.

Cultural background

Chapati is weekend food and ordinary supper food. A stack disappears with beans, sukuma wiki, or a stew. The dough is mixed, rested, rolled, oiled, and rolled again. None of that is a bowl of batter. Homes in Kampala and Dar es Salaam cook the same bread.

Why it tastes this way

Wheat and salt are the flavor, with oil toasted between thin layers. The griddle adds brown spots that taste slightly smoky. Because fat is rolled in, the bread stays soft and flaky instead of crisp like a cracker. It does not taste sour. There is no ferment and no egg.

How it is made

Mix flour, salt, water, and a little oil into a soft dough and rest it. Roll each piece out, brush it with oil, coil it, and roll it again. Cook on a hot dry griddle, turning, until both sides blister and spot. Keep the stack covered so it steams soft.

Authentic touch. Treat it as a griddled flatbread. Roll, oil, and coil the dough so it flakes. Pouring a batter will not make Kenyan chapati. Cook it dry, with only the oil already in the dough.

How it is used

Chapati is an accompaniment to beans, sukuma wiki, and stews, and it is also breakfast with tea. It is a savory bread. It is not a dessert and not a pancake rolled around sugar.

Field notes

  • Kenyan chapati is a griddled flatbread, not a batter pancake.
  • The same bread is everyday food in Uganda and Tanzania.
  • Oil rolled into the dough is what makes the layers flake.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups wheat flour (about 250 g)
  • 3/4 teaspoon fine salt
  • 3/4 cup warm water
  • 3 tablespoons neutral oil, plus more for brushing

Steps

  1. Mix the flour, salt, warm water, and a spoon of oil into a soft dough. Knead until smooth. Cover and rest for 20 minutes.
  2. Divide the dough into 8 pieces. Roll one piece into a thin round.
  3. Brush the round with oil, roll it into a rope, and coil the rope into a disk. Roll the disk out again. This is a flatbread, not a batter.
  4. Cook on a hot dry griddle, turning several times, until both sides are blistered and spotted. Press the edges with a spatula so they cook through.
  5. Stack the chapatis under a cloth while you cook the rest.

The dough contains wheat. The griddle is dry but very hot. Oil in the layers can smoke if the pan is past medium-high.

Wrap leftover chapati so the surface does not dry. Refrigerate and reheat on a dry skillet. They stiffen in the refrigerator. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • More oil and an extra coil make a flakier bread.
  • Whole-wheat flour makes a denser chapati and still contains wheat.
  • A stuffed chapati is a different, thicker bread and not this plain one.

Pairings and uses

  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Savory meal
  • Breakfast
  • Stewed beans
  • Sukuma wiki, braised greens
  • Tea
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

South Korea · Medium · 2 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

South Korea · East Asia

Kimchi jeon

A tangy, medium-hot pancake of ripe kimchi in a wheat batter, crisp at the edge.

15 min prep · 18 min cook · Serves 4 · 2 pancakes

History

Kimchi jeon is a pancake made to use ripe kimchi, the fermented napa cabbage that is a daily food in Korea. Sour kimchi is chopped and folded into wheat flour, often with onion and an egg, then pan-fried. This recipe uses kimchi seasoned with fish sauce and salted shrimp, which is a common jar, so the tags include fish and shellfish. It is not a vegetarian pancake unless the kimchi itself is free of seafood. The heat is medium from the gochugaru already in the cabbage.

Cultural background

Older, sour kimchi is preferred. Fresh sweet kimchi makes a duller cake. The pancake is a home way to finish a jar, and it is also sold as a snack. Scallion pajeon does not taste like this, because the sour cabbage is the point.

Why it tastes this way

Fermented cabbage brings acid, garlic, and chile that a raw scallion cannot. The longer the kimchi has aged, the sharper the pancake. Wheat batter tames that sharpness and turns the edges brown. Onion adds sweetness. Fish sauce and salted shrimp in the cabbage deepen the savor. The result is tangy and medium-hot, not a mild scallion cake.

How it is made

Chop sour kimchi, fold it into a wheat and egg batter with some of its juice, and pan-fry both sides.

Authentic touch. Squeeze the kimchi lightly so the batter is not flooded, but do not rinse it. The juice is the seasoning. Use that juice in the batter.

How it is used

Cut into squares and eat hot, with a small dish of vinegar and soy if you want more acid. Rice makes it a meal.

Field notes

  • Kimchi is fermented napa cabbage. Ripe, sour kimchi makes the better pancake.
  • Many kimchi recipes use fish sauce or salted shrimp. This one does, so it is not vegetarian.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup chopped ripe kimchi made with napa cabbage, garlic, ginger, gochugaru, fish sauce, and salted shrimp
  • 2 tablespoons kimchi juice
  • 1/2 small onion, sliced
  • 3/4 cup all-purpose wheat flour
  • 1 egg
  • 1/4 cup water
  • A pinch of salt only if the kimchi is mild
  • Neutral oil

Steps

  1. Chop the kimchi into small pieces. Save 2 tablespoons of the juice.
  2. Stir flour, egg, water, and kimchi juice into a thick batter. Fold in kimchi and onion.
  3. Heat oil in a pan over medium heat. Spread half the batter into a round.
  4. Cook until the underside is browned and crisp. Flip and cook until the center is set and the kimchi is hot.
  5. Repeat. Slice and serve.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of gochujang in the dip
  • Pork in the batter, which is a known heartier version and still meat

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Snack
  • Steamed rice
  • A vinegar-soy dip
  • Sliced cucumber
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Sweden · No chili heat · 4 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Sweden · Northern Europe

Kolbullar

A simple pork and flour pancake from the charcoal woods of central Sweden.

10 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 pancakes

History

Kolbullar are the portable pancakes of charcoal makers and forest workers in central Sweden, including Dalarna and the old mining districts. Kol refers to charcoal. Pork, flour, water, and salt were cooked on a flat iron by the charcoal stack. This is a skillet version of that food. It is a meat dish. It is not a sweet pancake and not raggmunk.

Cultural background

The cake was working food: cheap grain, salt pork, and a fire that was already burning. At home it is still a plain, salty pancake. The romance of the woods is optional. The pork and the flour are not.

Why it tastes this way

Pork fat is the fat and the salt. The flour batter fries crisp at the rim and stays chewy. There is no egg and no sugar, so it tastes blunt and savory. Pepper helps. If you expect a dessert pancake, this will seem austere, which is accurate.

How it is made

Salt pork is diced and rendered. A batter of flour, water, and salt is poured into the fat, with the pork left in, and fried until set.

Authentic touch. Render the pork in the pan and fry the batter in that fat. The home pan stands in for the iron sheet by the charcoal stack. Do not add sugar.

How it is used

Hot, as a savory snack or a simple meal. It is not dessert.

Field notes

  • The name is tied to charcoal makers, kolare, who cooked them in the woods.
  • The batter is flour, water, salt, and pork. There is no sugar in the traditional cake.
  • This skillet version stands in for a flat iron over a charcoal fire.

Ingredients

  • 200 g salt pork, diced small
  • 150 g plain wheat flour
  • 300 ml cold water
  • 1/2 tsp fine salt
  • 1/4 tsp black pepper

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, water, salt, and pepper into a smooth batter.
  2. Fry the salt pork in a pan until the fat renders and the pieces brown.
  3. Pour in enough batter to make a thick pancake around the pork. Cook until the bottom is brown and the top is set.
  4. Turn carefully and finish the other side. Repeat with the remaining batter. Serve hot.

Salt pork is already salty. Taste the batter before you add the full amount of salt.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Bacon instead of salt pork
  • An egg in the batter, which changes the texture and adds an egg allergen

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Snack
  • Lingonberry jam, a later table addition
  • Black coffee
  • A crisp pickle
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Iran · No chili heat · 1 round cake, about 8 squares

Flying Pancakes of the World

Iran · Middle East

Kuku sabzi

A dense herb frittata, bright green inside, with walnut and a sour note from barberries.

25 min prep · 35 min cook · Serves 6 · 1 round cake, about 8 squares

History

Kuku sabzi is an Iranian herb and egg cake served at Nowruz, the new year, and at other tables through the year. It is a frittata, not a pancake. Eggs bind a large quantity of parsley, cilantro, and dill, with a spoon of flour for structure, and the cake is cooked slowly until the center sets. Walnuts and dried barberries, zereshk, are the usual scatter inside. It is not one of the symbolic haft-sin items. It is a dish on the Nowruz table.

Cultural background

The herbs are the body of the dish. A kuku that is mostly egg and a pinch of green is a different, thinner omelette. In Tehran and in other Iranian cities it is cut into squares for guests. The same cake appears at breakfast the next day, eaten cold or warm.

Why it tastes this way

Parsley, cilantro, and dill in this amount taste grassy, citrusy, and a little bitter, and egg makes that mass rich instead of like a salad. Turmeric colors the egg gold at the edges. Walnuts add fat and a tannic bite. Barberries are sour and small, so they pop against the herbs. Because it is a frittata, the texture is set and sliceable, not folded like a crepe.

How it is made

Chop a large bunch of herbs finely, squeeze them dry, and stir them into beaten eggs with turmeric, salt, a spoon of flour, chopped walnuts, and barberries. Cook the cake slowly in an oiled skillet, covered, until the center is set. Flip it or finish it under heat if the top is still wet.

Authentic touch. Call it a frittata, not a pancake. Use enough herbs that the batter looks green before it cooks. Walnuts mean this cake is not nut-free. If you cannot find barberries, leave them out rather than substituting a sweet dried fruit.

How it is used

Kuku sabzi is a Nowruz dish, a breakfast, and a savory plate with flatbread, yogurt, and herbs. The yogurt is extra dairy. The cake itself already has egg, wheat, and tree nut. It is feast food when the new year is the occasion, and ordinary food the rest of the year.

Field notes

  • Kuku sabzi is a frittata, an egg cake, not a pancake.
  • It is served at Nowruz, the Iranian new year, and it is not one of the haft-sin symbols.
  • Walnuts in this recipe are a tree nut, so the cake is not nut-free.

Ingredients

  • 4 eggs
  • 2 cups finely chopped parsley
  • 1 1/2 cups finely chopped cilantro
  • 1 cup finely chopped dill
  • 4 scallions, finely chopped
  • 1 tablespoon wheat flour
  • 1/2 teaspoon ground turmeric
  • 3/4 teaspoon fine salt
  • 1/3 cup chopped walnuts
  • 2 tablespoons dried barberries, rinsed
  • 2 tablespoons oil

Steps

  1. Chop the herbs finely and squeeze out excess moisture. Beat the eggs in a bowl.
  2. Stir the herbs, scallions, flour, turmeric, salt, walnuts, and barberries into the eggs. The mixture should look green, not pale. The walnuts are a tree nut.
  3. Heat the oil in a skillet over medium-low heat. Pour in the herb mixture and smooth the top. This is a frittata, not a pancake batter.
  4. Cover and cook slowly until the sides are set and the center no longer jiggles like liquid, about 20 minutes.
  5. Flip the cake onto a plate and slide it back to cook the second side for a few minutes, or set the top under a hot broiler. Cool slightly, then cut.

Cook the egg until the center is set. Walnuts are a tree nut. The spoon of flour is wheat. Barberries are sour berries, not a nut.

Refrigerate kuku the same day and eat it within a couple of days, cold or reheated. Do not leave the egg cake out. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Without walnuts the cake drops the tree-nut allergen. This written recipe includes them.
  • A pinch of fenugreek leaf is common and makes the aroma stronger.
  • Baking the pan in a moderate oven, after a start on the stove, sets the center evenly.

Pairings and uses

  • Feast or fast day
  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Flatbread
  • Plain yogurt, which adds dairy
  • More fresh herbs
  • Vegetarian

Open the journal page

India · Mild · 18 small cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Kuzhi paniyaram

Small fermented rice-and-urad balls, crisp outside, soft inside, with mustard seed and curry leaf.

25 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 18 small cakes

History

Kuzhi paniyaram is fermented rice and urad batter cooked in a dimpled pan, a Tamil Nadu breakfast and tiffin. Kuzhi means the pit or hollow of the pan. The batter is the idli-dosa ferment. The pan, not a new grain, makes the shape. Savory versions take a tempering of mustard seed and curry leaf. Sweet versions use jaggery. This page is the savory one.

Cultural background

Leftover idli batter is the honest weeknight source. The dimples fry the outside while the center steams. A flat griddle would simply make dosa. Telugu and Kannada kitchens have cousin cakes in similar pans. This recipe stays with the Tamil savory paniyaram.

Why it tastes this way

The ferment is mildly sour and bready because the ball is thick. Mustard seed and curry leaf, fried and then folded in, perfume the batter before it ever hits the pan. Oil in the dimples browns a crust that a steamed idli never gets. The contrast, crisp shell and soft sour crumb, is the reason to use the special pan.

How it is made

Fold a mustard and curry-leaf tempering into fermented rice and urad batter, then fry spoonfuls in a heated dimpled pan.

Authentic touch. Heat the empty pan before you oil the pits. Cold oil in a cold mold sticks and pulls the cakes apart.

How it is used

Unmold them when the bottoms are deep gold. Eat with coconut chutney while the crust still crackles.

Field notes

  • The batter is the fermented rice and urad batter. The dimpled pan is the change.
  • Kuzhi refers to the hollows the batter is poured into.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups fermented rice and urad idli or dosa batter
  • 1 teaspoon mustard seeds
  • 10 curry leaves
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • 1 tablespoon neutral oil, plus more for the pan
  • A pinch of salt if needed

Steps

  1. Use batter that has already fermented and smells pleasantly sour. Stir it down.
  2. Heat a tablespoon of oil, pop the mustard seeds, and add curry leaves and green chile for a few seconds.
  3. Fold that tempering into the batter.
  4. Heat a kuzhi paniyaram pan until hot. Add a few drops of oil to each dip. Fill the dips about three-quarters full.
  5. Cover and cook, turning the cakes once with a skewer, until both sides are brown and the centers are set. Drain briefly and serve.

Discard fermented batter that is moldy or smells putrid. Turn the cakes so the centers cook through.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Chopped onion in the batter
  • A sweet batch with jaggery instead of the tempering

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Snack
  • Savory meal
  • Coconut chutney
  • Sambar
  • Milagai podi
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United Kingdom · No chili heat · 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

Lancashire potato cakes

Thick mashed-potato cakes, fried, from Lancashire breakfasts.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 cakes

History

Lancashire potato cakes are mashed potato bound with flour and fried. They are a breakfast cake, often made when there is mash left from the day before. They are not Irish boxty, which uses raw grated potato as well as mash, and they are not Scottish tattie scones, which are rolled thin and cooked on a dry girdle. This version is a thicker round, fried in butter.

Cultural background

Potato cakes of this sort belong to the industrial breakfast as much as to a farmhouse. They use a staple that stores. The flavor depends on the potato, so a watery mash makes a weak cake.

Why it tastes this way

Flour and a little butter keep mashed potato from falling apart, and frying browns the outside. The inside stays soft and salty. Pepper is the usual seasoning. There is no egg in this recipe, so the cake tastes of potato rather than of omelet.

How it is made

Cold mashed potato is worked with flour, butter, and salt, shaped into thick rounds, and fried in butter until crusted.

Authentic touch. Dry the mash. A cake that weeps in the pan was too wet. Flour is the binder here, which means the cake is not gluten-free.

How it is used

At breakfast, with eggs or bacon, or with butter and salt alone. Bacon makes the plate a meat meal.

Field notes

  • These use mashed potato only. Boxty also uses raw grated potato.
  • Leftover mash is the usual reason to make them.
  • They are fried rounds, not the thin triangles of a tattie scone.

Ingredients

  • 500 g cold mashed potato, made without much liquid
  • 80 g plain wheat flour, plus more for shaping
  • 30 g unsalted butter, melted, plus more for frying
  • 1/2 tsp fine salt
  • 1/4 tsp black pepper

Steps

  1. Mix the mash, flour, melted butter, salt, and pepper into a soft dough. Add a spoon more flour if it sticks.
  2. Shape eight thick rounds on a floured board.
  3. Fry in butter over medium heat until both sides are brown and the center is hot.
  4. Serve at once with pepper, eggs, or bacon.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of chopped chives in the mash
  • Scottish tattie scones, which are thinner and cooked more dry

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Fried eggs
  • Bacon
  • Butter and black pepper
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Latvia · No chili heat · 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Latvia · Eastern Europe

Latvian potato pancakes

Latvian pancakes of half mashed and half raw potato, with dill, fried in butter.

25 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 pancakes

History

Latvian potato pancakes, kartupeļu pankūkas, are grated potato cakes served with sour cream. This page uses half raw grated potato and half mashed potato, with dill, so the texture is softer than an all-raw draniki and different from the Lithuanian kefir batter. Flour binds them. They are home food, not a copy of the Belarusian recipe. Butter in the pan means the cakes contain dairy.

Cultural background

Potato is a staple, and pancakes are a supper that uses it up. Dill is the herb. Sour cream is the sauce. A meat gravy is a possible side and is not required.

Why it tastes this way

Mashed potato makes the center soft. Raw potato still crisps at the edge and supplies starch. Dill tastes green and slightly sharp. Butter browns and tastes sweeter than a neutral oil. They are less brittle than a fully raw pancake and more herb-scented than the Lithuanian page.

How it is made

Raw grated potato and cold mash are mixed with egg, flour, dill, and salt, then fried in butter.

Authentic touch. Keep the half-and-half potato mix. All raw potato is a different cake. Dill goes into the batter, not only on the plate as a garnish you might forget.

How it is used

Hot, with sour cream, as a savory meal.

Field notes

  • This Latvian recipe mixes raw grated potato with mashed potato.
  • Dill is in the batter.
  • The Lithuanian page uses kefir and all raw potato. The Belarusian page uses no wheat flour.

Ingredients

  • 300 g raw potatoes, peeled
  • 300 g cold mashed potato
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tbsp plain wheat flour
  • 2 tbsp chopped fresh dill
  • 1 tsp fine salt
  • 30 g unsalted butter, for frying
  • Sour cream, for serving

Steps

  1. Grate the raw potatoes and squeeze them lightly. Mix with the mash, egg, flour, dill, and salt.
  2. Fry flattened cakes in butter over medium heat until both sides are brown and the raw potato is cooked.
  3. Keep the heat moderate so the butter browns instead of burning.
  4. Serve with sour cream.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • All raw potato, closer to draniki
  • Bacon fat for frying, which adds meat and removes the vegetarian diet

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Sour cream
  • More dill
  • A beet salad
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Lithuania · No chili heat · 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Lithuania · Eastern Europe

Lithuanian bulviniai blynai

Lithuanian potato pancakes with kefir in the batter, fried and served with sour cream.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 pancakes

History

Bulviniai blynai means potato pancakes in Lithuanian. They are grated potato cakes, a different dish from the Ashkenazi farmer-cheese latke pinned to Lithuania on that page. Kefir in the batter is the method here. A little wheat flour binds them, so they are not gluten-free. They are everyday food, served with sour cream or with bacon on a meat plate. Bacon is not in this recipe.

Cultural background

Potato pancakes are a home supper. The cheese latke is a holiday dairy cake with a separate history. Keeping the names apart matters, because both pages use Lithuania and they are not the same food.

Why it tastes this way

Kefir tangs the potato slightly and loosens the batter. Onion fries sweet at the edge. The crust is crisp and the center soft. Sour cream doubles the dairy note. They taste fresher and less purely starchy than a flour-only bind, because the kefir is sour.

How it is made

Grated potato and onion are mixed with kefir, egg, flour, and salt, then fried in oil.

Authentic touch. Use kefir, not sweet milk. Do not call these cheese latkes. Those are the Hanukkah page. Flour in this batter means it is not gluten-free.

How it is used

Hot, with sour cream, as a savory meal. Bacon on the side makes it a meat plate.

Field notes

  • Bulviniai blynai are potato pancakes. The cheese latke page is a different food.
  • Kefir is in this batter.
  • They are everyday food, not the Hanukkah cheese cake.

Ingredients

  • 600 g starchy potatoes, peeled
  • 1 small onion
  • 1 large egg
  • 80 ml kefir
  • 2 tbsp plain wheat flour
  • 1 tsp fine salt
  • Neutral oil, for frying
  • Sour cream, for serving

Steps

  1. Grate the potatoes and onion and squeeze lightly. Stir in the egg, kefir, flour, and salt.
  2. Heat a shallow layer of oil over medium-high heat.
  3. Fry rounds until both sides are brown and the potato is cooked through.
  4. Serve with sour cream.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • The farmer-cheese latke, a different cake and a different holiday story
  • No kefir, loosened with potato water only

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Sour cream
  • Fried bacon, for a meat plate
  • Dill
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Ecuador · No chili heat · 8 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Ecuador · South America

Llapingachos

Pan-fried potato cakes with cheese inside and a mild peanut sauce over the top.

25 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 cakes

History

Llapingachos are pan-fried potato cakes from the Ecuadorian Andes, especially the central highlands around Ambato. Mashed potato is stuffed with cheese, browned in a skillet, and often served with salsa de maní, a peanut sauce. Peanuts are an Andean crop. This recipe includes the sauce, so the plate contains peanut and is not nut-free. The cheese makes it a dairy dish. A fried egg or a sausage on the side is common and is not in the written steps.

Cultural background

They are breakfast and lunch in highland towns, sold in markets and cooked at home when potatoes are the meal. Annatto colors some batches a soft orange. The sauce is onion, roasted peanut, and milk, not a fiery chile paste. Ají can be served beside it. It is not mixed in here.

Why it tastes this way

Potato and salt are the body, fried so the outside browns and the cheese in the center softens. The peanut sauce tastes toasted and slightly sweet, loosened with milk and onion. Annatto adds color and a faint earthy note, not heat. Because there is no chile in the sauce, the dish stays at no chili heat. The peanut is obvious. Anyone who avoids peanuts should not make this version.

How it is made

Mash boiled potatoes with salt and a little annatto. Seal cubes of cheese inside cakes and pan-fry them. Cook onion and ground roasted peanuts with milk into a sauce and spoon it over.

Authentic touch. Chill the mashed potato before you stuff it, or the cheese leaks before a crust can form. Seal the cakes well. Simmer the peanut sauce until it is thick enough to spoon, not a broth, and salt it.

How it is used

Serve two cakes with sauce for breakfast or lunch. A fried egg on top adds the egg allergen. Chorizo adds meat and is not vegetarian. Neither is in this pan.

Field notes

  • The peanut sauce means this recipe is not nut-free.
  • Cheese inside the potato cake softens. It does not have to stretch like a pizza.
  • Ambato and the central highlands are the landscape people name with llapingachos.

Ingredients

  • 700 g potatoes, peeled
  • 1 teaspoon salt, divided
  • Pinch of ground annatto
  • 120 g fresh white cheese, in 8 cubes
  • 80 g roasted peanuts
  • 1 small onion, chopped
  • 120 ml milk
  • 2 tablespoons neutral oil

Steps

  1. Boil the potatoes until tender. Drain, mash, and season with 1/2 teaspoon salt and the annatto. Cool until you can handle the mash.
  2. Divide into 8 portions. Seal a cube of cheese inside each and flatten into a thick cake.
  3. Pan-fry in the oil over medium heat until both sides are browned and the center is hot.
  4. Meanwhile, cook the onion in a spoon of oil, add the peanuts, milk, and remaining salt, and simmer. Mash or blend the sauce until thick.
  5. Spoon the peanut sauce over the cakes and serve.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Skip the peanut sauce and the allergen list loses peanut, and the plate can be nut-free. It is a plainer dish.
  • A fried egg is a usual topping and adds egg.
  • Chorizo on the side adds meat and is not vegetarian.

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Breakfast
  • Street food
  • Ají on the side, which adds heat
  • A small salad of onion and tomato
  • Black coffee
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free

Israel · No chili heat · 4 skillet flatbreads

Flying Pancakes of the World

Israel · Middle East

Malawach

Flaky laminated flatbread, salty and toasted, with a pull-apart layer at the edge.

35 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 skillet flatbreads

History

Malawach is a Yemeni Jewish laminated flatbread. Families brought it from Yemen, and many arrived in Israel in the 1949 to 1950 airlift often called Operation Magic Carpet. In Israeli home kitchens it became a breakfast bread: the dough is layered with fat, chilled, and cooked in a skillet. This recipe uses oil, so the written dough has no dairy. Grated tomato, a hard-boiled egg, and zhug are the plate around it. The origin is Yemeni. The weekday pan on this page is an Israeli one.

Cultural background

The dough is often mixed at night and cooked in the morning, which suits a home kitchen more than a stall. It is richer than a plain pita and flakier than a single-layer chapati. Restaurants later put it on menus. The method here stays with the skillet.

Why it tastes this way

Lamination is the flavor: thin sheets of dough fried lightly in their own oil, so the bread tastes toasted and almost pastry-like without sugar. Salt is the only seasoning in the dough. Grated tomato adds acid, and zhug adds chile and herbs at the table. Those condiments are why one bread can taste different from one house to the next.

How it is made

Mix a soft dough, rest it, then stretch it thin, brush it with oil, and fold it into a packet. Chill the packet. Cook it in a dry skillet from cold-ish dough until both sides are browned and the layers pull apart. Serve with grated tomato.

Authentic touch. Layer the dough and chill it before you cook it, so the flakes separate. Remember both kitchens: the bread's Yemeni origin, and the Israeli home breakfast it became. Oil in this recipe keeps the dough free of dairy.

How it is used

Malawach is breakfast. A typical plate is the bread with grated tomato, zhug, and a hard-boiled egg. Honey is the sweet path. Egg and zhug are accompaniments. Zhug is hot even though the bread is not.

Field notes

  • Malawach is a laminated flatbread of Yemeni Jewish origin, cooked in Israeli home kitchens.
  • This dough uses oil, so the bread itself is dairy-free.
  • Grated tomato and zhug are breakfast partners, not fillings baked inside.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups wheat flour (about 250 g)
  • 3/4 teaspoon fine salt
  • 3/4 cup water
  • 4 tablespoons neutral oil, divided

Steps

  1. Mix the flour, salt, water, and one tablespoon of oil into a soft dough. Knead until smooth and rest it for 15 minutes.
  2. Divide the dough into 4. Stretch one piece into a thin sheet, brush it with oil, and fold it into a small layered packet.
  3. Repeat with the rest. Cover and chill the packets for at least 20 minutes so the layers firm up.
  4. Cook each packet in a dry skillet over medium heat, turning, until both sides are browned and the edge flakes. This is laminated dough, not a batter.
  5. Serve in an Israeli home-breakfast way: with grated tomato, and with zhug or egg if you want them.

The dough contains wheat. Zhug served alongside is a chile sauce. A hard-boiled egg adds egg only on plates that include it.

Refrigerate leftover malawach and reheat it in a dry skillet so the layers crisp. Raw packets can be kept refrigerated for a day before cooking. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Butter or margarine instead of oil changes the fat and may add dairy.
  • Honey instead of tomato makes a sweet breakfast.
  • A thinner packet cooks faster and flakes more. A thick one stays bready.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Grated fresh tomato
  • Zhug
  • A hard-boiled egg
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Lebanon · No chili heat · 6 paper-thin sheets

Flying Pancakes of the World

Lebanon · Middle East

Markook saj bread

Paper-thin wheat flatbread, soft and lightly smoky from a few seconds on a dome.

35 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 6 · 6 paper-thin sheets

History

Markook, also called saj bread, is the paper-thin flatbread cooked on a domed griddle. In Lebanon and in Syria the dough is stretched until it is almost transparent, then laid for a short time on the hot dome. It is a flatbread, not a batter pancake. Mountain and Beqaa kitchens, including Zahle, still use the saj for this bread. It is torn and used to scoop or to wrap, rather than sliced like a loaf.

Cultural background

The saj is a metal dome set over heat. The cook stretches the dough over a cushion or by hand and flips it onto the dome. The bread is done in well under a minute. It is everyday bread where the griddle is part of the kitchen, and it is also a bread people buy in stacks.

Why it tastes this way

There is almost no crumb, so the flavor is toasted wheat and a trace of olive oil. The dome gives a light smoky spot where the dough touched the metal. Because it is thin, it tastes fresh only when it is soft. Dried out, it becomes a cracker, which is a different use. Salt is the only seasoning.

How it is made

Mix a soft dough and rest it well. Divide it, stretch each piece paper thin, and lay it on a hot saj for a few seconds a side until it pales and freckles. Stack the sheets in a cloth so they stay flexible.

Authentic touch. Stretch it until you can see light through a torn edge, and cook it on a dome if you have one. A thick disk in a frying pan is not markook. An inverted wok is an imperfect home stand-in for the saj, and the bread should still be paper thin.

How it is used

Markook is an accompaniment. It wraps grilled meats and vegetables, scoops labneh, or sits beside a stew. Labneh and meat are not in the dough. The same thin bread is cooked in Syria.

Field notes

  • Markook is cooked paper thin on a domed saj griddle.
  • The same bread is made in Syria as well as Lebanon.
  • It is a flatbread. The dough is stretched, not poured as a batter.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups wheat flour (about 250 g)
  • 3/4 teaspoon fine salt
  • 3/4 cup water
  • 1 tablespoon olive oil

Steps

  1. Mix the flour, salt, water, and olive oil into a soft dough. Knead until smooth. Rest it, covered, for 20 minutes.
  2. Divide the dough into 6 pieces and keep them covered. Roll, then stretch one piece until it is paper thin.
  3. Heat a saj, a metal dome, over the burner. An overturned wok can stand in if it is stable and clean.
  4. Lay the dough on the dome for a few seconds, until it pales and freckles, then turn it briefly. It should be thin enough that a torn edge shows light.
  5. Stack the sheets in a cloth so they steam and stay soft. Repeat with the rest.

The dough contains wheat. The dome is extremely hot. If you use an overturned wok, make sure it cannot tip.

Keep markook wrapped in cloth, then in a bag, so it does not dry into crackers. Refrigerate leftovers and sprinkle them with a few drops of water before reheating on the dome. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A slightly thicker sheet is easier for a beginner and less like market markook.
  • A dusting of za'atar turns it toward manakish and adds sesame if the blend contains it.
  • Syrian kitchens cook the same dome bread under local names, including khubz saj.

Pairings and uses

  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Savory meal
  • Olives and olive oil
  • Labneh, which adds dairy
  • Grilled vegetables
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Indonesia · Mild · 2 large squares

Flying Pancakes of the World

Indonesia · Southeast Asia

Martabak telur

A thin, crisp wheat skin folded around a juicy filling of egg, seasoned beef, and onion.

30 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 2 large squares

History

Martabak telur is the savory stuffed martabak, a thin wheat dough stretched wide, filled with egg and meat, folded, and fried. Indonesia sells it from night stalls. Malaysia eats the same family of stuffed bread, so Malaysia is listed too. A related bread in the Arabian Peninsula is called mutabbaq. This page does not add Saudi Arabia, and it does not treat the names as one recipe. The written filling is egg and beef, so the tags include egg and meat. It is not the sweet martabak manis.

Cultural background

The skill is the stretch. The dough should be nearly transparent before the filling goes on. Then it is folded into a square and fried until the skin blisters. Scallion, onion, and a little chile season the meat. The heat of this version is mild.

Why it tastes this way

The crust is fried laminated dough, crisp and oily in a thin layer. Egg sets around the beef and keeps the center moist. Onion and scallion sweeten the meat. A little soy sauce and white pepper make the filling savory without a heavy curry. You taste crunch first and a peppery meat custard second. The sweet martabak has none of that pepper.

How it is made

Stretch an oiled wheat dough, cover it with beaten egg and seasoned beef, fold it into a square, and fry it.

Authentic touch. Stretch the dough until it wants to tear, then patch any hole before you add the egg. A thick skin never becomes the blistered crust stalls are known for. Cook the square until the meat is done, not only until the outside browns.

How it is used

Cut the square into pieces and eat it hot, with a pickle or a cucumber relish. It is late-night street food.

Field notes

  • Martabak telur is the savory stuffed pancake of egg and meat, not the sweet thick martabak.
  • Malaysia shares the stuffed bread. Mutabbaq is a related name elsewhere.

Ingredients

  • 1 1/2 cups all-purpose wheat flour
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1/2 cup water
  • 3 tablespoons neutral oil, plus more for frying
  • 3 eggs
  • 200 grams ground beef
  • 1 small onion, minced
  • 2 scallions, chopped
  • 1 tablespoon soy sauce
  • 1 green chile, minced

Steps

  1. Mix flour, salt, water, and 1 tablespoon of oil into a soft dough. Knead, then rest 30 minutes coated in oil.
  2. Mix beef, onion, scallion, soy sauce, and chile. Beat the eggs in a separate bowl.
  3. Divide the dough in half. Stretch one piece on an oiled board until it is very thin.
  4. Scatter half the beef over it and pour half the eggs on top. Fold the edges in to make a square.
  5. Fry in shallow oil over medium heat until both sides are brown and the beef is cooked through. Repeat.

Fry until the beef in the center is no longer pink.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Chicken instead of beef, still meat
  • Extra green chile for a hotter filling

Pairings and uses

  • Street food
  • Savory meal
  • Cucumber and vinegar relish
  • A little chile sauce
  • Sweet tea
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

India · Mild · 6 stuffed dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Masala dosa

A fermented rice-and-urad sheet wrapped around a turmeric potato filling, with curry leaf and green chile.

45 min prep · 40 min cook · Serves 4 · 6 stuffed dosas

History

Masala dosa uses the same fermented rice and urad batter as a plain dosa. The potato masala is a filling, not a second grain and not a different ferment. Restaurant kitchens in Karnataka, including Bengaluru's hotel dosas, made the potato-stuffed fold a city breakfast. The filling itself is a home potato palya that already belonged on the plate beside a plain dosa.

Cultural background

The potato should be dry enough to sit inside the roll without soaking the crisp side. Onion cooked in oil with mustard seed, curry leaf, and turmeric is the usual base. A wet curry would belong in a bowl, not in the fold.

Why it tastes this way

The batter still tastes of fermented rice and urad. The masala adds an earthy, lightly bitter turmeric note, the sweetness of onion, and the pop of mustard seed. Because the potato is spiced and then wrapped, each bite mixes a sour crisp sheet with a soft, salted center. The green chile is there for aroma more than burn.

How it is made

Ferment a rice and urad batter, cook a dry spiced potato, spread a thin dosa, lay the potato in a line, and roll.

Authentic touch. Spread the potato in a strip on the set dosa, not in a tall mound. Too much filling cracks the sheet before it can be rolled.

How it is used

Cut the roll only if you must. Most tables eat it with coconut chutney and sambar, starting at the crisp tail.

Field notes

  • The batter is still rice and urad. Potato never replaces either grain.
  • A masala dosa that sits too long steams the crust from the inside out.

Ingredients

  • 1 batch fermented rice and urad dosa batter, about 3 cups
  • 3 medium potatoes, boiled, peeled, and crumbled
  • 1 small onion, sliced
  • 1 green chile, slit
  • 1/2 teaspoon mustard seeds
  • 8 curry leaves
  • 1/4 teaspoon turmeric
  • 2 tablespoons neutral oil, plus more for the griddle
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt, or to taste

Steps

  1. Use a bubbly fermented rice and urad batter, ground and rested as for a plain dosa. Active prep here is the filling and the cooking, not a second grind.
  2. Heat 2 tablespoons of oil. Add mustard seeds. When they pop, add curry leaves, onion, and green chile. Cook until the onion softens.
  3. Add turmeric and the crumbled potato. Season with salt. Mash lightly and cook until the mixture looks dry. Cool a little.
  4. Heat a griddle, grease it, and spread a ladle of batter in a thin round. Oil the edge and cook until the underside is golden.
  5. Lay a strip of potato across the center. Fold the dosa over the filling. Serve at once with chutney.

Use batter that smells pleasantly sour. Discard it if it is moldy or putrid.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of ghee on the potato, which adds dairy
  • A little cooked green pea in the masala

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Street food
  • Coconut chutney
  • Sambar
  • Fresh coconut and cilantro
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free
