South Korea · No chili heat · 8 small pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

South Korea · East Asia

Memiljeon

A nutty buckwheat pancake, earthy and a little bitter, bound with egg and scallion.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 small pancakes

History

Memiljeon is a buckwheat pancake associated with Gangwon, the mountainous province where buckwheat is a local grain. Memil means buckwheat. The flour is naturally free of gluten and often milled where wheat dust is present, so this recipe calls for certified gluten-free buckwheat and adds no wheat. An egg binds the cake. It is a savory pancake, kin to other jeon, with a flavor that wheat batter does not have.

Cultural background

Pyeongchang and the towns around it serve buckwheat as noodles and as pancakes. The pancake is the griddled form, good with a sharp radish salad. Because the flour can be dry, the batter needs enough water to spread and enough egg to hold.

Why it tastes this way

Buckwheat tastes earthy, slightly bitter, and toasted even before it browns. That bitterness is the point, not a fault. Egg softens it. Scallion adds a fresh edge. A vinegar dip cuts the grain the way it does for other jeon. There is no chile in the cake. The mountain grain is the flavor.

How it is made

Whisk certified gluten-free buckwheat flour with egg and water, fold in scallion, and fry small cakes.

Authentic touch. Cook the pancakes smaller than a pajeon. Buckwheat batter is fragile and breaks when you try to flip a wide sheet. Certified gluten-free flour is what makes the diet tag true.

How it is used

Serve warm with a vinegar dip and a radish salad. It is savory snack food from a buckwheat region.

Field notes

  • Memil is buckwheat. Gangwon is the province most associated with it.
  • The gluten-free tag depends on certified gluten-free buckwheat and no wheat in the recipe.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup certified gluten-free buckwheat flour
  • 1 egg
  • 3/4 cup water
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 2 scallions, chopped
  • Neutral oil
  • Gluten-free tamari and rice vinegar for dipping

Steps

  1. Whisk the certified gluten-free buckwheat flour, egg, water, and salt into a pourable batter.
  2. Stir in the scallions.
  3. Oil a pan over medium heat and cook small rounds, using less batter than you would for a wheat pancake.
  4. Flip when the underside is brown and the top is set. Buckwheat breaks if you flip too early.
  5. Serve with gluten-free tamari and vinegar.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Shredded potato in the batter
  • A spoon of wheat flour, which would cancel the gluten-free tag

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Snack
  • Soy-vinegar dip made with gluten-free tamari
  • A sharp radish salad
  • Steamed rice
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Japan · No chili heat · 1 shared pan

Flying Pancakes of the World

Japan · East Asia

Monjayaki

A loose, runny savory batter cooked on a griddle and eaten while it is still half set.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 2 · 1 shared pan

History

Monjayaki is Tokyo's runny savory pancake, especially the food of Tsukishima's monja street. The batter is much looser than Osaka okonomiyaki. Cabbage and other bits are fried first, then a wheat batter made with dashi is poured around them and cooked until it is partly set. People eat it from the griddle with small spatulas before it firms into a cake. This recipe uses bonito dashi, so it includes fish. It is a different dish from both Osaka and Hiroshima okonomiyaki.

Cultural background

The pleasure is the texture: a savory pool that stretches and then sets at the edges. Waiting for a firm pancake misses the point. Tsukishima shops expect you to cook it at the table. A home griddle or a wide pan is enough if you keep the batch small.

Why it tastes this way

Dashi is the flavor, a light fish stock that wheat and cabbage would not have on their own. The batter stays wet, so it tastes more like a savory custard than a browned pancake. Cabbage and a little onion sweetens it. The set edge is saltier and toastier. Eating it half-cooked on purpose is why the center tastes soft and marine rather than fried through.

How it is made

Fry cabbage, pour a very loose dashi batter over it, cook until the rim sets, and eat it from the pan half-set.

Authentic touch. Spread the batter thin and start eating from the firmed edge while the middle is still soft. If you flip it like a finished okonomiyaki, you have overcooked it.

How it is used

Serve it on the griddle with small spatulas. It is a shared savory snack, not a slice you pack for later.

Field notes

  • Monjayaki is meant to be eaten while the center is still soft.
  • Tsukishima in Tokyo is the neighborhood most tied to monja restaurants.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup all-purpose wheat flour
  • 1 cup bonito and kombu dashi
  • 1 egg
  • 200 grams finely chopped cabbage
  • 1/4 onion, sliced
  • 1 tablespoon neutral oil
  • A pinch of salt
  • Aonori, optional

Steps

  1. Whisk flour, dashi, egg, and salt into a thin batter, looser than crepe batter.
  2. Oil a griddle or wide pan on medium. Fry the cabbage and onion until they brighten.
  3. Push the vegetables into a ring. Pour the batter into the middle and across them.
  4. Cook without flipping until the edge looks set and the center is still soft and stretchy.
  5. Sprinkle aonori if you like and eat it from the pan with small spatulas, starting at the edge.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A few shrimp, which add shellfish
  • Cheese at the end, which adds dairy

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Street food
  • A cold barley tea
  • Pickled ginger
  • Aonori sprinkled at the table
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Myanmar · Mild · 8 joined pairs

Flying Pancakes of the World

Myanmar · Southeast Asia

Mont lin maya

A pair of small rice cakes from a dimpled pan, one plain and one topped with split peas and shallot, joined together.

20 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 joined pairs

History

Mont lin maya are Myanmar's small batter cakes cooked in a dimpled pan and sold as snacks. The name means husband and wife and refers to the pair: one plain cake and one with a topping, cooked in neighboring cups and then pressed together. Yangon street stalls turn them out through the day. The batter is rice flour. This recipe tops the second cake with boiled split peas and shallot, with no egg and no wheat. A quail egg is another common topping and would add an egg allergen.

Cultural background

The pan is the same idea as other Southeast Asian dimpled pans, with a Burmese batter and a paired serving. The cakes are finger food. A little chile in the pea topping keeps the heat mild. They are a snack, not a feast loaf.

Why it tastes this way

Rice batter fried in a little oil tastes lightly sweet and toasted at the rim. Split peas add an earthy, mealy contrast. Shallot and a green chile make the topping savory. Pressing the plain cake onto the topped one traps that filling and softens the crust just enough. Coconut milk in the batter, used here in a small amount, rounds the grain. The pair is the dish, which is what the name is describing.

How it is made

Cook rice batter in a dimpled pan, leave half the cups plain, top the other half with peas and shallot, and join each pair.

Authentic touch. Cook a plain cup and a topped cup at the same time, then clap them together while they are still flexible. Two plain cakes miss the point of the name.

How it is used

Eat them warm as a street snack. They are savory and mildly hot from the chile in the peas.

Field notes

  • The name mont lin maya refers to the pair of cakes, one plain and one topped, joined together.
  • They are cooked in a dimpled pan and sold as snacks in Myanmar.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup rice flour, with no wheat added
  • 1 cup water
  • 1/2 cup coconut milk
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1/2 cup cooked yellow split peas
  • 1 shallot, minced
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • Neutral oil

Steps

  1. Whisk rice flour, water, coconut milk, and salt into a pourable batter.
  2. Mix the split peas with shallot and green chile.
  3. Oil a hot dimpled pan. Fill the cups halfway.
  4. Leave half the cups plain. Spoon peas onto the other half. Cook until the bottoms are brown and the centers are set.
  5. Press a plain cake onto a topped cake to make a pair. Repeat with the rest.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A quail egg on the topped half, which adds egg
  • A pinch of turmeric in the batter for color

Pairings and uses

  • Snack
  • Street food
  • Savory meal
  • A cup of tea
  • A squeeze of lime
  • More fried shallot
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

India · Mild · 8 small cheelas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Moong dal cheela

A soft yellow-lentil pancake, bright with ginger and green chile, less nutty than a besan cheela.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 small cheelas

History

Moong dal cheela is a North Indian pancake made from soaked split yellow moong, ground and cooked the same day. It is not a besan cheela. The dal is the whole batter, not chickpea flour stirred with water. Rajasthani and other northern kitchens use it as a light breakfast. Andhra pesarattu uses whole green moong and has its own page. This one is the split yellow dal of the north.

Cultural background

Soaking is the only wait. There is no ferment to plan the night before. Ginger in the grind is what makes a plain dal batter taste finished. Jaipur homes serve it with a sharp chutney or a spoon of pickle.

Why it tastes this way

Split yellow moong grinds sweet and mild, with a legume aroma that stays fresher than toasted besan. Ginger and green chile cut that sweetness. Cumin adds a warm note without the thyme-like ajwain of a Delhi gram cheela. The pan dries the surface so the cake tastes toasted at the rim and custardy in the center.

How it is made

Soak yellow moong dal, grind it with ginger and chile, and cook small pancakes at once.

Authentic touch. Drain the dal well before grinding. Extra soak water makes a watery crepe that never browns. The batter should drop from a spoon in a ribbon.

How it is used

Serve warm with cilantro chutney. It is breakfast food, eaten with the hands or a fork the same morning it is ground.

Field notes

  • This cheela is ground moong dal, not chickpea flour.
  • Split yellow moong is the usual dal. Whole green moong is the Andhra pesarattu.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup split yellow moong dal
  • 1 inch ginger
  • 1 green chile
  • 1/2 teaspoon cumin seeds
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Water for soaking and a splash for grinding
  • Neutral oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Rinse the dal and soak it for 2 to 3 hours. Drain thoroughly.
  2. Grind with ginger, chile, cumin, salt, and as little water as you need for a thick, smooth batter.
  3. Cook without an overnight ferment. If the batter looks stiff, add a teaspoon of water.
  4. Oil a medium-hot pan. Spread a small ladle into a neat round and cook until the underside is golden.
  5. Flip and cook until the center is set. Repeat with the remaining batter.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Chopped spinach folded through the batter
  • A pinch of turmeric for color

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Cilantro chutney
  • A lime wedge
  • Sliced tomato
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

India · Medium · 6 chutney dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Mysore masala dosa

A crisp fermented dosa spread inside with red chile-garlic chutney and a line of potato masala.

45 min prep · 40 min cook · Serves 4 · 6 chutney dosas

History

Mysore masala dosa is a restaurant style associated with Mysuru and the wider Karnataka hotel kitchen. The batter is still fermented rice and urad. What marks the plate is a red chutney, usually dried red chile and garlic, spread on the dosa before the potato goes in. This page does not name an inventor or a founding year. The red smear is the distinction from a plain potato masala dosa.

Cultural background

The chutney is cooked or ground smooth enough to paint with the back of a spoon. It should season the sheet, not drown it. Kitchens in Mysuru vary the chile and the amount of garlic. The potato remains the familiar dry masala.

Why it tastes this way

Dried red chile brings heat and a brick-red color that fresh green chile does not. Garlic, raw or lightly cooked depending on the cook, stays sharp against the sour rice batter. Potato and turmeric soften those edges. Because the chutney is spread on the cooked surface, it fries for a few seconds and tastes toasted rather than like a cold sauce.

How it is made

Cook a thin fermented dosa, paint it with red chile chutney, add a strip of potato masala, and fold.

Authentic touch. Spread a thin film of red chutney on the dosa as soon as the top sets, then add potato. A thick layer of chutney burns before the potato warms.

How it is used

Serve immediately, chutney side in, with coconut chutney if you want a cool contrast. The interior is medium-hot.

Field notes

  • The red chutney spread is what makes a masala dosa a Mysore masala dosa.
  • The grain is still fermented rice and urad, the same family as a plain dosa.

Ingredients

  • 1 batch fermented rice and urad dosa batter, about 3 cups
  • 4 dried red chiles, stemmed
  • 3 garlic cloves
  • 1 tablespoon neutral oil, plus more for cooking
  • Salt
  • 3 boiled potatoes, crumbled
  • 1 small onion, sliced
  • 1/2 teaspoon mustard seeds
  • 1/4 teaspoon turmeric
  • 8 curry leaves

Steps

  1. Have bubbly fermented rice and urad batter ready, made as for a plain dosa.
  2. Soak the dried chiles in hot water for 10 minutes. Grind them with garlic, a pinch of salt, and a teaspoon of water to a thick paste.
  3. For the potato, heat a tablespoon of oil, pop the mustard seeds, add curry leaves and onion, then turmeric and potato. Salt it and cook until dry.
  4. Spread a thin dosa on a hot oiled griddle. When the top sets, smear a thin layer of red chutney over it.
  5. Add a line of potato, fold, and cook another half minute so the chutney heats through. Serve hot.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of jaggery on the side for a sweet-hot plate
  • Less dried chile for a milder chutney

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Coconut chutney
  • Sambar
  • A ripe banana, a common South Indian breakfast companion
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Ukraine · No chili heat · 8 rolled crepes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Ukraine · Eastern Europe

Nalysnyky

Ukrainian crepes rolled with cheese and baked in sour cream.

30 min prep · 30 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 rolled crepes

History

Nalysnyky are Ukrainian filled crepes. A cheese filling baked under sour cream is the version written here. Meat fillings are also traditional and would make the dish not vegetarian. They are feast and Sunday food as well as a way to use cheese. They are not Russian blinchiki, which this journal fries after rolling, and they are not deruny, the potato pancakes.

Cultural background

The crepes are thin wheat sheets. The dish is finished in the oven so the sour cream thickens around the rolls. It can be a main course.

Why it tastes this way

Farmer cheese is mild and slightly sour. Egg and a pinch of salt keep the filling savory rather than dessert-sweet. This page does not sugar the cheese. Sour cream browns at the edges and sauces the crepes. Butter in the crepe batter tastes toasted. A sweet nalysnyk exists. This one is the savory cheese bake.

How it is made

Thin crepes are filled with seasoned farmer cheese, rolled, covered with sour cream, and baked until bubbling.

Authentic touch. Bake them in sour cream, covered for part of the time, so the crepes soften and the sauce thickens. Do not serve them as a dry roll if you want this dish.

How it is used

Hot, as a savory meal or a festive dish, with a salad. A sweet filling is a different plate.

Field notes

  • Nalysnyky are Ukrainian filled crepes.
  • This version is savory cheese baked in sour cream.
  • Deruny, the potato pancakes, are a different page.

Ingredients

  • 150 g plain wheat flour
  • 2 large eggs, plus 1 for the filling
  • 350 ml whole milk
  • 1/2 tsp fine salt, divided
  • 30 g unsalted butter, for the pan
  • 400 g farmer cheese, drained
  • 300 ml sour cream
  • 2 tbsp chopped dill

Steps

  1. Whisk the flour, 2 eggs, milk, and 1/4 tsp salt into a thin batter. Rest 15 minutes and cook eight crepes in butter.
  2. Mix the farmer cheese with the remaining egg, the rest of the salt, and the dill.
  3. Fill and roll the crepes. Lay them in a buttered baking dish and spoon the sour cream over them.
  4. Bake at 190°C until the cream is bubbling and the edges brown, about 20 minutes.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A meat filling, which adds a meat allergen
  • A sweet cheese and raisin filling, closer to a dessert

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Feast or fast day
  • The sour cream they bake in
  • Dill
  • A green salad
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

India · No chili heat · 10 soft dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Neer dosa

A soft, pale, almost translucent rice roll with a clean cereal taste and no sour ferment.

25 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 10 soft dosas

History

Neer dosa belongs to Mangaluru and the Tulu coast of Karnataka. Neer means water, which matches the batter: soaked rice ground with enough water to look like thin rice milk. No fermentation is required. The dosa is cooked quickly, stays flexible, and is rolled rather than crisped into a wafer. It is a coastal rice bread, separate from the fermented rice-and-urad dosa of the inland hotel menu.

Cultural background

Tulu and other coastal kitchens serve it with coconut chutney, a chicken or fish curry, or a simple scrape of jaggery and grated coconut. This recipe keeps the dosa itself free of fish and meat so the tags match the written batter.

Why it tastes this way

Without urad and without a ferment, there is no tang and no lacy wheat crust. Freshly ground rice tastes sweet and mild. The extra water lets the sheet set thin and pliable instead of brittle. Coconut, if you add a little to the grind, rounds the flavor the way coastal cooking often does, with the fat of the nut rather than with ghee.

How it is made

Soak rice, grind it with plenty of water to a watery batter, and cook soft rounds that can be rolled at once.

Authentic touch. Use a warm, not screaming-hot, pan and spread with the back of the ladle in one or two strokes. The dosa is done when the top turns from shiny to matte. Browning it hard dries it out.

How it is used

Stack them under a clean cloth so they stay flexible. Serve with coconut chutney. A fish curry is a common Mangaluru partner and would add a fish allergen.

Field notes

  • Neer dosa does not need an overnight ferment. The batter is cooked the day it is ground.
  • The sheet is supposed to bend. A cracker-crisp result is a different dosa.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup raw rice
  • 1 1/4 cups water, plus more for grinding
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 tablespoon grated coconut, optional
  • Neutral oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Rinse the rice and soak it in water for 3 to 4 hours. Soaking is waiting, not active prep.
  2. Drain and grind the rice with the coconut, if using, and fresh water until the batter is completely smooth and as thin as light cream.
  3. Stir in the salt. The batter should pour in a steady stream. Add water if the grind is thick.
  4. Heat a nonstick or well-seasoned pan over medium heat. Grease it lightly. Pour a ladle of batter and tilt the pan, or spread once, to make a thin round.
  5. Cover for a few seconds if the top looks wet. When the surface is matte and the edge lifts, roll the dosa off. Keep the rolls covered while you finish the batch.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A small piece of coconut ground with the rice
  • A pinch of salt only, which is the plainest home version

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Coconut chutney
  • Jaggery and fresh coconut
  • A vegetable coconut curry
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Japan · No chili heat · 2 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Japan · East Asia

Negiyaki

A scallion-heavy savory pancake, sweeter and greener than cabbage okonomiyaki, with a soft wheat crumb.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 2 pancakes

History

Negiyaki is a scallion pancake of the Kansai, associated with Osaka and Kobe scallion cakes that predate the modern cabbage-heavy okonomiyaki. Negi means scallion. This page does not give the dish an invention year. The batter is wheat and egg, crowded with scallion, and cooked thicker than Korean pajeon. A kombu and shiitake stock keeps this version vegetarian. It is an older relative in the savory batter family, not a cabbage okonomiyaki with a new sauce.

Cultural background

Kobe and Osaka both still serve negiyaki, sometimes under names that vary by shop. The pancake is softer and more onion-sweet than a Korean jeon, and it lacks the tall cabbage of later okonomiyaki. Sauce and mayonnaise are optional. The scallion should be obvious in the crumb.

Why it tastes this way

A large amount of scallion steams inside the batter and turns sweet, with a green bite at the tips. Wheat and egg make a cushioned crumb rather than a thin cracker. Kombu and dried shiitake add a quiet savor without fish. Because cabbage is not the bulk, the cake tastes cleaner and more onion-like than Osaka okonomiyaki.

How it is made

Fold a large quantity of scallion into a wheat batter made with kombu-shiitake stock, then griddle it slowly.

Authentic touch. Cut the scallions fairly fine and fold them in until the batter looks green. A few strips on top will not make negiyaki.

How it is used

Cut and eat it hot, with sauce if you want it, or with only salt. It is a savory street cake.

Field notes

  • Negiyaki is tied to Kansai scallion cakes that came before cabbage-heavy okonomiyaki.
  • This page does not state an invention year for it.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup all-purpose wheat flour
  • 1 egg
  • 3/4 cup kombu and shiitake stock
  • 6 scallions, finely chopped
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Neutral oil

Steps

  1. Simmer a piece of kombu and two dried shiitakes in 2 cups of water for 10 minutes. Measure 3/4 cup and cool it. Save the rest for another pot.
  2. Whisk flour, egg, stock, and salt. Fold in the scallions.
  3. Oil a pan over medium-low. Spread half the batter into a thick round.
  4. Cover and cook until the underside browns and the center sets. Flip and finish the other side.
  5. Repeat. Slice and serve.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A pinch of tenkasu, tempura bits, which are wheat
  • Bonito flakes, which would add fish

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Street food
  • Okonomiyaki sauce
  • Mayonnaise
  • Aonori
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Mexico · Mild · 8 memelas

Flying Pancakes of the World

Mexico · Mexico & Central America

Oaxacan memelas

An elongated masa cake spread with oil, black beans, and a mild salsa, toasted at the edges.

30 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 memelas

History

Memelas are an Oaxacan comal cake: masa patted into a long oval, cooked, then brushed with fat and salsa. In many Oaxacan kitchens the fat is asiento, the soft drippings left after pork is rendered, or lard. This recipe uses neutral vegetable oil instead, so the plate stays vegetarian and vegan and the allergen list has no meat. The dough is still nixtamalized masa, a corn food older in Mexico than wheat hotcakes. A memela is longer than a sope and is not split like a gordita.

Cultural background

On a Oaxacan breakfast table, memelas sit beside eggs, beans, and salsa, or they are eaten from a market comal. The shape is elongated on purpose. A round sope from another state is a different antojito.

Why it tastes this way

The masa toasts along the thin edges and stays soft in the thicker middle. Oil carries the aroma of the salsa into the surface. Black beans add an earthy layer. The salsa, tomato and a small chile, is acidic and only mildly hot. Without asiento, you lose the porky depth those drippings give, and you taste the corn and the salsa more directly.

How it is made

Griddle oval masa cakes, brush with vegetable oil, spread a thin layer of black beans and salsa, and toast them again until the edges crisp.

Authentic touch. Pat the dough into a long oval, not a circle. Brush the oil on after the first cook, then return the memela to the comal so the salsa dries a little and sticks.

How it is used

Eat them for breakfast or as a market snack, with extra salsa if you want it. If you switch the oil for asiento, say so, because the dish is then made with pork.

Field notes

  • Asiento is pork fat, not a spice. This page leaves it out and uses vegetable oil.
  • Memelas are elongated. Sopes are smaller rounds with a pinched rim.
  • The masa is nixtamalized. Plain cornmeal will not stretch into the same oval.

Ingredients

  • 250 g masa harina
  • 320 ml warm water
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 3 tablespoons neutral vegetable oil
  • 200 g cooked black beans, mashed smooth
  • 2 tomatoes, 1 jalapeño, and a pinch of salt, chopped into a mild salsa

Steps

  1. Mix the masa harina, salt, and warm water. Knead, cover, and rest 20 minutes.
  2. Divide into 8 pieces and pat each into an elongated oval, thicker in the center.
  3. Cook on a dry comal over medium heat until both sides are set and spotted, about 3 minutes a side.
  4. Brush each memela with vegetable oil. Spread a thin layer of black beans and a spoon of salsa.
  5. Return them to the comal for a minute so the toppings heat and the edges crisp. Serve hot.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Asiento or lard in place of vegetable oil is the older Oaxacan spread. It adds meat and is not vegetarian or vegan.
  • Crumbled fresh cheese is a common finish. It adds dairy.
  • A red salsa de chile pasilla oaxaqueño is a local sauce. Use a mild amount if you want to keep the heat down.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Street food
  • Savory meal
  • Black coffee or hot chocolate
  • Eggs cooked in salsa, on the side
  • Sliced avocado
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

India · Mild · 8 onion dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Onion rava dosa

Crisp semolina lace crowded with sweet onion, green chile, and curry leaf.

25 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 onion dosas

History

Onion rava dosa keeps the unfermented semolina batter and puts onion in the sheet itself. Udupi hotel kitchens, which sent many South Indian breakfasts to other cities, treat it as its own dosa on the board, next to a plain rava dosa. The onion is mixed through or scattered on the pour so it fries into the lace. It is still wheat semolina, not a fermented rice and urad dosa with onions on the side.

Cultural background

Cut the onion small enough to cook in the time the lace crisps. Large raw chunks steam and leave pale pockets. A pinch of ginger is common in Udupi batters and keeps the onion from tasting flat.

Why it tastes this way

Onion fries in the oil at the edge of the batter and turns sweet. Semolina still supplies the wheat toast and the brittle holes. Chile and curry leaf cut that sweetness. Because there is no overnight souring, the onion stays bright instead of fading into a fermented tang.

How it is made

Mix a thin semolina and rice-flour batter, fold in plenty of onion, and pour it loosely on a hot griddle.

Authentic touch. Scatter the onion as you pour, then leave the pan alone until the rim lifts. Stirring after that pulls the lace apart.

How it is used

Serve open-faced so the onion stays visible, with coconut chutney. Folding is fine once the sheet is crisp.

Field notes

  • Onion rava dosa is still a semolina batter. The onion does not change the grain.
  • Udupi restaurants often list it apart from both plain dosa and plain rava dosa.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup fine wheat semolina
  • 1/3 cup rice flour
  • 1 1/2 cups cold water
  • 1 medium onion, finely chopped
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • 1 teaspoon grated ginger
  • 10 curry leaves, chopped
  • 1/2 teaspoon cumin seeds
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Neutral oil for the griddle

Steps

  1. Stir semolina, rice flour, salt, and water until smooth and thin. Rest 15 minutes.
  2. Fold in onion, chile, ginger, curry leaves, and cumin. The batter should look busy with onion and still pour from a ladle.
  3. Heat a griddle well. Oil it. Pour the batter in ribbons so holes remain, carrying onion along with each pour.
  4. Add extra onion to any bare spot. Drizzle oil and cook until the onion browns and the sheet snaps at the edge.
  5. Remove it whole. Stir the bowl before the next ladle so the onion does not settle at the bottom.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A teaspoon of grated ginger in the batter
  • A few cashews on one dosa, which adds tree nut

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Coconut chutney
  • Sambar
  • A small bowl of sugar and ghee for people who want a sweet bite
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Japan · No chili heat · 2 thick pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Japan · East Asia

Osaka okonomiyaki

A thick savory pancake with cabbage mixed into the batter, topped with sauce, mayonnaise, and bonito flakes.

25 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 2 thick pancakes

History

Osaka okonomiyaki mixes shredded cabbage into a wheat batter and griddles it as one thick cake. That mix is the Osaka method. Hiroshima's okonomiyaki is a different dish, built in layers, and it has its own page. Pork is a standard add-in. Bonito flakes on top are dried fish. The cake grew up as street and restaurant food in Osaka. This page does not give it a single invention year. It is not the scallion-only negiyaki, and it is not a sweet pancake.

Cultural background

Okonomi means as you like it, which explains the add-ins, not the shape. In Osaka the cabbage is still inside the batter. Grated yam helps the texture in many kitchens. Aonori and a brown savory sauce finish the crust. No trademark sauce is required. A homemade or unlabeled okonomiyaki sauce works.

Why it tastes this way

Cabbage wilts into the batter and tastes sweet and juicy, so the pancake is not a dry flour cake. Wheat and egg make a soft crumb. Pork browns at the edge and adds salt. The sauce is sweet and tangy, mayonnaise is rich, and bonito flakes add a smoky fish note that moves in the heat. The flavor is savory and layered on top of a vegetable-heavy interior.

How it is made

Mix cabbage into wheat batter, griddle it slowly with pork, flip once, and finish with sauce, mayonnaise, aonori, and bonito.

Authentic touch. Fold the cabbage in until every strand is wet, then cook covered on medium-low. A hot rush burns the outside and leaves the cabbage raw.

How it is used

Cut into wedges and share at the griddle if you have one. Eat it hot, while the bonito still twists. It is a savory meal.

Field notes

  • In Osaka the cabbage goes into the batter. Hiroshima stacks its ingredients in layers instead.
  • Bonito flakes are dried fish, so this plate is not vegetarian.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup all-purpose wheat flour
  • 1 egg
  • 3/4 cup water or unsalted bonito dashi
  • 400 grams shredded green cabbage
  • 2 scallions, chopped
  • 80 grams thinly sliced pork belly
  • Neutral oil
  • Okonomiyaki sauce, mayonnaise, aonori, and bonito flakes to finish

Steps

  1. Whisk flour, egg, and dashi or water into a batter. Fold in cabbage and scallions until the shreds are coated.
  2. Oil a wide pan over medium-low heat. Spread half the batter into a thick round. Lay pork on top.
  3. Cover and cook about 8 minutes, until the underside is brown and the batter has set most of the way.
  4. Flip so the pork hits the pan. Cover and cook until the pork is done and the center is not raw.
  5. Turn the cake pork-side up. Spread sauce and mayonnaise, then aonori and bonito. Repeat with the rest.

Cook the pork through. The center of a thick cake needs the covered time.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Squid instead of pork, which swaps meat for more shellfish
  • A handful of shrimp, which adds shellfish

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Street food
  • Okonomiyaki sauce
  • Mayonnaise
  • Aonori and bonito flakes
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

South Korea · Mild · 2 large pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

South Korea · East Asia

Pajeon

A savory wheat pancake packed with scallion, crisp at the rim and tender in the middle.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 2 large pancakes

History

Pajeon is Korea's scallion pancake, a wheat batter cooked on a griddle with a great quantity of scallion. Seoul rainy-day menus and home skillets both claim it. The batter often includes a little rice flour for crispness and an egg for binding. This page is the vegetable scallion pancake. Seafood pajeon and kimchi jeon are different batters with their own pages. The cake is wheat, so it is not gluten-free.

Cultural background

A dipping sauce of soy, vinegar, and a pinch of gochugaru is part of eating pajeon, more than a syrup would be. The pancake itself is only mildly hot if the dip carries the chile. Markets sell it by the piece. Homes cut it into squares.

Why it tastes this way

Scallion fries in the batter and turns sweet and green. Wheat browns at the contact with oil. Egg keeps the interior soft so the pancake is not a cracker. Rice flour, used in a small amount, dries the surface and helps the edge crackle. The dip adds salt, acid, and a mild chile heat that the batter does not need to carry alone.

How it is made

Mix a wheat batter, fold in a large bunch of scallion, and pan-fry it as one savory cake, finished with a soy-vinegar dip.

Authentic touch. Use more scallion than batter. If the pan looks like a plain pancake with a few greens, add another handful. Press the scallions into the batter as it sets.

How it is used

Cut it with kitchen scissors or a spatula and eat it hot with the dip. It is a snack, a side, or a rainy-day meal with rice.

Field notes

  • Pa means scallion. Pajeon is the scallion pancake, not the seafood one.
  • The batter is wheat. A gluten-free claim would not match this recipe.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup all-purpose wheat flour
  • 2 tablespoons rice flour
  • 1 egg
  • 3/4 cup cold water
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 2 bunches scallions, cut into 2-inch lengths
  • 2 tablespoons neutral oil
  • 2 tablespoons soy sauce
  • 1 tablespoon rice vinegar
  • 1/2 teaspoon gochugaru

Steps

  1. Whisk the flours, egg, water, and salt into a smooth batter. Stir in the scallions so they are coated, not drowned.
  2. Stir soy sauce, vinegar, and gochugaru together for the dip.
  3. Heat oil in a wide pan over medium-high heat. Spread half the batter into a round and press the scallions flat.
  4. Cook until the underside is deep gold and crisp, then flip and cook the other side through.
  5. Repeat with the rest. Slice and serve with the dip.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of rice flour for a crisper edge
  • Extra gochugaru in the dip

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Snack
  • Street food
  • Soy and vinegar dip
  • Steamed rice
  • Sliced cucumber
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

India · Mild · 6 green cheelas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Palak cheela

A green spinach and gram-flour pancake, savory with ginger and cumin, soft rather than crisp.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 3 · 6 green cheelas

History

Palak cheela is a North Indian gram-flour pancake with spinach ground or finely chopped into the batter. Besan is still the structure. The spinach is the vegetable, not a dye from somewhere else. Kitchens from Punjab through Delhi make it as a breakfast that uses a bunch of greens. It is unfermented. It is not the plain onion besan cheela, and it is not a moong dal cake.

Cultural background

Chandigarh markets sell spinach through the cool months, which is when this cheela tastes sweetest. The leaf should be washed well. Grit in the batter is the usual failure, not the spice. A mild green chile is enough.

Why it tastes this way

Spinach sweetens and loosens besan, and it turns the crumb green and moist. Ginger keeps the green from tasting grassy in a raw way. Cumin is the savory line. Because the cake is thicker and wetter than a plain cheela, the chickpea flavor is softer and the spinach reads in every bite.

How it is made

Blend spinach with besan, ginger, and spices, then cook small green pancakes on a moderate pan.

Authentic touch. Squeeze the cooked or very finely chopped spinach so the batter does not become a soup. Extra leaf water steams the cake and stops it browning.

How it is used

Serve hot with a spoon of pickle or a dairy-free chutney. It is a savory breakfast.

Field notes

  • Palak is spinach. The flour is still besan.
  • The cake is mixed and cooked at once, with no ferment.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups spinach leaves, washed
  • 1 cup besan (chickpea flour), with no wheat added
  • 1 inch ginger
  • 1 green chile
  • 1/2 teaspoon cumin seeds
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Water only if the batter is stiff
  • Neutral oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Blend spinach, ginger, and green chile to a coarse puree.
  2. Stir in besan, cumin, and salt. Add a spoon of water only if you cannot spread the batter. Rest 10 minutes.
  3. Oil a pan over medium heat. Spread a small round.
  4. Cook until the underside browns and the top loses its raw shine. Flip and cook through so the besan is done.
  5. Repeat. The green darkens as it cooks, which is expected.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A handful of fenugreek leaves with the spinach
  • Grated carrot for sweetness

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Mint chutney
  • Lime
  • A side of yogurt, which is dairy
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

India · No chili heat · 4 very large dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Paper dosa

A very large, very thin fermented rice sheet that shatters at the edge and tastes toasted.

40 min prep · 30 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 very large dosas

History

Paper dosa is a size and a cook, not a different grain. The batter is the fermented rice and urad batter of a plain dosa. The cook spreads it across a wide griddle until the sheet is almost translucent, then keeps it on the heat until the rim turns brittle. Restaurant menus sometimes call that spread a paper roast. The spectacle is the diameter. The cereal is unchanged.

Cultural background

Homes with a smaller pan still make a paper-style dosa by using less batter and a hotter surface. The restaurant version is larger because the griddle is larger. Either way, the test is thinness and a dry crackle, not a new recipe of flours.

Why it tastes this way

More of the batter touches iron, so toast and a light ferment sourness dominate. There is almost no soft center to cushion the taste. Oil at the edge fries the lace. Because the grain is still rice and urad, the aroma matches a plain dosa that has been cooked further and thinner.

How it is made

Spread fermented rice and urad batter very thin on a broad hot griddle and cook it until the sheet is crisp enough to crack.

Authentic touch. Use less batter than pride suggests, and spread in one confident spiral. A second pass piles batter back onto the center and ruins the paper thickness.

How it is used

Bring it to the table whole, standing or folded once, with chutney and sambar. It is a shared breakfast more than a stuffed one.

Field notes

  • Paper dosa does not introduce wheat, rava, or millet. It is a thin cook of rice and urad batter.
  • The wide restaurant griddle is why the famous ones look longer than a home dosa.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup idli rice
  • 1/3 cup skinned urad dal
  • 1/4 teaspoon fenugreek seeds
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Water for soaking and grinding
  • Neutral oil for a wide griddle

Steps

  1. Soak rice, and soak urad with fenugreek, in separate bowls for 4 to 6 hours. Grind the urad fluffy and the rice fine. Mix with salt.
  2. Ferment until bubbly and mildly sour, commonly 8 to 14 hours. Active prep does not include that rest. Discard spoiled batter.
  3. Loosen the batter with water until it spreads easily in a thin film. Heat a wide griddle until a water drop dances.
  4. Grease lightly. Pour a small ladle and spread it at once to the full width of the pan, as thin as the batter will allow.
  5. Oil the edge and cook without flipping until the sheet is pale gold, dry, and brittle at the rim. Fold once and serve.

Discard batter that is moldy or smells putrid.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A brush of oil only, no filling
  • Ghee on the last ten seconds of cooking, which adds dairy

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Street food
  • Coconut chutney
  • Sambar
  • Molaga podi with sesame oil
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United States · No chili heat · 12 potato cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United States · North America

Pennsylvania Dutch potato pancakes

Shredded potato, onion, and pepper, crisp at the edge and soft in the middle.

25 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 potato cakes

History

Pennsylvania Dutch potato pancakes are shredded-potato cakes griddled by German-speaking communities in Pennsylvania. Pennsylvania Dutch means Deutsch, not a recipe from the Netherlands. The cakes are cooked on a griddle, with crisp edges and a soft center. Lancaster County is the map pin.

Cultural background

Potato cakes traveled with German-speaking cooks and became everyday food, not only a holiday plate. They are related to other shredded-potato cakes and are not the same page as a Jewish latke.

Why it tastes this way

Potato starch that stays in the bowl is part of what holds the cake together. Rinsing the shreds until the water runs clear can make them fall apart. Onion browns at the edge and keeps the potato from tasting flat. The griddle, with only a thin film of oil, fries the surface without deep-frying the center.

How it is made

Shredded potato and onion griddled in a thin film of oil.

Authentic touch. Griddle them, do not deep-fry them. Squeeze excess water from the potatoes and leave the white starch in the bowl.

How it is used

Hot, with applesauce or sour cream if you want dairy at the table. The cakes themselves have no milk or butter.

Field notes

  • Pennsylvania Dutch comes from Deutsch, meaning German, not from the Netherlands.
  • These cakes are griddled. The funnel cake from the same communities is the fried one.

Ingredients

  • 1 1/2 pounds russet potatoes
  • 1 small yellow onion
  • 2 large eggs
  • 3 tablespoons all-purpose flour
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 1/4 teaspoon black pepper
  • 3 tablespoons neutral oil

Steps

  1. Peel the potatoes and grate them on the large holes of a box grater. Grate the onion the same way.
  2. Squeeze the mixture in a towel over a bowl. Pour off the water that collects and keep the white starch at the bottom of the bowl.
  3. Stir the potato, onion, starch, eggs, flour, salt, and pepper together.
  4. Heat a thin film of oil in a skillet over medium heat.
  5. Drop 1/4 cup mounds, flatten them, and cook until the underside is deep brown, about 3 to 4 minutes. Flip and cook until the center is tender.
  6. Add oil as needed. Serve hot.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Add a spoon of minced parsley for a greener cake.
  • Flour can be reduced if the potatoes are very starchy, but do not rinse the starch away.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Applesauce
  • Sour cream, if you eat dairy
  • Chives
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Peru · Mild · 2 cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Peru · South America

Peruvian tacu-tacu

A pan-fried cake of leftover rice and beans, garlicky, with a crisp face and mild ají amarillo.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 2 · 2 cakes

History

Tacu-tacu is a cake of leftover rice and beans, fried in a skillet until the outside is crisp. It is Lima criollo cooking. Afro-Peruvian cooks helped build that coastal cuisine in the colonial city and afterward, working with Indigenous ingredients and Spanish techniques in kitchens shaped by slavery and, later, by free Black communities. The dish itself is practical: the previous day's rice and beans, garlic, onion, and ají amarillo, pressed into a hot pan. A fried egg or a breaded steak is a common addition. Those are not in this recipe.

Cultural background

Criollo cooking is the coastal home cooking of Lima and other Peruvian port cities. Tacu-tacu is what a cook does with rice and beans that are already seasoned and a little dry. It is lunch or a heavy breakfast. This version stays with the rice and beans so the plate can be vegan.

Why it tastes this way

Day-old rice fries harder and crisper than fresh rice, which is why leftovers are the right start. Beans break into the grains and salt the cake. Garlic and onion, cooked first, perfume the oil. Ají amarillo is fruity and mildly hot, enough to set the heat at mild without burning. There is no cheese and no egg in the cake, so the flavor stays with the beans, the rice, and the chile.

How it is made

Fry onion and garlic, add ají amarillo, cooked beans, and cooked rice. Mash part of the mixture, press it into a cake in the skillet, and fry until a crust forms on both sides.

Authentic touch. Use rice and beans that are dry enough to mash and hold a shape. Wet rice steams and never crusts. Press the cake into the pan and leave it alone until the bottom is brown. Then flip once. Stirring makes fried rice, not tacu-tacu.

How it is used

Slide the cake onto a plate for lunch or breakfast. A fried egg on top is a different plate and adds egg. Breaded steak adds meat. The cake here is the rice and beans alone.

Field notes

  • Tacu-tacu is leftover rice and beans fried into a cake, a Lima criollo dish.
  • Afro-Peruvian cooks were part of building that coastal kitchen. The page stays with the dish and that history.
  • Let the cake sit in the pan long enough to crust. Stirring it turns it into fried rice.

Ingredients

  • 3 cups cooked white rice, cold
  • 2 cups cooked canary or black beans, drained
  • 1 small onion, chopped
  • 2 garlic cloves, minced
  • 1 tablespoon ají amarillo paste
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 3 tablespoons neutral oil

Steps

  1. Heat 1 tablespoon oil and cook the onion and garlic until soft. Stir in the ají amarillo paste.
  2. Add the beans and mash about half of them into the onion. Fold in the rice and salt. The mixture should hold together.
  3. Heat the remaining oil in a skillet over medium heat. Add half the mixture and press it into a thick cake.
  4. Cook without stirring until the bottom is brown, then flip and brown the other side. Repeat with the rest.
  5. Serve hot.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Canary beans, black beans, or lentils all appear in Lima kitchens. Keep them cooked and dry.
  • A breaded steak beside the cake is a restaurant plate and adds meat.
  • Leave out the ají and the heat drops to none. The written recipe includes a tablespoon.

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Breakfast
  • A fried egg, which adds egg and means the plate is no longer vegan
  • Salsa criolla of onion and lime
  • Avocado
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

India · Mild · 8 pesarattu

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Pesarattu

A green-gold moong dal pancake, herbal from ginger and green chile, with a soft crisp edge.

25 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 pesarattu

History

Pesarattu is the moong-dal pancake of Andhra cooking. Whole green gram is soaked and ground. Many kitchens cook it after that soak, with no long ferment, or after only a short rest. It is often eaten with ginger chutney, and a famous plate pairs it with upma. The batter is lentil, not the rice-and-urad ferment of a classic dosa, even though both are griddled breakfasts in South India.

Cultural background

Andhra tables treat pesarattu as its own order, especially when upma is tucked inside or served beside it. This recipe is the plain moong pancake. Upma is a pairing, and it is usually wheat semolina, so adding it would change the gluten-free tag.

Why it tastes this way

Green moong tastes grassy and slightly sweet, different from the sour rice dosa beside it on many menus. Ginger and green chile are ground in, so the heat is aromatic and mild. Because the batter is often unfermented, there is little tang. The griddle dries the surface and leaves a legume toast.

How it is made

Soak whole green moong, grind it with ginger and chile, and cook small pancakes on an oiled griddle without waiting for a long ferment.

Authentic touch. Grind the soaked moong until it is smooth but not watery. A batter that is too thin tears. A batter that is pasty never crisps at the rim.

How it is used

Serve with ginger chutney. If you add semolina upma, say so at the table, because that side contains wheat.

Field notes

  • Pesarattu is a moong pancake. It is not a rice-and-urad dosa renamed.
  • Many Andhra cooks grind it and cook it the same morning.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup whole green moong dal
  • 1 inch ginger
  • 1 or 2 green chiles
  • 1/2 teaspoon cumin seeds
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Water for soaking and grinding
  • Neutral oil for the griddle

Steps

  1. Rinse the moong and soak it in water for 3 to 4 hours. Drain.
  2. Grind with ginger, chile, cumin, salt, and just enough fresh water to make a thick, pourable batter. Cook it the same day, or rest it up to an hour. No overnight ferment is required.
  3. Heat a griddle and grease it. Pour a ladle and spread it into a small round, thicker than a paper dosa.
  4. Oil the edges. Cook until the underside is spotted brown and the top looks set. Flip if you want both sides dry.
  5. Repeat with the rest of the batter, stirring between pancakes so the cumin does not sink.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A handful of chopped onion pressed on top
  • A short one-hour rest if you like a little tang

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Ginger chutney
  • Chopped onion
  • A rice upma made without wheat, if you want the stuffed plate and still need it gluten-free
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Italy · No chili heat · 4 flatbreads

Flying Pancakes of the World

Italy · Southern Europe

Piadina

The griddled flatbread of Romagna. It is a flatbread, folded around a filling.

30 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 flatbreads

History

Piadina is the flatbread of Romagna, griddled, folded, and filled. It is a flatbread, not a poured pancake and not a Bolognese pasta dough. The older fat in many Romagna kitchens is lard. This recipe uses olive oil so the bread itself stays vegetarian and dairy-free. Lard is named as a variation and would add a meat allergen. The usual filling, cured pork and a soft fresh cheese such as squacquerone, is put on after cooking. That filling is not vegan. The dough is.

Cultural background

Piadina is street food and home food, cooked on a testo, a flat griddle. It is eaten folded, often standing up. The bread should be pliable and spotted, with a few bubbles.

Why it tastes this way

Hot griddle spots taste toasted. Olive oil makes the crumb short rather than lean. Salt is noticeable because the dough is simple. The filling does the rest: salty cured meat, sharp greens, and mild creamy cheese. Without a filling, the bread still tastes good with olive oil and salt, which is how you can judge the dough.

How it is made

Flour, salt, a pinch of baking soda, olive oil, and water are kneaded briefly, rested, rolled into thin rounds, and cooked on a hot griddle.

Authentic touch. Call it a flatbread. Cook it on a dry or lightly oiled griddle and fold it while warm. If you use lard, say that you have left the vegan dough. Do not put the filling in the dough.

How it is used

Folded around greens and cheese, or around cured meat, as a street meal. The dough recipe is vegan. The classic filled plate usually is not.

Field notes

  • Piadina is a flatbread from Romagna, griddled and folded.
  • It is not a pancake batter and not a pasta dough.
  • This dough uses olive oil. Lard is the older fat in many kitchens and changes the diet.

Ingredients

  • 250 g plain wheat flour
  • 1/2 tsp fine salt
  • 1/4 tsp baking soda
  • 40 ml olive oil
  • 120 ml warm water

Steps

  1. Mix the flour, salt, and baking soda. Add the oil and enough water to make a soft dough. Knead briefly and rest 20 minutes.
  2. Divide into four and roll each piece into a thin round.
  3. Cook on a hot griddle until brown spots appear and the bread puffs in places, then turn.
  4. Fold while warm around the filling you have chosen, or serve plain with oil and salt.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Lard instead of olive oil, the older fat, which is not vegetarian
  • A whole-wheat share of the flour

Pairings and uses

  • Street food
  • Savory meal
  • Squacquerone or another fresh soft cheese, a dairy filling
  • Arugula
  • Cured pork, a meat filling
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Poland · No chili heat · 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Poland · Eastern Europe

Placki ziemniaczane

Polish potato pancakes, shredded and fried, with sugar or sour cream.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 pancakes

History

Placki ziemniaczane are Poland's everyday shredded-potato pancakes. They are fried crisp and eaten with sugar, sour cream, or a savory stew. They use the same raw-potato technique as deruny and as Ashkenazi latkes. The latke page is the Hanukkah history. This page is the Polish home and street pancake, not a holiday claim. Flour and egg bind them. They are fried in oil, so the cake itself is dairy-free. Sour cream is a topping, not an ingredient.

Cultural background

They are sold at counters and made at home, often from a bowl of grated potato that has to be cooked before it turns gray. Sugar on a potato pancake surprises people who expect only sour cream. Both are served in Poland.

Why it tastes this way

The crust is salty, browned starch. Onion makes it savory. Sugar on top is a direct contrast, grainy and sweet against the salt. Sour cream is the softer contrast. The inside should be tender, not raw. Too much flour makes them bready.

How it is made

Potato and onion are grated, mixed with egg, flour, and salt, and fried in oil until crisp.

Authentic touch. Offer sugar and sour cream as two real options, not as a mistake. Keep the cakes thin. This is not the Hanukkah latke page, even though a cook could mistake one pan for the other.

How it is used

Hot, with sugar or with sour cream. A meat goulash beside them is a meal and adds meat to the plate.

Field notes

  • Placki ziemniaczane are the Polish potato pancakes of ordinary days.
  • Sugar and sour cream are both served on them.
  • The Hanukkah latke has its own page and its own history.

Ingredients

  • 600 g starchy potatoes, peeled
  • 1 small onion
  • 1 large egg
  • 2 tbsp plain wheat flour
  • 1 tsp fine salt
  • Neutral oil, for frying
  • Sugar or sour cream, for serving

Steps

  1. Grate the potatoes and onion, squeeze lightly, and mix with the egg, flour, and salt.
  2. Heat a shallow layer of oil over medium-high heat.
  3. Fry thin rounds until both sides are brown and the potato is cooked.
  4. Serve at once with sugar or sour cream.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Sugar only, which keeps the plate dairy-free
  • Latkes, the Ashkenazi Hanukkah page

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Snack
  • Sugar
  • Sour cream
  • A mushroom or meat stew, as a separate choice
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

India · No chili heat · 8 dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Plain dosa

Crisp at the edge, faintly sour, with a toasted rice smell and a soft underside.

40 min prep · 30 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 dosas

History

Dosa is old in South India. The exact origin century is not settled, so this page does not name one. What the kitchens agree on is the batter: raw rice and skinned black gram, soaked, ground, salted, and fermented, then spread on a hot griddle. Tamil Nadu, Karnataka, Andhra Pradesh, Telangana, and Kerala all keep that ferment and change the sides.

Cultural background

A home batter is judged by smell and bubbles. A warm night may finish it by morning. A cool kitchen needs more time near the stove. The sourness should stay gentle. Harsh smell or visible mold means the bowl is discarded.

Why it tastes this way

Rice supplies the starch that dries against iron and turns crisp. Urad dal brings protein and a soft stickiness so the sheet can spread without tearing. Fermentation adds a mild tang and the gas that opens lacy holes at the rim. A few fenugreek seeds, traditional in many batters, leave a faint bitter aroma under the toast.

How it is made

Soak rice and urad separately, grind them to different textures, ferment until the batter is lively, and cook thin rounds on a lightly oiled griddle.

Authentic touch. Spread from the center while the batter still flows. A pan that is too hot tears the sheet. A pan that is too cool glues it down and never crisps.

How it is used

Eat it at once with coconut chutney and sambar. A covered dosa steams itself and loses the crisp edge.

Field notes

  • Skinned black gram is the urad dal in this batter, sometimes sold as white urad.
  • The same fermented batter can be steamed as idli. The griddle is what makes the dosa.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup idli rice or other short-grain raw rice
  • 1/3 cup skinned urad dal (black gram)
  • 1/4 teaspoon fenugreek seeds
  • 1/2 teaspoon fine salt
  • Water for soaking and grinding
  • Neutral oil for the griddle

Steps

  1. Rinse the rice. Rinse the urad with the fenugreek. Soak them in separate bowls for 4 to 6 hours.
  2. Drain and grind the urad with cold water to a light, fluffy paste. Grind the rice with water to a slightly grainy batter. Stir them together with the salt. The mix should thickly coat a spoon.
  3. Cover the bowl and ferment until it is bubbly and mildly sour, often 8 to 14 hours. In a cool room give it longer. This wait is not part of the active prep time. Discard the batter if it smells putrid or grows mold.
  4. Stir the batter down and loosen it with a spoon or two of water if it is too stiff to spread. Heat a griddle until a drop of water skitters. Grease it lightly.
  5. Pour a ladle in the center and spiral outward. Drizzle a little oil around the edge. Cook until the underside is spotted gold and the top looks dry. Fold and serve immediately.

Ferment in a clean bowl. Discard batter that smells rotten, turns an odd color, or shows mold.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Ghee on the rim instead of oil
  • A spoon of cooked rice in the rice grind for a softer crumb

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Coconut chutney
  • Sambar
  • Tomato chutney
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Sri Lanka · Mild · 6 rotis

Flying Pancakes of the World

Sri Lanka · South Asia

Pol roti

A griddled coconut flatbread, lightly sweet from the coconut, with onion and green chile in the dough.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 6 rotis

History

Pol roti is a Sri Lankan coconut roti, griddled rather than baked in a tandoor. Pol means coconut. Scraped coconut is mixed into wheat flour with salt, onion, and often green chile, then patted and cooked on a flat pan. It is everyday breakfast and accompaniment food, especially along the south coast and in home kitchens. It is a flatbread, not a hopper and not a sweet tea-time pancake.

Cultural background

Galle kitchens serve it with lunumiris, with seeni sambol, or beside a curry. The bread itself can be mild. This recipe puts a little green chile in the dough so the heat matches the tag. Coconut here is fresh and unsweetened.

Why it tastes this way

Fresh coconut melts into the dough and tastes sweet and rich against wheat. Onion softens on the griddle. Green chile flickers rather than burns if it is chopped fine. Because the bread is cooked dry with only a film of oil, you taste toasted coconut and flour instead of a fried crust.

How it is made

Mix wheat flour with grated coconut, onion, and green chile, pat thick rounds, and griddle both sides.

Authentic touch. Pat the roti thicker than a chapati. A thin one dries out before the coconut toasts. Medium heat and a patient few minutes are the method.

How it is used

Serve warm, broken by hand, with an onion sambol. It also sits beside a vegetable curry as an accompaniment.

Field notes

  • Pol means coconut in Sinhala. The roti is built around fresh coconut.
  • It is cooked on a griddle, in rounds you pat by hand.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups whole wheat flour or all-purpose wheat flour
  • 1 cup freshly grated coconut
  • 1 small onion, finely chopped
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • 3/4 teaspoon salt
  • About 1/2 cup water
  • Neutral oil for the griddle

Steps

  1. Mix flour, coconut, onion, chile, and salt. Add water gradually until a soft dough forms. Rest 15 minutes.
  2. Divide into six balls. Pat each into a thick round on a floured sheet.
  3. Heat a griddle and film it with oil. Cook a roti on medium until the underside is spotted brown.
  4. Flip and cook until the center is no longer doughy and the coconut smells toasted.
  5. Repeat. Keep the stack covered with a cloth.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A few curry leaves chopped into the dough
  • Shredded carrot for a lunchbox roti

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Onion sambol
  • Seeni sambol
  • A mild dhal curry
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Poland · No chili heat · 12 latkes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Poland · Eastern Europe

Potato latkes

Ashkenazi potato pancakes for Hanukkah, fried in oil, with the older cheese custom named beside them.

25 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 latkes

History

Potato latkes are the Ashkenazi Hanukkah pancake of grated potato, onion, egg, and flour or matzo meal, fried in oil. Potatoes became common in Eastern Europe in the 19th century. Cheese pancakes are the older Hanukkah dairy custom, tied in Ashkenazi teaching to Judith, who in the Book of Judith fed an enemy general salty cheese and wine. That cheese cake has its own page. This one is the potato cake, and it is not gluten-free, because flour or matzo meal is wheat. The pin is Poland. The same cakes are cooked in Ukraine, the United States, and Israel.

Cultural background

Hanukkah food is fried, a reminder of oil in the Temple story. A dairy-free latke fried in oil can sit on a meat meal. Sour cream is a dairy topping and belongs on a dairy meal, not beside meat. Applesauce keeps the plate neutral.

Why it tastes this way

Oil frying makes the crust crisp and savory. Onion sweetens it. The inside stays soft if the shreds were not ground to a paste. Salt and pepper are the seasoning. Applesauce is tart and cool. Because there is no baking powder, the flavor is potato, onion, and browned oil, not a diner pancake.

How it is made

Potato and onion are grated, squeezed, mixed with egg, flour, and salt, and fried in oil until crisp.

Authentic touch. Use flour or matzo meal and say so: this is not gluten-free. Fry in oil, not butter, if the latkes need to stay dairy-free for a meat meal. Keep the starch that settles from the grated potato.

How it is used

Hot at Hanukkah, with applesauce, or with sour cream when the meal is dairy. They are a feast food.

Field notes

  • Cheese latkes are the older Ashkenazi Hanukkah dairy custom. Potato latkes spread after potatoes did.
  • Potatoes became common in Eastern Europe in the 19th century.
  • Flour or matzo meal means these latkes are not gluten-free.

Ingredients

  • 700 g starchy potatoes, peeled
  • 1 onion
  • 1 large egg
  • 3 tbsp plain wheat flour or matzo meal
  • 1 tsp fine salt
  • 1/4 tsp black pepper
  • Neutral oil, for frying
  • Applesauce, for serving

Steps

  1. Grate the potatoes and onion. Collect the liquid, let the starch settle, pour off the water, and return the starch to the potato.
  2. Stir in the egg, flour or matzo meal, salt, and pepper.
  3. Fry flattened spoonfuls in hot oil until both sides are deep golden and the centers are cooked.
  4. Drain on a rack and serve with applesauce, or with sour cream if the meal is dairy.

Fry in enough oil to brown the cakes and do not leave the pan. Drain them so they stay crisp.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • The older farmer-cheese latke, on its own page
  • Matzo meal instead of flour, still wheat

Pairings and uses

  • Feast or fast day
  • Savory meal
  • Applesauce
  • Sour cream, on a dairy meal
  • Salt
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

India · Mild · 6 pudlas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Pudla

A turmeric-yellow Gujarati besan pancake, savory and a little sweet, eaten with chutney.

15 min prep · 18 min cook · Serves 3 · 6 pudlas

History

Pudla is the Gujarati besan pancake that opens a breakfast, often with chutney and a cup of tea. Turmeric is mixed into the gram flour, so the cake arrives yellow. Green chile, cilantro, and sometimes sesame sit in the batter. It is usually unfermented and cooked as soon as the bowl is smooth. A Delhi cheela can share the flour and still be a different plate: this one belongs to a Gujarati morning, not to a tiffin counter seasoned with ajwain.

Cultural background

Ahmedabad homes treat pudla as ordinary breakfast cooking, the way other regions treat a toast. The chutney beside it is part of the habit, not a restaurant garnish. Sesame, when it is used, is toasted by the pan rather than ground into a paste.

Why it tastes this way

Turmeric colors the crumb and adds a light bitter note that Gujarati food often balances with a pinch of sugar. Besan still tastes of chickpea. Cilantro and green chile keep it fresh. A little sugar, common on Gujarati savory tables, rounds the spices without turning the pudla into a dessert.

How it is made

Whisk besan with turmeric, chile, cilantro, and a pinch of sugar, then griddle thin yellow pancakes.

Authentic touch. Cook it fully. Besan that is still raw in the center tastes chalky and bitter in a way turmeric did not intend. Medium heat and an extra minute are the fix.

How it is used

Serve straight from the pan with green chutney. Breakfast is the right hour. A sweeter hando version exists and is a different bowl.

Field notes

  • Pudla is a Gujarati breakfast cake. Turmeric in the batter is part of that habit.
  • It is besan, mixed and cooked the same morning, without a ferment.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup besan (chickpea flour), with no wheat added
  • 3/4 cup water
  • 1/4 teaspoon turmeric
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • 2 tablespoons chopped cilantro
  • 1 teaspoon sesame seeds
  • 1/2 teaspoon sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Neutral oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Whisk besan, turmeric, sugar, salt, and water into a smooth batter. Rest it 10 minutes.
  2. Stir in chile, cilantro, and sesame seeds.
  3. Oil a pan over medium heat. Pour a ladle and spread a thin round.
  4. Cook until the surface looks dry and the underside is speckled deep gold. Flip and cook through so no raw besan remains.
  5. Keep finished pudlas warm under a cloth and send them out with chutney.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Shredded cabbage in the batter
  • Methi leaves when they are in season

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Cilantro-mint chutney
  • Sweet mango pickle
  • Tea
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free
