El Salvador · Mild · 8 pupusas

Flying Pancakes of the World

El Salvador · Mexico & Central America

Pupusas

Griddled masa stuffed with beans and melting cheese, served with tangy curtido and a light tomato salsa.

40 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 pupusas

History

Pupusas are the stuffed masa cakes of El Salvador, cooked on a griddle and served with curtido, a quick pickled cabbage, and a thin tomato salsa. The corn dough belongs to the older masa cooking of the isthmus, which predates wheat hotcakes in the region. Stuffing the round with cheese, beans, or both is the form people eat now. Chicharrón, fried pork, is another everyday filling. It is not in this recipe, and a pupusa made with it is not vegetarian.

Cultural background

Pupusas are dinner, a night-market meal, and Sunday food. In San Salvador they are cooked to order, patted by hand, and slid onto a hot comal. Curtido is made ahead so it has time to sour slightly.

Why it tastes this way

Nixtamalized masa toasts on the outside and stays moist where it meets the filling. Quesillo melts into the beans, salty and stretchy. The curtido is sour from vinegar, sweet from carrot, and bitter-green from cabbage, which cuts the starch. A mild salsa adds acid and a little chile. The combination is the dish, not the masa alone.

How it is made

Make a soft masa dough, seal beans and cheese inside thick rounds, and cook them in a lightly oiled comal until spotted and melted inside. Serve with curtido and salsa.

Authentic touch. Cup the dough in one hand, press a hole, add a small amount of filling, and seal it completely before you pat the round flat. Leaking cheese welds itself to the comal. Oil the comal lightly, unlike a dry tortilla griddle.

How it is used

Serve two pupusas on a plate with a pile of curtido and a spoon of salsa. They are eaten hot, often for dinner or a late snack.

Field notes

  • Curtido is cabbage, carrot, onion, oregano, and vinegar. It is a relish, not a cooked slaw.
  • A pupusa is sealed before it is cooked. A sope is left open and pinched at the rim.
  • This recipe uses cheese and beans. Add pork only if you accept that the pupusa is no longer vegetarian.

Ingredients

  • 250 g masa harina
  • 320 ml warm water
  • 1 teaspoon salt, divided
  • 200 g cooked red beans, mashed and cooled
  • 150 g grated quesillo or another fresh melting cow-milk cheese
  • 2 cups shredded cabbage, 1 carrot, 1/2 onion, 1 teaspoon dried oregano, and 80 ml vinegar for curtido
  • 2 tomatoes, 1 small green chile, and a pinch of salt for salsa
  • 1 tablespoon oil for the comal

Steps

  1. Stir cabbage, grated carrot, sliced onion, oregano, vinegar, and 1/2 teaspoon salt. Set the curtido aside while you cook.
  2. Mix masa harina, the remaining salt, and warm water. Rest the dough, covered, for 15 minutes. Chop the tomatoes and chile into a thin salsa.
  3. Mix the mashed beans with the cheese. Divide the dough into 8 pieces. Cup each piece, add a spoon of filling, seal, and pat into a round about 1 centimeter thick.
  4. Cook on a lightly oiled comal over medium heat for 4 to 5 minutes a side, until brown spots form and the center feels set.
  5. Serve hot with curtido and salsa.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Chicharrón, or beans mixed with chicharrón, is a standard filling. It contains meat and is not vegetarian.
  • Cheese alone, without beans, is the other common vegetarian filling.
  • Squash and cheese is a seasonal filling in some kitchens. The cheese keeps it a dairy dish.

Pairings and uses

  • Street food
  • Savory meal
  • Feast or fast day
  • Curtido heaped on the side
  • A thin tomato salsa
  • Horchata de morro, the Salvadoran seed drink
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Sweden · No chili heat · 8 pancakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Sweden · Northern Europe

Raggmunk

Crisp Swedish potato pancakes from raw grated potato, with lingonberries.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 pancakes

History

Raggmunk is a Swedish potato pancake made with raw grated potato in a batter of flour, egg, and milk. It fries crisp at the edge. The usual plate adds fried pork and lingonberries. This page keeps the pancake vegetarian and names the pork as the traditional accompaniment, not as an ingredient. It is not äggakaka, which is an egg cake, and it is not lefse.

Cultural background

Potato pancakes of this kind are weekday food in Sweden. The raw potato has to be grated just before cooking or it darkens. A crisp raggmunk and a soft one are both defended. This recipe aims for a lacy, crisp edge.

Why it tastes this way

Potato starch browns and crisps. Milk and egg make the middle tender. Salt and white pepper season it. Lingonberry jam is the sour-sweet contrast, the same idea as applesauce beside a German potato pancake, and not the same plate. The wheat flour binds and means this is not gluten-free.

How it is made

Raw potato is grated into a batter of flour, egg, and milk, then fried thin in butter until the edges are brown.

Authentic touch. Grate the potato on a fine side and do not wash all the starch away. Spread the batter thin if you want the crisp version. Serve the jam on the side, not cooked into the batter.

How it is used

Hot, with lingonberry jam. Fried pork is the traditional meat side and is not in this batter.

Field notes

  • Raggmunk uses raw grated potato in a batter, then fries crisp.
  • Lingonberries, and often pork, are the plate. This recipe leaves the pork as a choice.
  • It is not the Scanian äggakaka and not Norwegian lefse.

Ingredients

  • 500 g raw potatoes, peeled
  • 1 large egg
  • 100 ml whole milk
  • 60 g plain wheat flour
  • 1 tsp fine salt
  • 1/4 tsp white pepper
  • 40 g unsalted butter, for frying
  • Lingonberry jam, for serving

Steps

  1. Grate the potatoes. Stir in the egg, milk, flour, salt, and pepper at once so the potato does not sit and darken.
  2. Heat butter in a frying pan over medium-high heat.
  3. Spread thin rounds and fry until the edges are brown and crisp. Turn and cook through.
  4. Serve hot with lingonberry jam.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A thicker, softer cake
  • Onion grated into the batter

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Lingonberry jam
  • Fried pork, for the classic meat plate
  • Salt and white pepper
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

India · Mild · 8 ragi dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Ragi dosa

An earthy, malt-dark finger-millet dosa with a mild chile edge and a crisp skin.

20 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 ragi dosas

History

Ragi dosa is a finger-millet griddle cake of Karnataka and Tamil kitchens, where ragi is an everyday grain. This version mixes ragi flour with a little rice flour and ferments the batter so it belongs with the dosa family. It is gluten-free only when neither flour has wheat added, which is how the ingredients here are written. Instant ragi dosas mixed with yogurt are another home habit. They are a different bowl from this ferment.

Cultural background

Ragi porridge and ragi mudde are the better-known millet dishes. The dosa is the griddle form, useful when cooks want the same grain in a crisp sheet. The color is naturally dark. That darkness is the millet, not burnt batter.

Why it tastes this way

Finger millet tastes earthy and slightly malty, with a bitterness that toasting on the pan turns pleasant. A little rice flour keeps the sheet from crumbling. Fermentation softens the bitterness and adds a small tang. Onion and green chile, if you scatter them on top, brighten a grain that can otherwise taste flat.

How it is made

Stir ragi flour and rice flour into a batter, ferment it until mildly sour, and cook thin seasoned dosas.

Authentic touch. Oil the pan well. Ragi batter sticks more than a rice-and-urad batter. Cook it a shade longer than a pale dosa so the millet flavor rounds out.

How it is used

Serve with coconut chutney. The dark color looks fully cooked even early, so trust the edge: it should lift cleanly.

Field notes

  • Ragi is finger millet, a different grain from rice, wheat, and sorghum.
  • The dosa is gluten-free in this recipe because the flours are ragi and rice, with no wheat.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup ragi flour (finger millet), with no wheat added
  • 1/4 cup rice flour, with no wheat added
  • 1 1/2 cups water
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • 2 tablespoons chopped cilantro
  • Neutral oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Whisk both flours with water and salt into a smooth batter about as thick as crepe batter.
  2. Cover and ferment 8 to 12 hours until mildly sour and a few bubbles show. Active prep is only the mixing. Discard the batter if it spoils.
  3. Stir in chile and cilantro. Add a splash of water if the ferment made it stiff.
  4. Heat a griddle, oil it generously, and spread a thin round.
  5. Cook until the edge lifts and the underside smells toasted. Flip briefly if the center is still wet. Oil the pan again before the next dosa.

Discard the ferment if it smells rotten or grows mold.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Chopped onion and cilantro pressed on top
  • A pinch of cumin in the batter

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Coconut chutney
  • A dry potato palya
  • Plain yogurt on the side, which is dairy and separate from this batter
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

India · Mild · 8 crisp dosas

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Rava dosa

A lacy, cracker-crisp semolina sheet scented with cumin, black pepper, and curry leaf.

20 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 crisp dosas

History

Rava dosa is a semolina batter, usually stirred and cooked without the long rice-and-urad ferment. Wheat semolina, often called rava or sooji, is the grain that builds the crisp web. Rice flour is there to help the sheet set. It became a restaurant standard because a cook can mix it to order when a fermented batter has run out. It is a different dosa from the fermented rice and urad kind.

Cultural background

The batter is meant to be thin and a little lumpy. Those lumps and the water around them are what leave the holes. A thick batter makes a pancake, which is not the point of this page.

Why it tastes this way

Semolina browns faster than a wet fermented rice batter and tastes toasted and wheat-y. Cumin and crushed black pepper sit on the surface instead of disappearing into a ferment. Curry leaf, dropped into hot oil or onto the batter, adds a sharp green note. The lack of a sour ferment is the flavor clue that this is rava dosa.

How it is made

Whisk semolina, rice flour, and water into a thin batter, season it, and pour it loosely onto a hot griddle so it laces.

Authentic touch. Pour the thin batter from the ladle in separate streams and let the holes stay open. Do not spiral it into a solid disk.

How it is used

Lift it off while it is still brittle. Coconut chutney is the usual partner. It softens if it waits in a stack.

Field notes

  • Rava here is wheat semolina, so this dosa is not gluten-free.
  • The lacy holes are a pouring method. They are not a sign of fermentation.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup fine wheat semolina (rava or sooji)
  • 1/3 cup rice flour
  • 1 1/2 cups cold water, plus more if needed
  • 1/2 teaspoon cumin seeds
  • 1/4 teaspoon crushed black pepper
  • 1 green chile, finely chopped
  • 8 curry leaves, torn
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Neutral oil for the griddle

Steps

  1. Whisk semolina, rice flour, salt, and cold water into a thin batter. Rest it 15 minutes so the semolina hydrates. It should stay pourable.
  2. Stir in cumin, pepper, green chile, and curry leaves. Thin with a splash of water if it has thickened.
  3. Heat a griddle until very hot. Grease it lightly and pour the batter in separate ribbons rather than one tight spiral.
  4. Fill obvious gaps with a little more batter, then drizzle oil around the lace. Cook until the sheet is golden and rigid.
  5. Loosen the edge and lift the dosa off. Cook the rest, stirring the batter each time so the spices do not sink.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A few cumin seeds extra on the surface
  • Buttermilk in place of part of the water, which adds dairy

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Snack
  • Coconut chutney
  • Allam-style ginger chutney
  • Sambar
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Malaysia · No chili heat · 6 flatbreads

Flying Pancakes of the World

Malaysia · Southeast Asia

Roti canai

A flaky, laminated wheat flatbread, griddled until the outside shatters and the inside stays stretchy.

30 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 6 flatbreads

History

Roti canai is a Malaysian griddled flatbread made from laminated dough. Oil or ghee is spread through a wheat dough that is stretched, coiled, and cooked on a griddle until it flakes. The bread has Indian-Muslim heritage in Malaysia, carried by cooks and shops rather than by a single named inventor on this page. It is breakfast and late-night food, usually with dal or a curry. Singapore's roti prata is the same family and has its own page about coffee-shop life there. This page is the Malaysian bread.

Cultural background

Mamak stalls clap the dough on the board until it is wide and thin, then crumple it onto the griddle. The clap is skill. At home, rolling and coiling still makes layers if the dough is well oiled and rested. The bread itself is not spicy. The curry beside it may be.

Why it tastes this way

Layers of dough fried in ghee taste rich and wheat-y, with a crisp leaf and a chewy center. Salt is the only seasoning in the bread, so the flavor is fat and toast. A lentil curry or a spicy dal adds the salt and chile the roti does not carry. The lamination is why it tastes different from a single chapati: you get many surfaces of fried dough in one piece.

How it is made

Laminate a wheat dough with ghee, stretch and coil it, and griddle it until flaky.

Authentic touch. Oil the dough generously and rest it. A dry dough will not stretch into layers. Cook it on a moderately hot griddle and press it so the coil makes contact.

How it is used

Serve hot with dal or a chicken curry. The chicken curry would add meat. A lentil dal keeps the meal vegetarian. Tear the bread and dip it.

Field notes

  • Roti canai is laminated dough, stretched and griddled, not a poured batter.
  • Its heritage in Malaysia is Indian-Muslim cooking, and this page does not name one inventor.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups all-purpose wheat flour
  • 3/4 teaspoon salt
  • 2/3 cup water
  • 3 tablespoons ghee or neutral oil, plus more for cooking
  • 1 teaspoon sugar

Steps

  1. Mix flour, salt, sugar, water, and a tablespoon of ghee into a soft dough. Knead until smooth.
  2. Divide into six balls. Coat each with ghee. Rest at least 1 hour. That rest is not active prep.
  3. Stretch or roll one ball thin, spread a little ghee, and fold or coil it into a layered round.
  4. Griddle on medium heat with a film of ghee, pressing, until both sides are flaky and spotted brown.
  5. Repeat. Serve at once with dal.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Roti telur, with an egg cracked on the griddle, which adds egg
  • A banana and sugar version

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Street food
  • Lentil dal
  • Sugar on a piece, for a sweet bite
  • A fish curry, which would add fish
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Russia · No chili heat · 12 blini

Flying Pancakes of the World

Russia · Eastern Europe

Russian blini

Yeasted wheat blini for Maslenitsa, butter week, with sour cream and butter.

1 hr 40 min prep · 30 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 blini

History

These are yeasted wheat blini from Russia. Maslenitsa is butter week, the week before Lent, not a single Tuesday. Blini are cooked through that week and also outside it. Buckwheat blini are traditional too and have their own page. This page is the wheat batter, the one most tables mean when they pile on butter and sour cream. A popular image treats the round blin as a sun at the end of winter. That is a popular reading. The kitchen facts are yeast, butter, and a week of cooking.

Cultural background

Maslenitsa food is rich in butter and dairy before the fast. Blini are breakfast, snack, and feast food in that week. Caviar and smoked fish are festive toppings. Sour cream and butter are the everyday ones, and they are what this recipe requires.

Why it tastes this way

Yeast gives a mild sourness and a softer crumb than an unleavened crepe. Butter in the pan and on the cake is the point of butter week. Sour cream adds tang and fat. The cakes are a little thicker than a French crepe and full of small holes. Sugar in the batter is only a pinch, so the topping does the sweetening if you want it.

How it is made

A yeast batter of wheat flour, milk, egg, and butter rises until bubbly, then is cooked in a buttered pan and stacked with more butter.

Authentic touch. Cook them through butter week if you are keeping Maslenitsa, and do not call that week a Tuesday. Brush each blin with butter as it comes off the pan. Keep them covered so they steam and stay flexible.

How it is used

Hot, with butter and sour cream. Jam, herring, or smoked fish are toppings. Fish would add a fish allergen and is not in this batter.

Field notes

  • Maslenitsa is butter week before Lent, not one Tuesday.
  • Wheat blini and buckwheat blini both exist. This page is the wheat yeast batter.
  • The sun story is a popular reading. The recipe is yeast and butter.

Ingredients

  • 250 g plain wheat flour
  • 1 tsp instant yeast
  • 1 tsp sugar
  • 300 ml warm milk
  • 1 large egg
  • 1/2 tsp fine salt
  • 40 g unsalted butter, melted, plus more for the pan and for brushing
  • Sour cream, for serving

Steps

  1. Stir the flour, yeast, sugar, warm milk, egg, salt, and melted butter into a batter like cream. Cover until bubbly and risen, about 1 hour.
  2. Stir it down. Add a splash of milk if it is too thick to spread.
  3. Cook small rounds in a buttered pan until the top is set and full of holes, then turn and brown the other side.
  4. Brush each blin with butter and stack them. Serve with sour cream.

The batter should smell yeasty. Discard it if it smells spoiled.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of buckwheat in the batter, still not the buckwheat page
  • A thin unleavened blinchik, which is the filled crepe page

Pairings and uses

  • Feast or fast day
  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Melted butter
  • Sour cream
  • Jam, or herring for a fish plate
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Saudi Arabia · Mild · 4 folded breads

Flying Pancakes of the World

Saudi Arabia · Middle East

Saudi mutabbaq

A thin, pan-fried stuffed flatbread with a savory leek and egg center and a mild chile edge.

40 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 folded breads

History

Mutabbaq is a stuffed flatbread of the Hijaz, sold in Jeddah and other Saudi cities and also known as mutabbaq, a name that means folded. The dough is stretched thin, wrapped around a filling, and pan-fried until blistered. Leek and egg are one home and stall filling. Minced meat is another. A related folded bread is called murtabak in parts of Southeast Asia. This page is the Jeddah street bread, written as a stuffed flatbread, not as a poured pancake.

Cultural background

Mutabbaq is night and late-afternoon street food as much as a home pan. The stretch of the dough is the skill: thin enough to blister, strong enough to hold egg. The mild chile in this filling is a warm note, not a hot sauce bath.

Why it tastes this way

The wrapper tastes of toasted wheat and frying oil, with crisp bubbles where the dough was thin. Leek goes sweet in the pan. Egg sets into a savory custard. Cumin and a little chile make the heat mild and the aroma warm. Because the dough is folded, some bites are all crust and some are mostly filling.

How it is made

Rest a soft, oiled dough. Cook leek with cumin and a mild chile, then stir in beaten egg just until set. Stretch the dough, fold the filling inside an envelope, and pan-fry it in a film of oil until both sides blister.

Authentic touch. Stretch the dough thin and fold it around the filling before it goes into the pan. It is a stuffed flatbread, also known as mutabbaq. A meat filling is a common stall choice and is not the vegetarian recipe written here.

How it is used

Mutabbaq is street food and a savory snack, eaten hot, sometimes with a lime wedge or a simple salad. It is not a dessert. The meat version should be ordered as meat, not assumed from this recipe.

Field notes

  • Mutabbaq means folded, and the bread is also known by that same name.
  • This recipe is a stuffed flatbread, pan-fried, not a batter pancake.
  • The Jeddah filling here is leek and egg, with a mild chile.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups wheat flour (about 250 g)
  • 3/4 teaspoon fine salt, divided
  • 3/4 cup water
  • 3 tablespoons oil, plus more for the pan
  • 2 leeks, sliced and washed
  • 1 mild green chile, chopped
  • 1/2 teaspoon ground cumin
  • 2 eggs, beaten

Steps

  1. Mix the flour, half the salt, the water, and a spoon of oil into a soft dough. Rest it for 20 minutes.
  2. Cook the leeks, chile, cumin, and remaining salt in a spoon of oil until the leeks are soft. Pour in the eggs and stir until just set. Cool the filling. The chile is mild.
  3. Divide the dough into 4. Oil the board and stretch each piece thin. This is a stuffed flatbread, also called mutabbaq.
  4. Spoon filling into the center and fold the dough into an envelope, sealing the edges.
  5. Pan-fry in a film of oil, turning once, until both sides are blistered and golden and the dough is cooked through.

The dough contains wheat and the filling contains egg. Cook the egg in the filling until it is set. The pan oil is hot. Heat from the chile is mild.

Refrigerate leftover mutabbaq and reheat it in a hot pan. Do not leave the egg filling at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Minced meat replaces the egg and leek in many stalls and is not vegetarian.
  • A greener filling uses more leek and less chile, which softens the mild heat further.
  • The Southeast Asian murtabak is a related folded bread, not this Hijazi batch.

Pairings and uses

  • Street food
  • Savory meal
  • Snack
  • Lime wedges
  • A chopped salad
  • Black tea
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

India · No chili heat · 8 thick dosas, about 3 sets

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Set dosa

Thick, soft, and mildly sour, with a spongy face and a pale golden underside.

40 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 3 · 8 thick dosas, about 3 sets

History

Set dosa is a Karnataka breakfast dosa made from fermented rice and urad, cooked thick and served in a set, usually two or three cakes on one plate. The name comes from that serving, not from a new grain. A little flattened rice in the grind is a common way to keep the crumb soft. Hotel kitchens in Bengaluru and Mysuru plate it with a potato palya and chutney, and homes do the same with a smaller pan.

Cultural background

The batter is close to an idli batter, sometimes a touch looser. Cooks cover the pan so steam finishes the top. Leaving it uncovered and thin would turn it into an ordinary crisp dosa, which misses the set.

Why it tastes this way

A thicker pool of fermented batter keeps moisture in the center, so the sourness reads as soft and bready instead of as a toasted edge. Flattened rice lightens the crumb. Oil on the underside adds a little browning, but the cake is not chased to a cracker. The mild ferment is the main flavor.

How it is made

Ferment a soft rice and urad batter, pour small thick rounds, cover them, and serve two or three together.

Authentic touch. Cook on medium-low and cover the pan. If you spread the batter thin, you have left set dosa behind.

How it is used

Bring the set to the table stacked, with potato palya and coconut chutney. They are eaten with the hands, torn, not cut like a cake.

Field notes

  • A set is the portion: two or three thick dosas, not a different cereal.
  • Covering the pan is what keeps the center spongy.

Ingredients

  • 3/4 cup idli rice
  • 1/4 cup skinned urad dal
  • 2 tablespoons flattened rice (poha)
  • 1/8 teaspoon fenugreek seeds
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Water for soaking and grinding
  • Neutral oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Rinse and soak the rice in one bowl. Soak the urad, fenugreek, and flattened rice in another for 4 to 5 hours.
  2. Grind the urad mixture to a fluffy paste and the rice to a fine batter. Combine with salt. The batter should be thick, like a soft idli batter.
  3. Ferment covered until risen and mildly sour, often 8 to 12 hours. Do not count the ferment as active prep. Discard it if it spoils.
  4. Stir gently. Heat a pan on medium-low, grease it, and pour a small ladle without spreading it to the rim. The round should stay about a finger thick.
  5. Cover and cook until the top is set and the underside is pale gold. Repeat. Serve three cakes as one set.

Discard fermented batter that smells rotten or shows mold.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A pinch of sugar in the batter, common in some Karnataka homes
  • Ghee on the griddle, which adds dairy

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Potato palya
  • Coconut chutney
  • A thin vegetable sagu
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

China · No chili heat · 4 crisp crepes

Flying Pancakes of the World

China · East Asia

Shandong jianbing

A very thin, cracker-crisp crepe, torn into pieces and eaten with scallion and a savory paste.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 crisp crepes

History

Shandong jianbing is a crisp street crepe from Shandong, and it is a different food from the Tianjin jianbing that wraps a youtiao. This one is often cracker-crisp. Cooks tear it into pieces. It is not always wrapped around a fried dough stick. The batter is thin, sometimes with millet or other coarse grain beside wheat, and it is cooked until it dries and blisters. Jinan is a fair place to meet it. The two jianbing share a family name and not a single assembly.

Cultural background

A brittle sheet can be broken over a plate of condiments or folded loosely. Treating it like the soft Tianjin wrap makes it crack in the wrong way and misses the texture Shandong stalls chase. Egg is common. A youtiao is not the defining center of this page.

Why it tastes this way

Cooking the batter dry concentrates a toasted wheat flavor and leaves almost no soft crumb. Millet, if you include it, adds a faint bitterness and a coarser bite. Scallion and fermented bean paste supply the salt and the green note the crisp sheet lacks. Because there is no juicy fritter inside, the taste is cleaner and more cracker-like than a Tianjin breakfast.

How it is made

Cook an extra-thin wheat and millet batter until it is brittle, then tear it and eat it with scallion and bean paste.

Authentic touch. Spread the batter thinner than a crepe you mean to fold, and leave it on the heat until it snaps. If it bends like cloth, it needs another minute.

How it is used

Break the sheet at the griddle and eat the pieces with the condiments. It is a street breakfast, not a wrapped sandwich.

Field notes

  • Shandong jianbing is often cracker-crisp and torn, not wrapped around a youtiao.
  • Tianjin's egg-and-fritter jianbing is the other famous street version.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup all-purpose wheat flour
  • 2 tablespoons millet flour
  • 3/4 cup water
  • 1 egg
  • 1/4 teaspoon salt
  • 2 scallions, chopped
  • 2 tablespoons fermented bean sauce
  • Neutral oil

Steps

  1. Whisk both flours, salt, and water into a very thin batter.
  2. Oil a griddle well and heat it until hot. Pour a small ladle and spread it as thin as you can.
  3. Break the egg onto the sheet if you want it richer, and spread it into a film.
  4. Cook without folding until the crepe is dry, blistered, and brittle enough to tear.
  5. Tear it into pieces. Eat them with scallion and fermented bean sauce.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • All wheat flour if you have no millet
  • An egg spread on the sheet before it dries

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Street food
  • Savory meal
  • Fermented bean paste
  • Chopped scallion
  • A spoon of chile oil if you want heat
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Singapore · No chili heat · 6 pratas

Flying Pancakes of the World

Singapore · Southeast Asia

Singaporean roti prata

A flaky laminated flatbread from a Singapore coffee shop, crisp at the edge and chewy inside, served with curry.

30 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 6 pratas

History

Roti prata is Singapore's coffee-shop flatbread, cooked to order on a griddle and flipped until the layers separate. The dough is laminated with ghee or oil, the same family as Malaysian roti canai, and this page keeps the Singapore plate: a morning or late supper in a coffee shop, torn into curry gravy. Indian-Muslim cooks built that trade in Singapore as well as across the causeway. The pages stay separate so the coffee-shop habit is not collapsed into the Malaysian one. Tissue prata, stretched paper-thin and dusted with sugar, is a sweet variation.

Cultural background

In a Singapore coffee shop the prata arrives with a small bowl of curry and, often, a drink. The savory bread is the breakfast. A tissue prata is ordered when someone wants sugar instead of gravy. Home cooks can coil an oiled dough and griddle it. The flake still depends on fat between the layers.

Why it tastes this way

Ghee and wheat, fried in thin layers, taste toasted and rich. The interior stretches. Salt in the dough is plain, so the curry provides spice, coconut, and salt. A tissue prata tastes of sugar and crisp dough instead. The coffee-shop setting does not change the chemistry. It changes the meal around the bread: curry at breakfast, and the option of a sweet paper-thin cousin from the same ball of dough.

How it is made

Laminate a wheat dough with ghee, griddle it until flaky, and serve it with a curry gravy in the coffee-shop way.

Authentic touch. Stretch the dough thin and do not be afraid of an uneven edge. Those thin flaps are what crisp. Rest the oiled dough long enough that it relaxes, or it will snap back into a bun.

How it is used

Tear it and dip it in curry for a savory breakfast. For a sweet order, skip the curry and dust a thinner bread with sugar.

Field notes

  • Singapore roti prata is the same laminated-dough family as Malaysian roti canai, eaten here as coffee-shop food.
  • Tissue prata is the sweet, very thin version from the same dough.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups all-purpose wheat flour
  • 3/4 teaspoon salt
  • 1 teaspoon sugar
  • 2/3 cup water
  • 3 tablespoons ghee, plus more for the griddle

Steps

  1. Mix flour, salt, sugar, water, and a spoon of ghee into a soft dough. Knead until smooth.
  2. Divide into six pieces, coat them in ghee, and rest at least 1 hour. The rest is not active prep.
  3. Stretch one piece thin, spread a little ghee, and fold or coil it into layers.
  4. Griddle with ghee over medium heat, pressing and flipping, until both sides are spotted and flaky.
  5. Repeat and serve with curry, or stretch one piece thinner, cook it crisp, and dust it with sugar.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Tissue prata dusted with sugar
  • An egg fried onto the prata, which adds egg

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Street food
  • A mild potato and lentil curry
  • Sugar for a tissue-style piece
  • Coffee or tea
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Mexico · Mild · 8 sopes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Mexico · Mexico & Central America

Sopes

A small thick masa base with a raised rim, topped with beans, cheese, onion, and salsa.

30 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 sopes

History

Sopes are small, thick masa cakes. While the dough is still soft from the first side on the comal, the cook pinches a rim so the toppings stay on. They are not gorditas. A gordita is split into a pocket. A sope is an open boat. They are not tlacoyos either, which are oval and filled before cooking. The dough is nixtamalized masa, part of the corn cooking that is older in Mexico than wheat hotcakes.

Cultural background

Sopes are market and home antojitos. Toluca and Mexico City both sell them with beans, salsa, onion, and cheese. The rim is the defining move, and it has to be pinched before the dough dries out.

Why it tastes this way

The base is thick enough to taste of steamed corn in the center and toasted masa at the bottom. Refried beans add salt and body. Fresh cheese is milky and a little sour. Raw onion and a mild tomato salsa cut the starch. Because the toppings stay on the surface, you taste them separately from the corn instead of as a hidden filling.

How it is made

Pat small thick disks of masa, griddle one side, pinch a rim, cook through, and top with warm beans, cheese, onion, and salsa.

Authentic touch. After the first side sets, flip the sope and pinch the rim quickly while the masa is flexible. If you wait until it is crisp, the edge cracks instead of rising into a wall.

How it is used

Serve two or three sopes as a snack or a light meal. Eat them with your hands once the cheese softens from the heat of the masa.

Field notes

  • The pinched rim is made with your fingers, not with a mold, while the dough is half cooked.
  • A sope is open on top. A gordita is split. They start from similar masa and become different cakes.
  • Plain cornmeal will not hold a rim the way nixtamalized masa does.

Ingredients

  • 250 g masa harina
  • 300 ml warm water
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • 250 g refried beans, warmed
  • 80 g crumbled fresh white cheese
  • 1 small onion, chopped
  • 1 cup mild tomato and jalapeño salsa

Steps

  1. Mix masa harina, salt, and warm water. Knead, cover, and rest 20 minutes.
  2. Divide into 8 pieces. Pat each into a thick disk about 8 centimeters across.
  3. Cook on a dry comal over medium heat for 2 minutes. Flip. While the top is soft, pinch up a rim all the way around.
  4. Cook another 3 to 4 minutes, turning once, until the base is spotted and the center is set.
  5. Spread on warm refried beans. Add cheese, onion, and salsa. Serve hot.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Potatoes and chorizo are a common topping. Chorizo adds meat and is not vegetarian.
  • Leave the cheese off and the sopes can be vegan and dairy-free.
  • A wider sope is sometimes called a picadita or a huarache when the shape changes. This recipe stays small and round.

Pairings and uses

  • Street food
  • Savory meal
  • Snack
  • Shredded lettuce for a colder crunch
  • Radishes and lime
  • Agua fresca
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Spain · No chili heat · 1 omelet, 8 wedges

Flying Pancakes of the World

Spain · Southern Europe

Spanish tortilla de patatas

A potato omelet. It is not a pancake. This page says so and still cooks the omelet.

15 min prep · 40 min cook · Serves 8 · 1 omelet, 8 wedges

History

Spanish tortilla de patatas is a potato omelet, cooked in olive oil and served in wedges. It is not a pancake. It is on this journal because the word tortilla is constantly confused with tortillas and with tortitas, and because the dish is a real Spanish egg cake. Whether it should contain onion is a genuine disagreement. This recipe uses onion. Leaving the onion out is the other defended style, not a failed tortilla. There is no flour and no dairy.

Cultural background

Tortilla is tapas, picnic food, and a meal. It is cooked slowly so the potato becomes soft in olive oil, then set with egg. The center may be just firm or slightly damp. Both are served. This recipe sets the center.

Why it tastes this way

Potato that has stewed in olive oil tastes sweet and rich, not fried like a chip. Egg binds it into a custard. Onion, when you use it, adds sweetness. Salt is essential. The outside is only lightly browned. It does not taste like a crepe, because there is no milk batter and no browned flour.

How it is made

Potatoes and onion are simmered in plenty of olive oil until tender, drained, folded into beaten eggs, and set in a frying pan into a round omelet.

Authentic touch. Say, before you cook, that this is an omelet. Use plenty of olive oil and cook the potatoes gently. Onion is included. The version without onion is a real tortilla, not a correction of this one.

How it is used

Warm or at room temperature, in wedges, as a tapa or a meal. It is not a breakfast pancake stack.

Field notes

  • Tortilla de patatas is an omelet, not a pancake.
  • Onion or no onion is a real disagreement. This recipe uses onion.
  • There is no flour in the omelet.

Ingredients

  • 800 g waxy potatoes, peeled and sliced thinly
  • 1 onion, thinly sliced
  • 300 ml olive oil, for cooking the potatoes
  • 6 large eggs
  • 1 tsp fine salt

Steps

  1. Put the potatoes and onion in a pan, cover with the olive oil, and cook gently until the potatoes are tender and not browned. This is a confit, not a fry. Drain them and save the oil.
  2. Beat the eggs with the salt. Fold in the warm potatoes and onion. Rest 5 minutes.
  3. Heat 2 tablespoons of the saved oil in a nonstick pan. Add the egg mixture and cook over medium-low heat until the bottom is set and the top is almost set.
  4. Invert onto a plate, slide it back, and cook until the center is set. Rest 5 minutes and cut in wedges.

The potatoes cook in a deep layer of oil. Stay with the pan. Strain leftover oil only if it is not burnt, and do not pour hot oil down a drain.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • No onion, the other defended style
  • A few roasted red peppers folded in

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Snack
  • Bread, which adds wheat if you eat it
  • A green salad
  • Aioli, which adds garlic and more egg
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Netherlands · No chili heat · 1 thick pancake, 4 wedges

Flying Pancakes of the World

Netherlands · Western Europe

Spekdik

A thick Groningen buckwheat pancake with bacon in the batter, served with syrup.

15 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 1 thick pancake, 4 wedges

History

Spekdik is a buckwheat pancake from Groningen with diced bacon cooked in the batter. It is winter food in that province, especially around the turn of the year. It is not spekkoek. Spekkoek is a spiced layer cake tied to Dutch-Indonesian cooking and does not belong on this page. The batter here is buckwheat, milk, and egg, so it is gluten-free only if the buckwheat was milled apart from wheat. The bacon makes it a meat dish.

Cultural background

Sweet syrup on a bacon pancake is the Groningen combination. The cake is thick, closer to a hearth bread than to a French crepe. It is cut in wedges.

Why it tastes this way

Buckwheat is earthy. Bacon fat seasons the crumb and crisps at the edge. Syrup cuts the salt. Milk and egg make the center soft. If the cake is thin, you have made a different pancake. Spekdik should slice.

How it is made

Bacon is rendered in a pan. A thick buckwheat batter is poured over it and cooked slowly, covered, then turned, and served with syrup.

Authentic touch. Dice the bacon small and cook it in the pan until the fat runs, then pour the batter over it so the meat stays in the cake. Serve with dark syrup.

How it is used

In wedges, as a winter or New Year plate in the Groningen style, with syrup. It is savory and rich.

Field notes

  • Spekdik is a Groningen bacon buckwheat pancake. It is not the layered spice cake called spekkoek.
  • Syrup on a bacon pancake is the local sweet-salty plate.
  • It is associated with the dark months and New Year in Groningen.

Ingredients

  • 150 g diced smoked bacon
  • 200 g buckwheat flour
  • 2 large eggs
  • 400 ml milk
  • 1/2 tsp fine salt
  • Dark syrup, for serving

Steps

  1. Whisk the buckwheat flour, eggs, milk, and salt into a thick batter.
  2. Fry the bacon in a wide ovenproof pan until the fat renders and the pieces start to brown.
  3. Pour the batter over the bacon, spread it, and cook over medium-low heat, covered, until the top is almost set.
  4. Slide it onto a plate, return it to the pan raw side down, and finish until cooked through. Serve with syrup.

The center must be set, not raw batter. Covering the pan helps it cook through.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A meat-free buckwheat pancake, which is no longer spekdik
  • Extra bacon on the side

Pairings and uses

  • Feast or fast day
  • Savory meal
  • Dark syrup
  • Coffee
  • A pickled gherkin if you want something sharp
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Sri Lanka · No chili heat · 8 hoppers

Flying Pancakes of the World

Sri Lanka · South Asia

Sri Lankan hoppers

A crisp-edged rice bowl with a soft coconut center and a light ferment tang.

35 min prep · 30 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 hoppers

History

Hoppers are Sri Lanka's bowl-shaped rice cakes, kin to Kerala appam and still their own daily bread. Fermented rice and coconut milk are poured into a small curved pan, swirled, and covered. Coconut toddy is a traditional ferment. Yeast is the home substitute in this recipe. A hopper plate in Colombo might come with lunumiris or seeni sambol. Egg hoppers, with an egg broken into the center, are a separate page.

Cultural background

Hopper shops pour from a bowl of batter that has worked overnight. The edge laces where it is thin, and the middle stays pale and spongy. String hoppers, which are pressed rice noodles, are a different food and not this pan.

Why it tastes this way

Coconut milk sweetens the rice and keeps the center custardy. Fermentation adds a clean sour note that toddy makes fruitier and yeast makes milder. The hot curved wall fries the thin film into a cracker while the covered center steams. Salt is spare. The sambol beside the hopper carries chile and, often, dried fish.

How it is made

Ferment rice ground with coconut milk, swirl it in a hot hopper pan, and cover until the bowl sets.

Authentic touch. Swirl once and cover. If you leave the batter pooled without climbing the sides, you have a thick pancake, not a hopper.

How it is used

Serve immediately with onion sambol. Some sambols include dried Maldive fish, which would add a fish allergen. The hopper batter here does not.

Field notes

  • Sri Lankan hoppers and Kerala appam share a pan shape and keep different names and tables.
  • An egg cracked into the center is an egg hopper, written on its own page.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup raw rice
  • 1/4 cup cooked rice
  • 3/4 cup coconut milk
  • 1/4 teaspoon dry yeast
  • 1 teaspoon sugar
  • 1/2 teaspoon salt
  • Water as needed
  • Neutral oil for the pan

Steps

  1. Soak the raw rice for 3 hours, drain, and grind it with the cooked rice and a little water until smooth.
  2. Stir in coconut milk, yeast, sugar, and salt. Aim for a batter that pours in a ribbon.
  3. Ferment covered until bubbly, often 6 to 12 hours. Exclude that rest from active prep.
  4. Heat a hopper pan, grease it, pour a ladle, and swirl so the sides film and the center stays thick.
  5. Cover and cook on medium-low until the rim is crisp and the center is spongy and set. Repeat.

Discard batter that smells rotten or grows mold.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Toddy in place of yeast where it is sold fresh and safe
  • A sweeter center with an extra spoon of coconut milk

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Onion sambol without dried fish
  • Seeni sambol
  • A mild coconut gravy
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United Kingdom · No chili heat · 8 large oatcakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

Staffordshire oatcakes

Large, soft, yeasted oatmeal pancakes from the Potteries, rolled around a savory filling.

1 hr 20 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 large oatcakes

History

Staffordshire oatcakes are yeasted oatmeal pancakes from the Potteries, the towns around Stoke-on-Trent. They are large, thin, and flexible, sold in oatcake shops and rolled around bacon, cheese, or sausage. They are not the dry Scottish oatcake, and they are not the Derbyshire batter on the next page. Both counties use yeast and oats. The Staffordshire cake is oat-heavy and meant to fold.

Cultural background

An oatcake shop batter is a local product. Home cooks keep a jug of it warm so the yeast stays alive. The cake is breakfast and lunch food, eaten hot.

Why it tastes this way

Oatmeal makes the crumb soft and a little sweet, and the yeast adds a mild sour note. The griddle dries the surface without turning it into a biscuit. A salty filling, cheese or bacon, is the usual contrast. This recipe leaves the filling for the plate so the batter stays meat-free.

How it is made

Fine oatmeal and a smaller share of wheat flour are mixed with yeast and warm milk, left until bubbly, then poured as large thin rounds on a greased bakestone or frying pan.

Authentic touch. Cook them large and thin enough to roll. A cake that cracks when folded needs a looser batter or a slightly hotter pan and a shorter cook.

How it is used

Hot, rolled or folded with cheese. Bacon or sausage is the shop-counter filling and adds meat. The batter itself is vegetarian.

Field notes

  • Potteries oatcakes are soft and rolled. Scottish oatcakes are crisp and dry.
  • Oatcake shops in Stoke-on-Trent still sell them as everyday food.
  • The batter is yeasted. It is not a baking-powder pancake.

Ingredients

  • 200 g fine oatmeal
  • 80 g plain wheat flour
  • 1 tsp instant yeast
  • 1 tsp sugar
  • 450 ml warm milk and water, mixed half and half
  • 1/2 tsp fine salt
  • Oil or butter, for the pan
  • 100 g cheddar, for serving

Steps

  1. Stir the oatmeal, flour, yeast, sugar, salt, and warm liquid into a batter like thin cream. Cover and leave until foamy, about 1 hour.
  2. Heat a wide pan or griddle and grease it lightly.
  3. Pour a thin round and cook until the top sets and the underside is pale gold. Turn once and cook through.
  4. Stack the cakes in a cloth so they stay flexible. Fill with cheese, roll, and eat hot.

Keep the rising batter covered. Discard it if it smells rotten.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Derbyshire oatcakes, a related but thicker local batter
  • A sweet roll with butter and honey, less typical than the savory shop cake

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Melted cheddar
  • Bacon or a pork sausage, if you eat meat
  • A grilled tomato
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Sudan · No chili heat · About 12 thin sheets

Flying Pancakes of the World

Sudan · East Africa

Sudanese kisra

Thin, tangy sorghum sheets with a flexible, faintly bitter grain taste.

20 min prep · 30 min cook · Serves 6 · About 12 thin sheets

History

Kisra is the fermented sorghum flatbread of Sudan, cooked in sheets thin enough to fold. The grain is sorghum, dura in the local market, mixed with water and soured before it ever meets the pan. The same bread is part of the daily table in South Sudan as well. It is an accompaniment to mullah, the stews, including okra. This recipe is 100 percent sorghum, so it is gluten-free if the flour is uncontaminated.

Cultural background

Kisra is torn or folded and used to pinch stew. A meal can be several sheets beside a bowl of bamia, okra, or another mullah. The ferment is ordinary home practice, not a special-occasion loaf. Cooks who have an older batter use a spoon of it to start the next one.

Why it tastes this way

Sorghum has a grainy, slightly tannic taste that wheat does not. Fermentation adds a clean sourness and loosens the batter so it can be spread paper thin. The hot plate dries the surface just enough to peel the sheet off while the inside stays flexible. No sweetener is part of the bread.

How it is made

Stir sorghum flour into water and ferment it until sour, salting only at the end. On a hot flat griddle, spread a ladle of batter in a quick thin sweep and cook it on one side until it peels free. Fold the sheets and keep them covered so they stay pliable for scooping stew.

Authentic touch. Spread the batter thinner than you think you should. Kisra is a sheet, not a plump pancake. Keep the flour sorghum only if you want the gluten-free version written here.

How it is used

Kisra is the utensil for Sudanese stews. Okra mullah and other vegetable or meat stews sit beside a stack of folded sheets. The bread on this page has no meat in it. South Sudanese tables use the same kind of sheet.

Field notes

  • Kisra is fermented sorghum, cooked in thin sheets rather than fluffy rounds.
  • The bread is also a staple in South Sudan, which is why that country is listed here.
  • One hundred percent sorghum kisra is gluten-free if the flour was not cut with wheat.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups sorghum flour (about 240 g)
  • 3 cups water, plus more for thinning
  • 1/2 teaspoon fine salt
  • 1 teaspoon neutral oil for the griddle, if it sticks

Steps

  1. Whisk the sorghum flour and water into a thin batter with no lumps. Cover the bowl. Do not add wheat.
  2. Leave it at room temperature for one to two days, until it smells sour and small bubbles hold on the surface. Stir once if it separates.
  3. Stir in the salt and thin the batter until it spreads under a quick push of the ladle.
  4. Heat a flat griddle or a large skillet until a drop of water dances. Wipe on a trace of oil only if needed. Pour a ladle of batter and sweep it into a thin sheet.
  5. Cook on one side until the sheet releases and the top looks dry. Fold it onto a plate and cover it. Repeat with the remaining batter.

Gluten-free only when the sorghum flour has no wheat in it. Taste the stew you serve alongside if you are watching chile.

Fold cooled kisra in a clean cloth, then refrigerate. Reheat it covered so it bends instead of cracking. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of old batter starts the ferment faster.
  • Some cooks add a little wheat. That version is not gluten-free and is not this recipe.
  • A slightly thicker sheet is easier for a beginner and less like market kisra.

Pairings and uses

  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Savory meal
  • Okra stew, bamia
  • A dried-okra mullah
  • A bowl of stewed vegetables
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Switzerland · No chili heat · 1 large cake, 4 wedges

Flying Pancakes of the World

Switzerland · Western Europe

Swiss rösti

A shredded potato cake from Switzerland, pan-fried as one round. Not a batter pancake.

40 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 1 large cake, 4 wedges

History

Rösti is a Swiss shredded-potato cake, fried as a round and served whole or in wedges. It is not a batter pancake. There is no flour and no egg in this recipe. The potatoes are parboiled, chilled, grated, and fried in butter. Bern's version often includes bacon. This page writes the plain cake and names bacon as a variation, so the main recipe stays vegetarian. The word Röstigraben is a common name for the cultural line between German-speaking and French-speaking Switzerland, taken from this potato cake.

Cultural background

Rösti is a meal beside fried eggs or a supplement beside meat and cheese. It is everyday food in German-speaking cantons, not a festival-only dish. The crust is the point.

Why it tastes this way

Parboiled potato gratings brown into a crust while the inside steams and softens. Butter tastes nutty when it browns. Salt is the seasoning. There is no egg to make it cakey and no flour to make it bready. A raw-potato rösti exists in some kitchens. This page uses the chilled, parboiled method because it shreds cleanly and crisps.

How it is made

Waxy potatoes are boiled in their skins, chilled, peeled, grated, seasoned, and fried in butter as one cake until a deep crust forms on both sides.

Authentic touch. Say plainly that this is a potato cake, not a poured pancake. Press the shreds into one round and leave them alone long enough to crust before you turn the cake. Bacon is the Bern variation, not a requirement.

How it is used

As a savory meal or a side, with eggs, cheese, or a salad. Bacon on top is the Bern-style variation and adds meat.

Field notes

  • Rösti is shredded potato fried as a cake. It is not a batter pancake.
  • This recipe has no flour and no egg.
  • Bacon is typical of Bern's version. The plain cake is written here.

Ingredients

  • 800 g waxy potatoes
  • 1 tsp fine salt
  • 40 g unsalted butter
  • 1 tbsp neutral oil

Steps

  1. Boil the potatoes in their skins until just tender. Drain, chill until cold, then peel and grate coarsely.
  2. Toss the gratings with salt.
  3. Heat the butter and oil in a wide nonstick pan. Add the potato and press it into an even round. Cook over medium heat until the bottom is a deep crust, about 10 minutes.
  4. Turn the cake onto a plate and slide it back to brown the other side. Cut in wedges.

Turning a large rösti is easier with a plate. The pan handle will be hot.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Bärner Rösti, with bacon, which adds a meat allergen and is not vegetarian
  • Raw grated potato instead of parboiled, a different and also real method

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Fried eggs
  • Cheese
  • A green salad
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Taiwan · No chili heat · 2 rolled crepes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Taiwan · East Asia

Taiwanese danbing

A thin breakfast crepe with egg cooked on its face, brushed with soy sauce and rolled with scallion.

10 min prep · 12 min cook · Serves 2 · 2 rolled crepes

History

Danbing is Taiwan's egg crepe, a breakfast from street griddles and from the windows of morning shops. Dan means egg. A thin wheat batter is spread, an egg is cooked on it, soy sauce and scallion go over the top, and the sheet is rolled. Fillings such as pork floss or cheese are common additions. This recipe keeps the egg crepe itself, without meat, so the tags stay vegetarian. It is Taipei breakfast food, fast and savory.

Cultural background

The griddle is flat and lightly oiled. The crepe has to stay flexible enough to roll and strong enough to hold the egg. Breakfast stands assemble it in a minute. At home the same batter works in a nonstick pan. Pork floss, if you add it, brings a meat allergen the written recipe does not have.

Why it tastes this way

Egg against a toasted wheat sheet is the whole flavor, salted hard by soy sauce. Scallion cuts the richness. Because the egg is spread thin, it tastes fried rather than like a folded omelet. The crepe itself is almost bland, a carrier, which is why the sauce matters. It is a breakfast built for eating out of a bag.

How it is made

Cook a thin wheat crepe, spread an egg on it with soy sauce and scallion, and roll it tight.

Authentic touch. Spread the egg while the crepe is still moist so they fuse. Brush soy sauce on the egg, not only on the plate, or the roll tastes flat.

How it is used

Eat it immediately, rolled, as a street breakfast. A hot soy milk is the usual cup beside it.

Field notes

  • Danbing means egg pancake. It is a Taiwanese breakfast crepe.
  • The plain egg roll is vegetarian. Pork floss is an addition, not the base recipe.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup all-purpose wheat flour
  • 2/3 cup water
  • A pinch of salt
  • 2 eggs
  • 1 tablespoon soy sauce
  • 2 scallions, chopped
  • Neutral oil

Steps

  1. Whisk flour, water, and salt into a thin batter.
  2. Oil a pan over medium heat. Pour half the batter and swirl a thin crepe.
  3. When the top is nearly set, crack an egg onto it and spread the white and yolk into a thin layer.
  4. Brush with soy sauce and scatter scallion. Cook until the egg sets.
  5. Roll the crepe tightly. Repeat with the second egg.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Pork floss in the roll, which adds meat
  • A slice of processed cheese, which adds dairy

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Street food
  • Savory meal
  • Sweet or salty soy milk
  • A few slices of cucumber
  • Chile crisp on the side
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Taiwan · Mild · 2 omelets

Flying Pancakes of the World

Taiwan · East Asia

Taiwanese oyster omelet

A soft, starch-set omelet crowded with oysters, finished with a sweet-savory sauce and greens.

15 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 2 · 2 omelets

History

Taiwanese oyster omelet, o-a-jian, is a night-market cake of small oysters bound with sweet potato starch or tapioca starch and egg. Tainan is famous for it, and stalls across the island cook it on a well-oiled griddle. The starch sets into a tender, slightly chewy sheet around the oysters. A pink-brown sweet sauce is spooned on at the end. This page tags shellfish and names the oysters. There is no wheat in the written recipe, so it is gluten-free. It is not a wheat danbing.

Cultural background

The oysters should be small and cooked just until the edges ruffle. A thick flour batter would make a different pancake. Greens, often chrysanthemum greens or spinach, wilt in at the end. The sauce is part of the dish, not an optional ketchup.

Why it tastes this way

Oysters bring brine and sweetness. Sweet potato starch keeps the egg soft and a little elastic instead of dry and browned like a French omelet. Garlic and a mild chile cut the richness. The sauce adds sugar, tomato, and acid, which is why the cake tastes sweet-savory rather than purely marine. You eat it with the sauce in the same bite as the oyster.

How it is made

Set a sweet-potato-starch batter on a hot griddle with oysters and egg, then sauce it.

Authentic touch. Boil or rinse the starch slurry onto a hot oiled pan and let it set before you stir. If you scramble it like loose eggs from the start, you never get the sheet that defines o-a-jian.

How it is used

Serve immediately from the pan, sauce on top, as street food. It toughens if it sits.

Field notes

  • O-a-jian is the Taiwanese oyster omelet, set with sweet potato or tapioca starch.
  • Tainan is one of the cities most associated with the dish.

Ingredients

  • 200 grams small oysters, drained
  • 3 tablespoons sweet potato starch
  • 1/2 cup water
  • 2 eggs
  • 2 cloves garlic, sliced
  • A handful of spinach or chrysanthemum greens
  • 1 tablespoon tomato paste
  • 1 tablespoon sugar
  • 1 tablespoon rice vinegar
  • Neutral oil and salt

Steps

  1. Stir sweet potato starch and water into a slurry. Salt the oysters lightly.
  2. Simmer tomato paste, sugar, vinegar, and a spoon of water into a thick sauce. Keep it warm.
  3. Heat a generous film of oil. Fry the garlic briefly, add the oysters, and cook until the edges curl.
  4. Pour in the slurry and let it set. Add the eggs and greens. Cook until the eggs set and the starch turns translucent, turning the cake once if it is sturdy.
  5. Spoon the sauce over the top and serve at once.

Keep oysters cold until cooking, and cook them until the edges curl and the centers are firm.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Tapioca starch in place of sweet potato starch
  • More garlic shoots when they are in season

Pairings and uses

  • Street food
  • Savory meal
  • Snack
  • The sweet savory sauce in the recipe
  • A plate of pickled mustard greens
  • A cold tea
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Japan · No chili heat · 16 balls

Flying Pancakes of the World

Japan · East Asia

Takoyaki

Crisp-edged batter balls with a piece of octopus in the center, finished with sauce and bonito.

20 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 16 balls

History

Takoyaki are octopus batter balls from Osaka, cooked in a pan of round molds. They are a cousin of the savory batter family that also produced okonomiyaki, and they are not a flat cake. Octopus, pickled ginger, and tenkasu sit in a thin wheat batter. The balls are turned with a pick until the outside browns and the inside stays soft. Octopus is a mollusk. This page tags shellfish and names the octopus in the recipe. Bonito flakes on top add fish. The snack is not vegetarian.

Cultural background

Osaka stalls sell them by the boat-shaped plate. The turn matters: an unturned ball is raw on top and burnt below. Mayonnaise and a brown sauce are the usual finish. No brand is required. Aonori and bonito complete the plate.

Why it tastes this way

The batter takes on dashi, so it tastes lightly marine even before the octopus. Octopus itself is sweet and chewy if it is not overcooked into rubber. Pickled ginger and scallion cut the softness. Crisped edges of batter contrast with a custardy center. Sauce adds sweetness, and bonito adds a smoky fish finish. The shape keeps a pocket of filling that a flat pancake would spread out.

How it is made

Pour a dashi batter into a takoyaki pan, add octopus and ginger, and turn the balls until they are round and browned.

Authentic touch. Fill the molds with batter first, add octopus, then keep turning. If you drop the octopus in late, it sits raw on the surface. Oil the pan well.

How it is used

Eat them hot from the plate, with picks. They are street food. The center stays very hot.

Field notes

  • Takoyaki are molded balls, a cousin of Osaka's savory batters, not a flat pancake.
  • The filling is octopus. The allergen tag is shellfish, and bonito on top is fish.

Ingredients

  • 1 cup all-purpose wheat flour
  • 1 egg
  • 1 1/2 cups bonito dashi
  • 150 grams cooked octopus, cut in bite-size pieces
  • 2 tablespoons pickled ginger, chopped
  • 2 scallions, chopped
  • 2 tablespoons tenkasu (tempura bits), optional
  • Neutral oil
  • Sauce, mayonnaise, aonori, and bonito flakes to finish

Steps

  1. Whisk flour, egg, and dashi into a thin batter. Stir in ginger and scallion.
  2. Oil a hot takoyaki pan. Fill each mold with batter and add a piece of octopus.
  3. Cook until the bottoms set, then break the edges and turn each ball with a pick.
  4. Keep turning until the balls are golden and the batter is cooked through, adding a few tenkasu if you use them.
  5. Pile them on a plate, add sauce, mayonnaise, aonori, and bonito, and eat while hot.

Use octopus that has been kept cold, and cook the batter until the center is no longer liquid. The filling stays hot after cooking.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A cube of cheese in place of one octopus piece, which adds dairy
  • Extra pickled ginger

Pairings and uses

  • Street food
  • Savory meal
  • Snack
  • Savory brown sauce
  • Mayonnaise
  • Aonori and bonito flakes
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

United Kingdom · No chili heat · 8 triangles

Flying Pancakes of the World

United Kingdom · Western Europe

Tattie scones

Thin potato triangles from a Scottish girdle, eaten at breakfast.

15 min prep · 12 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 triangles

History

Tattie scones are Scottish potato scones: mashed potato, a little flour and fat, rolled thin, cut into triangles, and cooked on a girdle. Tattie means potato. They are part of a fried breakfast or a weekend plate with eggs. They are not Lancashire's thicker fried rounds, and they are not boxty. Flour in the dough means they are not gluten-free.

Cultural background

Cold mash from the night before is the usual start. The scones are cooked in a dry or lightly greased pan so the outside toasts without becoming a fritter.

Why it tastes this way

Potato makes them mild and soft. A thin roll means the heat reaches the center and the surface dries. Salt and pepper matter because there is little else. Browning at the edge is the flavor. Butter on the hot scone melts into the face that was against the girdle.

How it is made

Mashed potato is kneaded with butter and flour, rolled into a thin round, quartered, and cooked on a hot girdle until spotted.

Authentic touch. Cut the round into quarters. A farl here is a practical cut, not the Ulster soda farl. Keep them thin enough that you can see the girdle color through the dough at the edge.

How it is used

At breakfast, with eggs, bacon, or butter. They are a savory cake.

Field notes

  • Tattie is potato. The scones are potato, not a sweet oven scone.
  • They are rolled thin and cut in triangles or quarters.
  • They belong on a Scottish breakfast plate.

Ingredients

  • 400 g cold mashed potato
  • 30 g unsalted butter, plus more for serving
  • 80 g plain wheat flour, plus more for rolling
  • 1/2 tsp fine salt
  • 1/4 tsp black pepper

Steps

  1. Mash the potato with the butter, salt, and pepper until smooth. Work in the flour to a soft dough.
  2. Roll into two thin rounds on a floured board and cut each into quarters.
  3. Cook on a hot dry girdle until brown spots appear, then turn and cook the other side.
  4. Serve warm with butter, eggs, or bacon.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of chopped chives
  • Lancashire potato cakes, thicker and pan-fried in more fat

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Eggs
  • Bacon or square sausage, for a meat breakfast
  • Butter
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

India · Mild · 6 flatbreads

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Thalipeeth

A hearty multigrain griddle bread, onion-flecked, with toasted millet and chickpea in the crumb.

25 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 6 flatbreads

History

Thalipeeth is a Maharashtrian griddle bread made from bhajani, a mix of roasted flours. Sorghum, millet, rice, chickpea, and wheat are a typical blend, though every household flour tin varies. Onion and cilantro are patted into the dough. It is a flatbread, not a poured dosa. This recipe includes wheat, so it is not gluten-free. Pune snacks and home breakfasts both rely on it.

Cultural background

Bhajani was a way to use several local grains in one dough. Roasting the flours before they are stored gives thalipeeth its toasted smell even before the pan. A hole poked in the center is a common cook's habit so the middle griddles through.

Why it tastes this way

Roasted sorghum and millet taste nutty and slightly sweet. Besan adds chickpea. Wheat makes the dough easier to pat. Onion and green chile steam inside the bread and keep the grains from tasting dry. Oil on the griddle, rather than a yeasted crumb, is the richness. There is no ferment tang.

How it is made

Mix a multigrain dough with onion and spices, pat it into thick rounds, and griddle both sides with oil.

Authentic touch. Pat the dough on a wet leaf, plastic sheet, or damp cloth. It cracks if you treat it like a smooth wheat chapati. Oil the cracks and keep going.

How it is used

Serve warm with a dry garlic chutney or a spoon of yogurt. Yogurt is a dairy side. The bread itself is cooked here in oil.

Field notes

  • Thalipeeth is a patted flatbread, not a ladle-poured pancake.
  • This blend includes wheat flour, so the bread is not gluten-free.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup sorghum flour
  • 1/4 cup rice flour
  • 1/4 cup besan
  • 1/2 cup whole wheat flour
  • 1 small onion, finely chopped
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • 2 tablespoons chopped cilantro
  • 1/2 teaspoon cumin seeds
  • 3/4 teaspoon salt
  • Warm water and neutral oil

Steps

  1. Stir the flours, salt, and cumin together. Add onion, chile, and cilantro.
  2. Add warm water a little at a time and mix to a stiff dough that can be patted. Rest 10 minutes.
  3. With wet hands, pat a ball into a thick round on a damp sheet. Poke a small hole in the center if you like.
  4. Transfer it to a hot oiled griddle. Cook on medium, oiling the surface, until both sides are spotted brown and the center is cooked.
  5. Repeat with the remaining dough. Serve warm.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Ghee in place of oil, which adds dairy
  • A little grated cucumber if the dough feels stiff

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Dry garlic chutney
  • Plain yogurt as a side
  • Sliced red onion
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free
