China · Mild · 2 wrapped crepes

Flying Pancakes of the World

China · East Asia

Tianjin jianbing

A soft savory crepe wrapped around egg, sauce, herbs, and a shatter of crisp fried dough.

20 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 2 · 2 wrapped crepes

History

Tianjin jianbing is a street breakfast crepe. The cook spreads a thin batter, cracks an egg onto it, paints a savory sauce, adds scallion, cilantro, and chile, then presses in a crisp fried dough stick, the youtiao or guozi, and folds the crepe around it. Tianjin is the city most tied to this wrapped form. A Shandong jianbing is a different street crepe, often cracker-crisp and torn, and it has its own page. This one is the egg-and-fritter package.

Cultural background

Morning stalls work fast on a round griddle. The crepe stays flexible so it can wrap. The fritter supplies the crunch the soft sheet does not have. Sauces vary. A sweet-savory bean sauce and a chile sauce are the usual pair. This recipe uses those and skips any brand name.

Why it tastes this way

Egg fried onto the crepe tastes rich against a plain grain sheet. Fermented bean sauce is salty and slightly sweet. Cilantro and scallion freshen it. Chile keeps the heat mild unless you add more. The youtiao is dry, airy, and fried, so it crackles inside a tender wrap. The combination is the Tianjin breakfast: soft, crisp, savory, and eaten on the way to work.

How it is made

Cook a thin crepe, fry an egg onto it, add sauce and herbs, press in a crisp youtiao, and fold.

Authentic touch. Crack the egg onto the crepe while the batter is still wet and break the yolk with the spreader. An egg added after the sheet dries will not bond, and the wrap falls apart.

How it is used

Eat it at once, folded, while the fritter is still loud. It is a street breakfast.

Field notes

  • Tianjin jianbing guozi is the crepe wrapped around a crisp fried dough stick.
  • The egg is cooked on the crepe, not scrambled in a separate pan.

Ingredients

  • 1/2 cup all-purpose wheat flour
  • 2 tablespoons mung bean flour or more wheat flour
  • 3/4 cup water
  • 2 eggs
  • 2 crisp youtiao (fried dough sticks)
  • 2 tablespoons sweet bean sauce or fermented bean sauce
  • Chile sauce to taste
  • 2 scallions, chopped
  • A handful of cilantro
  • Neutral oil

Steps

  1. Whisk the flours and water into a thin crepe batter.
  2. Oil a griddle over medium heat. Pour half the batter and spread a thin round.
  3. Crack an egg on the crepe, break the yolk, and spread it. When the top sets, flip so the egg browns briefly, then flip back.
  4. Paint on bean sauce and chile sauce. Add scallion and cilantro. Press a youtiao onto the crepe so it cracks.
  5. Fold the crepe around the fritter. Repeat with the rest.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Lettuce or a cracker in place of youtiao
  • More chile sauce for a hotter wrap

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Street food
  • Savory meal
  • Soy milk
  • A second chile spoon if you want more heat
  • Pickled mustard tuber
  • Vegetarian
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Mexico · Mild · 8 tlacoyos

Flying Pancakes of the World

Mexico · Mexico & Central America

Tlacoyos

An oval masa cake with a seam of mashed beans, topped with nopales, onion, and salsa.

35 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 tlacoyos

History

Tlacoyos are oval masa cakes from the Valley of Mexico. The filling, often mashed beans or habas, which are fava beans, goes inside the raw dough, and the cake is sealed before it meets the comal. That is different from a gordita, which is split after cooking, and from a sope, which is an open round with a pinched rim. The masa is nixtamalized corn, older in this region than wheat hotcakes. This recipe uses habas and keeps cheese off the cake.

Cultural background

Street cooks around Xochimilco and across Mexico City pat tlacoyos to order and set them on a clay or metal comal. Toppings are added after the cake is cooked. Queso fresco and crema are common. They are not in this version.

Why it tastes this way

The sealed bean filling steams inside the masa, so each bite is toasted corn and then a soft, salty center. Fava beans taste earthier and a little sweeter than black beans. A raw onion and a cooked nopal on top add bite and a green, tart note. The mild salsa brings acid and a small amount of chile without covering the corn.

How it is made

Season mashed cooked favas. Tuck them into oval masa cakes, seal, and griddle dry. Top with nopales, onion, and salsa.

Authentic touch. Shape a log, press a groove, lay in a thin rope of beans, and close the dough so the filling does not leak. The finished cake is oval and flatter than a gordita, with pointed or rounded ends.

How it is used

Eat them hot from the comal as a street snack or a light meal. Because this batch has no cheese or crema, it stays vegan. Add cheese only if you mean to change that.

Field notes

  • Habas, fava beans, are a classic tlacoyo filling along with black beans and requesón.
  • The oval shape and the sealed raw filling are what separate a tlacoyo from a gordita or a sope.
  • This batch is masa, beans, nopales, and salsa, so it stays vegan if you skip cheese and crema.

Ingredients

  • 250 g masa harina
  • 320 ml warm water
  • 1 teaspoon salt, divided
  • 200 g cooked fava beans, mashed with a spoon of their cooking liquid
  • 1 cup cooked nopales, drained
  • 1 small white onion, finely chopped
  • 2 tomatoes and 1 jalapeño for salsa

Steps

  1. Mix the masa harina, 1/2 teaspoon salt, and warm water. Knead, cover, and rest 20 minutes.
  2. Season the mashed favas with the remaining salt. They should be thick, not soupy. Chop the tomatoes and jalapeño with a pinch of salt for a mild salsa.
  3. Divide the dough into 8 pieces. Form each into an oval, press a groove down the center, add a spoon of favas, and seal the dough around them. Pat into a flat oval.
  4. Cook on a dry comal over medium heat for about 4 minutes a side, until the masa is spotted and cooked through.
  5. Top with nopales, raw onion, and salsa. Serve hot.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Mashed black beans can replace the favas. Keep the filling dry enough to seal.
  • Crumbled fresh cheese and crema are a common topping. They add dairy, so the plate is no longer vegan or dairy-free.
  • A hotter salsa is a table choice. The cake itself is the same.

Pairings and uses

  • Street food
  • Savory meal
  • Snack
  • Sliced radishes and lime
  • A cup of hot corn atole, if you want a drink beside them
  • Black coffee
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Spain · No chili heat · 12 fritters

Flying Pancakes of the World

Spain · Southern Europe

Tortillitas de camarones

Crisp Cádiz fritters of tiny shrimp and chickpea flour.

20 min prep · 15 min cook · Serves 4 · 12 fritters

History

Tortillitas de camarones are shrimp fritters from the Bay of Cádiz, especially Cádiz and San Fernando. The batter is chickpea flour and water, crowded with tiny shrimp, parsley, and onion, fried crisp in olive oil. They are bar food and festival food. There is no wheat in this recipe, so it is gluten-free if the chickpea flour was not milled with wheat. The shrimp make it a shellfish dish, not vegetarian. They are not a French socca and not a potato tortilla.

Cultural background

Camarones are the tiny local shrimp. If you cannot buy them that small, chop the smallest shrimp you can find and say that is a substitute. The fritter should be lacy, not a dense fish cake.

Why it tastes this way

Chickpea flour fries nutty and sweet. Shrimp taste briny and sweet. Parsley and onion keep it fresh. Olive oil in a hot pan makes the edge crisp and lacy. A thick, pale fritter means the oil was too cool or the batter too heavy. Salt and a squeeze of lemon are the finish. There is no chile on this page.

How it is made

Chickpea flour, water, onion, parsley, and chopped shrimp rest briefly, then spoonfuls are fried in hot olive oil until crisp.

Authentic touch. Use chickpea flour, not wheat. Fry thin and hot so the fritter laces. Tiny shrimp are the point. A wheat batter is a different tapa.

How it is used

Hot, as a bar snack or a first plate, with lemon. They are street and table food.

Field notes

  • These are Cádiz shrimp fritters. The flour is chickpea.
  • They are a shellfish dish.
  • They are not the potato omelet called a Spanish tortilla.

Ingredients

  • 150 g chickpea flour
  • 250 ml cold water
  • 1/2 tsp fine salt
  • 1 small onion, very finely chopped
  • 2 tbsp chopped parsley
  • 200 g tiny shrimp, or small shrimp chopped fine
  • Olive oil, for frying
  • Lemon wedges, for serving

Steps

  1. Whisk the chickpea flour, water, and salt. Stir in the onion, parsley, and shrimp. Rest 15 minutes. The batter should be thick but spoonable.
  2. Heat a shallow layer of olive oil until a drop of batter sizzles at once.
  3. Fry spoonfuls, spreading them thin, until the edges are brown and crisp. Turn once.
  4. Drain and serve with lemon.

The shrimp must be fully cooked and the oil hot. Do not leave the pan. If you have a shellfish allergy, skip this recipe.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A pinch of hot paprika, which would raise the heat
  • Socca, the Nice chickpea flatbread, which has no shrimp

Pairings and uses

  • Street food
  • Snack
  • Savory meal
  • Lemon wedges
  • A cold drink
  • Nothing creamy on top
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Trinidad and Tobago · No chili heat · 6 rotis

Flying Pancakes of the World

Trinidad and Tobago · Caribbean

Trinidadian dhalpuri roti

A soft wheat roti with a layer of seasoned ground split peas that flakes when the bread is clapped.

40 min prep · 35 min cook · Serves 6 · 6 rotis

History

After emancipation in the British Caribbean, planters recruited indentured workers from India to keep the cane fields going. Indenture was a fixed contract, often five years, and it was not free passage. The first ships from India reached Trinidad in 1845. Guyana received workers on the same system. Dhalpuri roti, a wheat flatbread stuffed with seasoned ground split peas, comes from that Indo-Caribbean kitchen. It is a shop and home staple in Trinidad and Tobago and in Guyana. This is the stuffed roti, not a stand-in for every Indian bread.

Cultural background

Dhalpuri is the wrap around curries at roti shops in Port of Spain and in Guyanese lunchrooms. The filling is called dhal, but it is ground and dried, not a soup. Families also cook it for Sunday lunch. The recipe uses oil, not ghee, so it stays vegan.

Why it tastes this way

The split-pea filling tastes of cumin, garlic, and black pepper, toasted by the griddle through the wheat skin. Because the peas are ground and dry, they flake inside the bread instead of steaming it into paste. The roti itself is plain wheat and salt, a little rich from the oil you wipe on while it cooks. Clapping the hot bread breaks the pea layer into a loose crumble.

How it is made

Boil yellow split peas, drain them very well, and grind them with garlic, cumin, salt, and pepper. Seal a spoonful inside a wheat dough, roll it out, and cook it on a hot tawa with a little oil.

Authentic touch. The pea paste has to be dry and fully cooled before it goes into the dough. Wet filling bursts and soaks the roti. Roll gently so the peas spread in a thin sheet without tearing the surface.

How it is used

Use the roti to wrap a curry, or eat it beside a stew. In a shop it is often the edible plate under goat, chicken, or a potato and chickpea curry. Those fillings are separate from this bread.

Field notes

  • The first indenture ships from India arrived in Trinidad in 1845, after emancipation.
  • Dhalpuri is also a Guyanese staple. The same labor system brought the kitchen there.
  • The split peas are ground and dried, not left as a poured lentil soup.

Ingredients

  • 200 g dried yellow split peas
  • 2 garlic cloves
  • 1 teaspoon ground cumin
  • 1 teaspoon salt, divided
  • 1/4 teaspoon black pepper
  • 300 g wheat flour, plus more for rolling
  • 1 tablespoon neutral oil, plus more for the tawa
  • 160 ml water

Steps

  1. Boil the split peas in unsalted water until they are very soft. Drain them and spread them out so the steam can leave. They must be dry.
  2. Grind the peas with the garlic, cumin, 1/2 teaspoon salt, and the pepper into a fine, dry paste. Cool it completely.
  3. Mix the flour, the remaining salt, 1 tablespoon oil, and the water into a soft dough. Rest it, covered, for 20 minutes.
  4. Divide into 6 pieces. Flatten one, put a spoon of pea paste in the center, seal, and roll carefully into a thin round.
  5. Cook on a hot tawa, turning and brushing with oil, until both sides are spotted. Clap the hot roti between clean hands, or fold it in a towel and press, so the pea layer crumbles. Serve warm.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A small minced hot pepper in the peas is common. It raises the heat above none.
  • Ghee on the tawa adds dairy, so the roti is no longer vegan or dairy-free.
  • Guyanese shop roti uses the same stuffed idea, with local differences in spice and size.

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Street food
  • Accompaniment to a meal
  • Potato and chickpea curry
  • Mango kuchela on the side
  • A meat curry, which makes the whole plate not vegetarian
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Turkey · No chili heat · 4 large flatbreads

Flying Pancakes of the World

Turkey · Middle East

Turkish gözleme

Thin stuffed flatbread, griddled, with spinach and salty white cheese in the fold.

40 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 large flatbreads

History

Gözleme is a stuffed flatbread. A soft wheat dough is rolled thin, filled, sealed, and cooked on a sac, the wide convex griddle, or in a heavy pan. It is a village and street food across Turkey, not a batter you pour. Spinach and white cheese is a common filling. Potato and minced meat are others. This recipe is the spinach and cheese one, cooked on a griddle, from a Denizli-style home kitchen.

Cultural background

At markets, the cook rolls the dough in the open and fills it to order. At home it is a way to turn greens and cheese into a meal. The bread is cut into pieces as soon as it leaves the pan. It is savory breakfast, lunch, or a snack.

Why it tastes this way

The dough tastes of wheat and salt, with brown spots from the griddle. Spinach keeps a green, slightly metallic flavor if it is only wilted, not boiled to mush. Salty white cheese seasons the filling so the bread does not need a sauce. A slick of butter on the finished bread tastes milky and rich. There is no sugar.

How it is made

Mix a soft dough and rest it. Wilt spinach with onion, salt it, and mix it with crumbled white cheese. Roll each dough piece thin, spread filling on half, fold, and cook on a hot sac or skillet, turning, until blistered. Brush with a little butter.

Authentic touch. Roll the dough thin, seal the filling inside, and cook it on a griddle. Say what it is: a stuffed flatbread, not a pancake. A minced-meat filling is a different batch and is not vegetarian.

How it is used

Gözleme is a savory meal or a street snack, often breakfast. It is eaten with tea and sometimes with yogurt on the side. It is not a dessert. The meat-filled version is common and is not the diet listed here.

Field notes

  • Gözleme is a stuffed flatbread cooked on a griddle, not a poured pancake.
  • Spinach and white cheese is one of the usual fillings.
  • A sac is the wide metal griddle many cooks still use for it.

Ingredients

  • 2 1/2 cups wheat flour (about 310 g)
  • 1 teaspoon fine salt, divided
  • 3/4 cup water
  • 2 tablespoons yogurt
  • 2 tablespoons olive oil
  • 1 onion, chopped
  • 300 g spinach, chopped
  • 150 g salty white cheese, crumbled
  • 1 tablespoon butter, for finishing

Steps

  1. Mix the flour, half the salt, water, yogurt, and olive oil into a soft dough. Knead until smooth and rest it for 20 minutes.
  2. Cook the onion in a dry pan until soft, add the spinach and the rest of the salt, and cook until the greens collapse and the pan is almost dry. Cool, then mix in the cheese.
  3. Divide the dough into 4. Roll each piece very thin. This is a stuffed flatbread, not a batter.
  4. Spread filling on half of each round, fold the other half over, and press the edges.
  5. Cook on a hot griddle or sac, turning once, until both sides are spotted and the dough is cooked. Brush with butter and cut into pieces.

The dough contains wheat. Yogurt, cheese, and butter are dairy. A meat filling is not part of this version.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry pan. Do not leave the cheese filling at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A potato and onion filling can be made without cheese if you also change the dough fat. This written filling contains dairy.
  • Minced meat with pepper is a widespread filling and is not vegetarian.
  • Cooking on a flat pan instead of a sac still counts if the dough is thin and turned.

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Street food
  • Breakfast
  • Black tea
  • Plain yogurt
  • A wedge of lemon
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Turkey · No chili heat · 1 skillet cake

Flying Pancakes of the World

Turkey · Middle East

Turkish kaygana

A soft flour-and-egg skillet cake, herb-flecked, with a tender center and a browned rim.

10 min prep · 12 min cook · Serves 2 · 1 skillet cake

History

Kaygana is a Turkish breakfast cake of flour, egg, and milk, cooked in a skillet. Herbs sometimes go in, and so do cheese or pepper in other houses. It is quicker and thinner than a baked herb frittata. This version is the herb one, the kind a Black Sea or inland home might cook on a weekday morning, written here with a Trabzon pin because that coast still keeps a strong breakfast pan. It is not gözleme and not kuku sabzi.

Cultural background

Kaygana uses pantry ingredients when there is no time to roll dough. It is cut in the pan and eaten with bread or instead of it, with tea. The herbs should be fresh. Dried mint is a different, sharper cake.

Why it tastes this way

Egg is the main flavor, with flour taking the edge off so the cake is less like an omelette and more like a tender pancake. Milk softens it. Parsley and dill taste green and slight, not like the huge mass of herbs in an Iranian kuku. The pan butter browns the rim. There is no syrup.

How it is made

Whisk eggs, milk, flour, salt, parsley, and dill into a pourable batter. Cook it in a buttered skillet over moderate heat, covered, until just set. Fold or cut it in the pan. Serve at once with tea.

Authentic touch. Keep it a flour-and-egg skillet cake for breakfast. Do not bake it into a thick frittata and do not stuff it like gözleme. Fresh herbs, if you use them, are mixed into the batter.

How it is used

Kaygana is breakfast and a savory meal. It is eaten with tea, yogurt, or a simple salad. It is not a dessert. Cheese on top is extra dairy on a dish that already has milk.

Field notes

  • Kaygana is a Turkish flour-and-egg pancake, usually cooked for breakfast.
  • Herbs are mixed into the batter in this version.
  • It is thinner and quicker than a baked herb frittata.

Ingredients

  • 3 eggs
  • 1/2 cup milk
  • 1/3 cup wheat flour
  • 1/4 teaspoon fine salt
  • 2 tablespoons chopped parsley
  • 2 tablespoons chopped dill
  • 1 tablespoon butter

Steps

  1. Whisk the eggs, milk, flour, and salt until smooth. Stir in the parsley and dill.
  2. Heat the butter in a skillet over medium heat until it foams.
  3. Pour in the batter and tilt the pan. This is a flour-and-egg cake, not a rolled flatbread.
  4. Cover and cook until the center is just set and the rim is browned. Do not rush it over high heat.
  5. Cut it in the pan and serve for breakfast.

The cake contains wheat, egg, and dairy. Cook it until the egg is set, not liquid in the center.

Refrigerate leftovers the same day and reheat them in a covered pan. Do not leave the egg batter waiting at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • A spoon of white cheese in the batter is a saltier breakfast.
  • Leave the herbs out for a plain flour-and-egg cake.
  • A little black pepper is enough seasoning. A chile paste would change the heat tag.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Black tea
  • Plain yogurt
  • Sliced tomato
  • Vegetarian
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

China · No chili heat · 1 loaf, about 8 slices

Flying Pancakes of the World

China · East Asia

Turnip cake

A savory daikon cake of rice flour, steamed until set, then pan-fried so the slices crust.

25 min prep · 50 min cook · Serves 4 · 1 loaf, about 8 slices

History

Turnip cake, lo bak go, is a Cantonese dim sum cake of rice flour and daikon. The English word turnip is the market name. The vegetable is daikon, a white radish. The batter is steamed in a tin until firm, cooled, sliced, and then pan-fried. That steam-then-sear method is the dish. Guangzhou and Hong Kong dim sum carts both serve it, so Hong Kong is listed beside China. Many carts add Chinese sausage and dried shrimp. Those add meat and shellfish. This written recipe uses daikon and shiitake so the tags stay vegetarian and free of shellfish.

Cultural background

The cake is a Lunar New Year dish as well as a dim sum standard. It should slice cleanly when cool and crust when it hits the pan. A wheat-flour batter would be a different cake. Rice flour is the structure, which is why this version is gluten-free.

Why it tastes this way

Daikon turns sweet and juicy when it is grated and cooked, and it lightens the rice flour. Shiitake adds a deep savory note that sausage would have brought in another kitchen. Sesame oil perfumes the batter. Steaming sets a soft, sliceable loaf. The later fry browns the surface so you get a crust against a moist interior. White pepper is the quiet heat, not chile.

How it is made

Cook grated daikon, stir in rice flour, steam the tin until firm, cool, slice, and pan-fry the slices.

Authentic touch. Steam until a skewer comes out clean, then cool the tin fully before you slice. Frying a hot, unset cake makes it crumble. The sear is a second step, not the only cooking.

How it is used

Serve the browned slices hot with chile sauce if you like. It is dim sum and New Year food, a savory snack or a feast plate.

Field notes

  • Lo bak go is steamed first and pan-fried second. Both steps belong to the method.
  • The vegetable is daikon. Rice flour, not wheat, sets the cake.

Ingredients

  • 500 grams daikon, grated
  • 1 cup rice flour, with no wheat added
  • 1/2 cup water
  • 4 dried shiitake mushrooms, soaked and chopped
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 1/4 teaspoon white pepper
  • 1 tablespoon sesame oil
  • Neutral oil for frying

Steps

  1. Simmer the grated daikon in a pan with a splash of water until it is translucent and sweet, about 10 minutes. Stir in the mushrooms, salt, pepper, and sesame oil.
  2. Sprinkle in the rice flour and the 1/2 cup water. Stir until a thick batter forms.
  3. Pack the batter into a greased tin. Steam over boiling water for 40 minutes, until a skewer comes out clean.
  4. Cool the cake completely so it firms. Slice it.
  5. Pan-fry the slices in a little oil until both sides are brown. Serve hot.

Steam the cake until it is fully set before you cool and fry it.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Lap cheong and dried shrimp, which add meat and shellfish
  • A little chopped cilantro on the fried slices

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Feast or fast day
  • Snack
  • Chile sauce
  • Hot tea
  • A vinegar dip
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

India · Mild · 6 uttapams

Flying Pancakes of the World

India · South Asia

Uttapam

A thick, soft fermented cake topped with onion, tomato, and green chile.

35 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 3 · 6 uttapams

History

Uttapam is the thick member of the South Indian fermented rice and urad family. Instead of a thin spread, the cook pours a deeper round and presses onion, tomato, or chile into the wet top. Tamil homes and vegetarian hotels both claim it as everyday breakfast food. It uses the dosa ferment, then chooses toppings and thickness rather than a new flour.

Cultural background

The toppings go on while the batter is still soft so they sink and hold. A crisp paper dosa cannot carry tomato pieces. Uttapam can. Green chile keeps the heat mild if you leave the seeds out.

Why it tastes this way

A deeper batter stays moist, so the ferment tastes round and a little sweet. Onion and tomato steam on the covered pan and add juice. Chile and curry leaf keep it savory. The underside still toasts against the pan, which is why uttapam is a griddle cake and not a steamed idli with vegetables.

How it is made

Pour thick rounds of fermented rice and urad batter, press onion, tomato, and chile into the top, cover, and flip once.

Authentic touch. Press the toppings in with a spatula as soon as you pour. If you wait until the top dries, they fall off when you flip.

How it is used

Serve open so the vegetables show, with coconut chutney. Two small uttapams make a breakfast.

Field notes

  • Uttapam is thick on purpose. Spreading it thin turns it back into a dosa.
  • Onion, tomato, and chile are toppings on the ferment, not a change of grain.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups fermented rice and urad dosa batter
  • 1 small onion, finely chopped
  • 1 small tomato, seeded and chopped
  • 1 green chile, minced
  • 2 tablespoons chopped cilantro
  • Neutral oil for the pan
  • Salt if the batter needs it

Steps

  1. Start with bubbly fermented rice and urad batter. If it is stiff, stir in a spoon of water. It should hold a thick round.
  2. Mix onion, tomato, chile, and cilantro in a bowl. Salt them lightly if the batter is already salted.
  3. Oil a medium-hot pan. Pour a ladle of batter and let it spread only into a cake about a centimeter thick.
  4. Scatter a handful of the vegetable mix on top and press gently. Drizzle oil around the edge. Cover for about 2 minutes.
  5. Flip once and cook until the vegetable side takes a little color. Serve the vegetable side up.

Use fermented batter that smells clean and sour, not rotten.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Grated carrot in place of tomato
  • A few curry leaves pressed into the top

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Coconut chutney
  • Sambar
  • Corriander leaves
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Open the journal page

Venezuela · No chili heat · 4 arepas

Flying Pancakes of the World

Venezuela · South America

Venezuelan arepas

Thick white-corn cakes, griddled until they crackle, split and filled with fresh cheese.

15 min prep · 20 min cook · Serves 4 · 4 arepas

History

The corn cake is older than the stuffed breakfast sandwich. People in Venezuela were cooking ground corn on a griddle long before wheat hotcakes, and long before a Caracas counter split the cake and filled it. There is no single pre-Columbian recipe that matches today's stuffed arepa. What is old is the corn cake. Splitting it and stuffing it is a later way of eating it. Precooked white cornmeal, the flour most homes use now, is a twentieth-century shortcut: the corn is cooked and dried before it is milled. It is not nixtamalized masa, and it is not fresh sweet corn.

Cultural background

An arepa in Caracas is breakfast, a packed lunch, and a night-time snack. Fillings run from cheese to black beans to shredded beef. This recipe is the corn cake plus fresh white cheese, the vegetarian counter version. The dough is only precooked cornmeal, water, and salt.

Why it tastes this way

Precooked cornmeal cooks up dense and corny, with a skin that toasts and a center that stays steamy if the dough was wet enough. Salt is the only seasoning in the cake, so the flavor is plain corn, not chile and not wheat. Fresh cheese melts at the edges and salts the interior once the cake is split. The contrast is the point of the modern sandwich: a bland, toasted shell and a rich filling.

How it is made

Stir precooked white cornmeal into warm salted water, rest it, and form thick cakes. Griddle them until a crust forms and the inside is set, then split and fill with cheese.

Authentic touch. The dough should feel like soft clay that does not stick to your hands and does not crack when you form a ball. Seal every crack before the cake hits the griddle, or it will split in the pan. Split it only after it is cooked, with a knife around the edge.

How it is used

Eat them hot for breakfast, plain or filled. A thinner Colombian arepa, a fresh-corn cachapa, and this thick stuffed cake are three different plates.

Field notes

  • The stuffed breakfast arepa is later than the corn cake itself. Do not treat them as one ancient recipe.
  • Precooked cornmeal is not nixtamalized Mexican masa and not the fresh corn in a cachapa.
  • If the dough cracks on the griddle, it needed more water or a wetter hand before cooking.

Ingredients

  • 250 g precooked white cornmeal
  • 360 ml warm water
  • 1 teaspoon salt
  • 150 g sliced fresh white cheese
  • 1 teaspoon oil for the griddle, if needed

Steps

  1. Stir the salt into the warm water. Add the precooked cornmeal and mix until a soft dough forms. Cover and rest 5 to 10 minutes.
  2. If the dough cracks, wet your hands and knead in a spoon of water. Divide into 4 pieces and form thick cakes about 2 centimeters tall. Smooth every crack.
  3. Cook on a lightly oiled or dry skillet over medium heat for about 7 minutes a side, until a crust forms and the cake sounds slightly hollow.
  4. Cool for a minute, then split each arepa around the edge with a knife, leaving a hinge if you can.
  5. Fill with the cheese and return the cake to the pan just long enough to warm the filling. Serve hot.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Shredded beef or chicken is a usual filling. Either one adds meat and is not vegetarian.
  • Leave the cheese out and the cake is vegan and dairy-free. It is still an arepa.
  • Yellow precooked cornmeal makes a deeper-colored cake. It is still not masa harina.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Street food
  • Black beans as a second filling
  • Guasacaca, a green avocado sauce
  • Black coffee or a small cup of hot chocolate
  • Vegetarian
  • Gluten-free
  • Nut-free

China · Mild · 8 stuffed cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

China · East Asia

Xianbing

A round northern wheat cake sealed around a juicy pork and scallion filling, pan-fried until spotted.

30 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · 8 stuffed cakes

History

Xianbing are stuffed savory pancakes of northern China. A wheat dough, often made with hot water, is filled with minced pork, scallion, ginger, and seasoning, then pleated shut and pan-fried or griddled. Xi'an is one city where stuffed breads are everyday food. The same family of cakes shows up across the north under local habits. This page calls the cake what it is: a stuffed griddled pancake, not a rolled scallion bread and not an open-faced crepe.

Cultural background

The filling should be juicy and the dough strong enough to hold it. A tear in the pan spills meat and burns. Street cooks press the rounds gently so they cook through without bursting. Beef and lamb fillings exist. This recipe writes pork.

Why it tastes this way

Pork and scallion are the northern savory pair: fat, salt, and a sharp green bite. Ginger and soy sauce season the juice that collects inside the cake. The hot-water dough stays soft and a little chewy, then the pan spots it with toast. You taste the filling first and the crust second. Mild heat comes from ginger and white pepper, not from a chile paste.

How it is made

Fill hot-water dough with seasoned pork and scallion, seal the rounds, and pan-fry them covered until the meat cooks through.

Authentic touch. Pleat and pinch the seam firmly, and cook on medium with a lid for part of the time. An open high flame browns the dough and leaves the pork underdone.

How it is used

Eat them hot, with vinegar or garlic water if you want a dip. They are street food and a savory snack.

Field notes

  • Xianbing are northern stuffed savory pancakes, sealed and then griddled.
  • The pork stays inside the dough. It is not a topping.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups all-purpose wheat flour
  • 3/4 cup hot water
  • 200 grams ground pork
  • 3 scallions, chopped
  • 1 tablespoon soy sauce
  • 1 teaspoon grated ginger
  • 1/4 teaspoon white pepper
  • Neutral oil
  • Salt

Steps

  1. Mix the flour with hot water and a pinch of salt into a dough. Knead until smooth and rest 20 minutes.
  2. Mix the pork with scallion, soy sauce, ginger, white pepper, and a teaspoon of oil.
  3. Divide the dough into eight. Flatten each piece, add filling, and pleat it shut. Press gently into a thick round.
  4. Pan-fry in a filmed pan over medium heat, covered, until the underside is spotted and the pork is cooked through. Flip and finish the other side.
  5. Rest two minutes so the juice settles, then serve.

Cook until the pork in the center is no longer pink.

Refrigerate leftovers and reheat on a dry skillet. Egg or dairy batter should not sit at room temperature. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Beef in place of pork, still a meat filling
  • A vegetarian filling of egg and chive, which would drop the meat tag and add egg

Pairings and uses

  • Savory meal
  • Street food
  • Snack
  • Black vinegar
  • A smashed cucumber salad
  • Hot millet porridge
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free

Yemen · No chili heat · About 10 spongy cakes

Flying Pancakes of the World

Yemen · Middle East

Yemeni lahoh

Spongy sorghum cakes, sour and earthy, with holes that hold beans or egg.

20 min prep · 25 min cook · Serves 4 · About 10 spongy cakes

History

Yemeni lahoh is a spongy fermented pancake, often on the breakfast table beside foul, which is stewed fava beans, or a fried egg and a spoon of samn, clarified butter. Sorghum is a traditional flour for it. This page uses sorghum alone and stays in Sana'a. Djiboutian lahoh and Somali canjeero are related names with their own recipes. They are not this plate of foul and eggs.

Cultural background

Lahoh is morning food. The batter is mixed ahead, and the pan is busy while tea is poured. Foul is a separate pot. The bread can also be eaten with honey or with a slick of samn. The sour crumb is supposed to meet something rich.

Why it tastes this way

Sorghum tastes earthier and drier than wheat, and the ferment adds sourness that cuts through beans and butter. The holes are small cups for foul or egg yolk. Because this batter has no wheat and no sugar, it does not eat like a sweet Djibouti breakfast cake. Samn on top tastes nutty because it is clarified butter, not because the grain is sweet.

How it is made

Mix sorghum flour and water and ferment it until sour. Salt it, then cook rounds on one side in a covered pan until the holes set. Serve with foul, fried eggs, or samn. Those accompaniments are cooked separately. The bread itself contains no wheat.

Authentic touch. Serve it as a Yemeni breakfast with foul or eggs. Cook one side until the surface is spongy. A wheat-and-sugar batter is a different lahoh. This one is sorghum.

How it is used

Yemeni lahoh is breakfast and a savory meal when it is next to foul or eggs. Honey is the sweeter path. Samn and eggs bring dairy and egg to the plate even though the batter has neither.

Field notes

  • Yemeni lahoh is often eaten with foul or eggs.
  • This recipe is sorghum only, so it can be gluten-free if the flour is pure.
  • The Djibouti and Somali pancakes with similar names are separate pages.

Ingredients

  • 2 cups sorghum flour (about 240 g)
  • 2 1/2 cups water, plus more as needed
  • 1/2 teaspoon instant yeast
  • 1/2 teaspoon fine salt

Steps

  1. Whisk the sorghum flour, water, and yeast into a batter like thin cream. Leave the salt out. There is no wheat in this recipe.
  2. Cover it overnight, until it smells sour and shows bubbles. Give it longer if the room is cold.
  3. Stir in the salt. Add water if the batter thickened past a pour.
  4. Cook rounds in a covered nonstick pan on one side until the top is full of holes and no longer wet. Do not flip.
  5. Serve with foul or eggs. Those are the Yemeni breakfast partners, cooked in their own pans.

Gluten-free only if the sorghum flour is free of wheat. Foul and eggs are separate dishes with their own ingredients.

Refrigerate leftover lahoh and reheat it covered. Cooked foul should be refrigerated promptly and not left on the bread. Home cooking, not a professional food-safety instruction.

Variations

  • Some cooks add wheat flour to help the batter hold. That version is not gluten-free.
  • A longer ferment tastes sharper and works well with bland beans.
  • Honey instead of foul makes the same bread a sweet breakfast.

Pairings and uses

  • Breakfast
  • Savory meal
  • Foul, stewed fava beans
  • Fried eggs
  • Samn, clarified butter
  • Vegetarian
  • Vegan
  • Gluten-free
  • Dairy-free
  • Nut-free
